Table 1
Financial Accounting Policy & CoA Governance
1. Q: What is the purpose of a Financial Accounting Policies and Chart of Accounts specification for an international NGO?
A: Show answer
A Financial Accounting Policies and Chart of Accounts specification establishes the controlled rules governing accounting classifications, account creation, financial dimensions, reporting mappings, approvals, system configuration, and continuing maintenance. It connects accounting decisions with transaction processing, the general ledger, financial statements, donor reporting, budget reporting, and management information. The supplied specification defines eight accounting-policy control areas, including framework selection, policy evidence, COA architecture, dimension governance, reporting maps, transaction interfaces, configuration controls, and data-quality monitoring. It also defines proposed workflows, forms, acceptance criteria, and testing requirements. Importantly, the source is marked CONTROLLED DRAFT - NOT ADOPTED. Its fictional organization, account balances, codes, and implementation examples must not be treated as approved organizational facts. Before implementation, the organization must establish its actual legal identity, applicable accounting framework, reporting obligations, approved policies, responsible personnel, and system requirements. The specification should become operational only after the required independent reviews, approvals, testing, and implementation evidence are complete.

Study instruction:
- "Trace the relationship between accounting policy, the chart of accounts, financial dimensions, reporting maps, and system controls in the supplied specification."
Key point:
- A policy specification connects accounting decisions with the systems and controls that implement them.
- The supplied specification remains a draft until the required adoption conditions have been satisfied.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual
2. Q: How should an NGO determine which accounting framework applies to its legal entity and reporting purpose?
A: Show answer
Framework selection begins with the actual legal entity, legal form, jurisdiction, registration status, statutory reporting obligations, reporting perimeter, donor agreements, contractual obligations, and purpose of the financial statements. The organization must obtain the current authoritative requirements applicable to those facts. For an Indonesian entity, the relevant Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards and applicable tier must be assessed rather than assumed. IFRS or another framework should be considered only where an authoritative legal, contractual, institutional, or reporting basis supports its use. Different reports may legitimately serve different purposes, but their accounting bases and reconciliations must be explicitly documented. The supplied specification leaves the actual accounting framework, accounting basis, and functional currency unresolved. These are decision gates, not fields that should be populated using the fictional example or the accounting software's default settings. The framework decision should identify the selected framework and version, source evidence, applicability analysis, accounting-policy implications, responsible reviewer, authorized approval, effective date, and unresolved matters. Until the decision is approved, affected accounting rules must remain on hold.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a framework decision record using actual legal documents, current authoritative standards, reporting requirements, and authorized approval evidence."
Key point:
- Framework applicability depends on entity facts and reporting requirements, not nonprofit status alone.
- An unresolved framework decision must block affected operative policies.
Source links:
- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards effective 1 January 2025 — IAI
- IFRS Accounting Standards — issued standards
3. Q: Why must an NGO distinguish source verification from source applicability?
A: Show answer
A source may be genuine and authoritative without being applicable to a particular organization, transaction, or reporting period. The specification separates source existence and verification from applicability to the actual entity. For example, an official law concerning Indonesian foundations is relevant if the organization falls within its scope, but its existence does not independently establish the organization's legal form, accounting framework, tax treatment, or audit obligations. Similarly, the UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes accounting policies and processes used by the United Nations. Its discussion of general ledger controls and chart-of-accounts architecture can inform system design, but its existence does not itself make IPSAS mandatory for an Indonesian NGO. A source register should record the issuing authority, exact URL, document title, relevant section, checked date, effective period, jurisdiction, applicability assessment, reviewer, and next review trigger. Where later amendments or more current standards may apply, the original source record should direct the reviewer to the necessary updated material instead of being treated as conclusive evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Review the source register and distinguish verified source existence, current validity, entity applicability, and approval to use a rule."
Key point:
- An authoritative source is not automatically applicable in every context.
- Source verification and applicability assessment require separate documented conclusions.
Source links:
- General Ledger — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
- Umoja Overview — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2
4. Q: How should legal form, registration, statutory reporting, and audit requirements be established before accounting policies are adopted?
A: Show answer
The organization should assemble its registration records, governing documents, legal-entity information, jurisdictional details, and applicable regulatory or donor obligations. Legal or Compliance personnel should assess the applicable legislation, including amendments and implementation requirements, while the accounting lead evaluates the consequences for financial reporting. The organization must establish its statutory reporting and audit requirements from current authoritative sources rather than copying assumptions from a demonstration specification. For an Indonesian foundation, the Foundation Law and its amendments are relevant legal starting points, but their applicability must be considered against actual entity facts and consolidated current law. The outcome should be a documented assessment of the entity's legal status, reporting perimeter, applicable accounting framework, financial reporting obligations, audit requirements, and outstanding legal questions. Where legal status or a reporting obligation remains uncertain, the relevant policies and configurations must remain restricted from operational use until the uncertainty is resolved by an appropriately authorized reviewer.

Study instruction:
- "Create a legal-entity verification checklist and attach authoritative evidence for each reporting and audit conclusion."
Key point:
- Actual legal and regulatory facts must be established before entity-specific accounting policies are finalized.
- A legal-source citation alone is not proof that every obligation applies to the organization.
Source links:
- Law No. 16 of 2001 concerning Foundations — JDIH BPK
- Law No. 28 of 2004 amending the Foundation Law — JDIH BPK
5. Q: What should an accounting-policy register contain to make every important decision traceable?
A: Show answer
The register should identify each policy by a stable ID and title and record its purpose, operative rule, source and relevant paragraph, source version, effective period, applicability analysis, scope, assumptions, owner, independent reviewer, authorized approver, effective date, review trigger, exceptions, test requirements, and retained evidence. It should also identify the affected natural accounts, financial dimensions, report mappings, systems, transaction workflows, and related procedures. For example, a grant-recognition policy should connect the applicable accounting authority and agreement analysis to the relevant accounts, grant dimensions, reporting mappings, tests, and operating procedures. The register must distinguish draft controls from approved operative rules. A general website, an unverified internal manual, or a verbal instruction is insufficient evidence for a material accounting conclusion. Changes in law, standards, donor agreements, audit findings, transaction characteristics, or system configuration should trigger an assessment of whether the policy requires revision and reapproval.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect one proposed policy and verify that its source, applicability rationale, approvals, effective dates, tests, and system dependencies are fully recorded."
Key point:
- Every material accounting policy needs a defensible authority, a responsible owner, and a controlled approval record.
- The policy register must link decisions to implementation and retained evidence.
Source links:
- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards — IAI
6. Q: How should accounting policies be organized across statutory, donor, group, and management reporting?
A: Show answer
An organization may need several reporting views, but it should not silently combine incompatible accounting requirements. The policy hierarchy should identify the primary applicable accounting framework for the entity's financial statements and separately document additional reporting obligations arising from donor agreements, grant contracts, management needs, statutory rules, or group reporting. Each additional reporting view must state its purpose, rules, accountable owner, relevant mappings, and reconciliation to the underlying accounting records. For example, donor expenditure eligibility and financial-statement expense recognition may differ because a contract defines allowable funding charges while accounting standards govern financial reporting. The system must preserve that distinction rather than treating a donor budget classification as the accounting treatment. Any differences between reporting views should be explainable and supported by evidence. The hierarchy must also identify which authority resolves conflicts, where specialist review is required, and how changes affect previously issued reports and comparative information.

Study instruction:
- "Map the reporting obligations of one actual project and distinguish primary financial reporting rules from supplemental donor and management requirements."
Key point:
- Different reporting purposes require explicit rules and reconciliations.
- Donor eligibility does not replace accounting recognition and measurement requirements.
Source links:
- Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16
7. Q: What is the chart of accounts, and how does it support reliable financial reporting?
A: Show answer
A chart of accounts is the controlled catalogue of general ledger accounts used to classify accounting transactions and aggregate balances for financial reporting. It defines account identities, descriptions, classifications, normal balances, posting restrictions, hierarchies, ownership, effective dates, and links to reporting destinations. In the UN IPSAS Finance Manual, the chart of accounts is described in the context of producing IPSAS-compliant financial statements. For an NGO, the exact design must follow its own applicable accounting framework and reporting requirements. A well-designed COA helps ensure that transactions with similar accounting characteristics are recorded consistently, while financial dimensions identify additional facts such as the responsible office, funding pool, donor, grant, or project. The account catalogue should therefore remain separate from funding and operational classifications. New accounts must not be activated simply because a user requests a code; they require a valid accounting purpose, approved ownership, complete mappings, compatible dimensions, and tested system behavior.

Study instruction:
- "Review the proposed account dictionary and trace sample accounts from the general ledger to their financial-statement destinations."
Key point:
- The COA provides the controlled accounting-classification structure.
- The correct account structure must follow the applicable framework and reporting purpose.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Chart of Accounts — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
- Umoja Overview and Financial Accounting — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2
8. Q: Why should account codes be unique, stable, and stored as text where leading zeros are possible?
A: Show answer
Each account should have a stable identifier that distinguishes it from every other account in the controlled dictionary. The supplied specification proposes four-character numeric account codes stored as strings. Storing codes as text protects their identity when leading zeros are meaningful and prevents software from interpreting an identifier as a numeric quantity. The code's first digit can provide a convenient broad classification, but it must not be the sole validation rule. The system should also validate the account's full identity, classification, permitted uses, ownership, effective dates, posting status, and mappings. Descriptions can change as policies evolve, so the stable account identifier should remain the principal key rather than a name that users might edit. Before a new code is accepted, the requester should check existing, inactive, and retired accounts for duplicates or substantially similar purposes. The organization should preserve prior versions and historical mappings so that changes in the dictionary do not erase the meaning of earlier transactions.

Study instruction:
- "Test the account dictionary using a code with leading zeros, a duplicate request, and a retired account to verify key and lifecycle validation."
Key point:
- Stable keys protect account identity and historical traceability.
- Code format alone cannot establish accounting validity or posting authorization.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Chart of Accounts — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
9. Q: How should natural-account classifications and normal balances be controlled?
A: Show answer
Each account needs an approved classification and expected normal balance that reflects its accounting purpose under the applicable framework. The supplied demonstration groups accounts into assets, liabilities, net assets or fund balance, income or support, expenses, and other operating costs or adjustments. It illustrates the usual debit normal balance for asset and expense accounts and credit normal balance for liability, net-asset, and income accounts, while recognizing that particular account designs may require documented exceptions. These categories are illustrative rather than universally applicable account definitions for every NGO or accounting framework. Account setup should include the account type, natural class, normal balance, parent if applicable, posting permissions, control-account status, required and forbidden dimensions, and reporting destinations. Automated checks can flag unexpected balances, but a debit or credit balance by itself does not prove an error; the reviewer must investigate the transaction and account purpose. Any approved exception should have a documented rationale and appropriate review.

Study instruction:
- "Classify representative asset, liability, net-asset, income, and expense accounts, then investigate a balance that differs from its expected normal balance."
Key point:
- Normal balances provide useful validation and investigation signals.
- Classification and exceptions must be supported by the applicable framework and approved account design.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Account Groups — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
10. Q: What is the difference between a natural account and a financial dimension?
A: Show answer
A natural account describes the accounting classification of a transaction, such as operating cash, receivables, payables, equipment, grant income, program costs, or administration costs. A financial dimension describes another attribute of the transaction, such as the legal entity, office, fund, project, donor, grant, or cost centre. These dimensions answer questions that a natural-account code cannot answer on its own. For example, an NGO may record a valid program expense in one natural account but need the project, donor, fund, grant, and office dimensions to determine which report should include it and who is responsible for the cost. Combining every possible attribute into one oversized account code can create unnecessary complexity and complicate changes in donor or organizational structure. The supplied specification consequently keeps natural-account classification and dimensions separate. The accounting system should validate their permitted combinations and maintain their histories independently.

Study instruction:
- "Take one program transaction and identify the natural account, funding attributes, project identity, donor, grant, and responsible cost centre separately."
Key point:
- Natural accounts classify transactions; dimensions provide additional reporting and accountability attributes.
- Required dimensions must be configured according to account and reporting needs.
Source links:
- Financial Accounting Coding Block — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2
11. Q: How should financial dimensions be defined and assigned to accountable owners?
A: Show answer
Every dimension should have a stable ID, unique code, clear purpose, accountable owner, authoritative source, lifecycle status, validity dates, access classification, and defined reporting use. The supplied specification identifies seven foundational dimensions: legal entity, office, fund, project, donor, grant or award, and cost centre. Each has a different purpose. A legal entity defines the legal reporting perimeter, while an office ordinarily identifies an operational location. A fund represents a funding pool, restriction, or designated purpose. Projects identify approved activities; donor and grant dimensions identify funding counterparties and award-specific terms; cost centres identify responsibility and allocation ownership. A master-data owner should approve definitions and coordinate with the relevant operational or grants owner. Codes must be supported by actual documentation, such as an approved project record or executed agreement. The dimension register must also specify which accounts require each dimension, which combinations are permitted, and when values should become inactive or retired.

Study instruction:
- "Build a dimension dictionary with the seven proposed dimension types, their owners, authoritative sources, intended uses, and permitted reporting applications."
Key point:
- Every dimension needs an explicit purpose and accountable owner.
- Master-data values must reflect verified organizational facts rather than fictional demonstration records.
Source links:
- Umoja Coding Block and Master Data Concepts — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2
12. Q: Why does an approved donor or grant code not prove that expenditure is eligible for donor funding?
A: Show answer
A donor or grant code identifies a reporting or contractual relationship; it does not itself authorize a payment, establish an available budget, or prove that a particular cost is eligible. The grant agreement and any approved amendments must establish applicable restrictions, project scope, funding period, allowable expenditure conditions, reporting obligations, and approval requirements. The grant owner should verify the agreement and set up the appropriate master-data values, while Finance independently verifies the account and dimension compatibility and applicable policy. If a donor code exists but its agreement is incomplete or its effective period cannot be established, the related configuration should not be treated as approved. Even where an expense can be mapped to a donor report, that mapping does not conclusively determine the accounting recognition or contractual eligibility of the expense. These are separate decisions and must be supported by the relevant accounting authority and agreement terms.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an executed grant agreement with its donor, grant, fund, and project master records and verify the supporting evidence for an eligible cost."
Key point:
- A code is an identifier, not evidence of donor authorization or expenditure eligibility.
- Accounting classification and donor eligibility require separate assessment.
Source links:
- Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
13. Q: How should financial dimensions and account combinations be validated before a transaction is posted?
A: Show answer
Validation should check account-specific required and forbidden dimensions, compatible funding and project combinations, donor and grant restrictions, entity and ledger scope, reporting mappings, and effective dates. For example, the supplied fictional specification permits a particular restricted education fund with the designated education project, donor, grant, program cost centre, and approved offices. It prohibits an unrestricted fund from being combined with that same restricted project under the example agreement. This is an illustrative control design, not a real donor restriction or universal rule. For the actual organization, each restriction must be supported by an approved source or agreement. Invalid combinations should fail closed: the system should block posting or activation and route the issue to the responsible owner instead of accepting an undocumented manual override. Any permitted exception must have a clear basis, authorized independent approval, limited scope and duration, testing, and closure evidence. Mandatory legal or donor requirements cannot be waived by internal approval.

Study instruction:
- "Create positive and negative tests for required dimensions and restricted-fund combinations, using actual approved agreements when testing a live system."
Key point:
- Dimension validation prevents unsupported or prohibited combinations from entering the ledger.
- Fictional compatibility rules must not be copied into production without actual evidence and approval.
Source links:
- General Ledger Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
14. Q: Why must financial dimensions have effective dates and controlled lifecycle statuses?
A: Show answer
Financial dimensions should pass through documented stages such as draft, review, approval, active, suspended, and retired. Effective-from and effective-to dates determine the periods in which an approved value is valid for use. These controls help prevent posting to projects or grants outside their permitted period and enable reviewers to identify the code definition that applied to historical transactions. The supplied specification includes an illustrative education project and grant that expire on 31 December 2026. That expiration is fictional and must not be interpreted as a real project deadline. In an actual system, the relevant agreement, project approval, or organizational decision must determine validity. Changes to a dimension's restriction, parent, owner, or definition should trigger an impact assessment, review of affected transactions, and new tests where necessary. Old values should not simply be deleted, because earlier postings and reports must remain traceable to the master data that governed them at the time.

Study instruction:
- "Test a transaction using a dimension before, during, and after its authorized effective period, and verify that historical records remain identifiable."
Key point:
- Effective dates control when a master-data value may be used.
- Historical validity and traceability must survive deactivation or retirement.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Master-Data Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
15. Q: How should the organization manage requests for a new account, dimension, or accounting-policy change?
A: Show answer
A change request should have a unique reference and explain the business need, proposed rule or code, source authority, requester, accountable owner, expected usage, applicable entity and ledger, financial dimensions, valid period, reporting consequences, system impact, risks, proposed tests, and desired implementation date. The request must be screened against existing, inactive, and retired records to detect duplicate or unnecessary codes. The independent reviewer evaluates classification, source applicability, permitted combinations, reporting effects, delegated authority, and supporting evidence. Incomplete or unsupported requests should be returned or held with a named owner and due date. Approved changes must be tied to an identifiable content version and effective date. This process prevents informal requests, familiar account labels, or historical practice from becoming accounting policy without a proper decision and an auditable implementation path.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a sample account-creation request and identify the evidence and tests required before it can be approved and implemented."
Key point:
- Master-data and policy changes begin with documented business needs and authoritative support.
- Duplicate screening and independent approval must precede activation.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Chart of Accounts — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
16. Q: What should an independent reviewer verify before approving a new account or policy?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should verify that the request answers a genuine need, identifies an authoritative basis, and uses a classification consistent with the applicable accounting framework. For a COA change, the reviewer should check uniqueness, account type, normal balance, posting permissions, control-account treatment, entity and ledger scope, required dimensions, forbidden combinations, owners, effective dates, and mappings to every required reporting view. For a policy change, the reviewer should also consider source currency, scope, assumptions, alternatives, relevant contracts, tax or donor implications, affected procedures, and implementation dependencies. The reviewer must establish that the proposed approval is within delegated authority and that the requester or drafter has not served as the sole independent approver. Approval must identify the exact version reviewed. When relevant content changes afterward, the earlier approval and associated tests must be reassessed rather than carried forward automatically.

Study instruction:
- "Use the independent review checklist to assess a proposed COA change, including source evidence, delegated authority, mapping effects, and version identity."
Key point:
- Approval requires both technical correctness and authority to approve.
- Approval applies to the reviewed version, not to any later file with a similar name.
Source links:
- General Ledger Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
17. Q: How should roles, delegated authority, and segregation of duties operate in accounting-policy governance?
A: Show answer
The governance model should distinguish requesting, policy drafting, independent review, authorization, technical implementation, testing, and post-release verification. Where resources do not permit complete separation, the organization must identify conflicts and establish documented compensating controls. Delegated authority must be verified by role, scope, legal entity, effective dates, currency, and limit. A person's job title alone does not establish permission to approve a material accounting-policy decision or authorize a production release. The source specification proposes governance roles including the Chief Accountant or Finance Director, policy owner, COA owner, financial reporting owner, grants or program data owner, and independent Finance approver. The final organization-specific authority matrix must identify actual role holders or approved positions, substitutes, escalation routes, and the decisions each role may make. Exceptions should not permit an individual to bypass mandatory external requirements or independently authorize their own high-risk system changes.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a RACI and delegation matrix and identify any process in which the requester, approver, implementer, and verifier are the same person."
Key point:
- Segregation of duties reduces unauthorized changes and unchecked errors.
- Delegated authority must be evidenced, scoped, current, and independently monitored.
Source links:
- General Ledger Process and Control — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
18. Q: How should account mappings connect the general ledger to financial statements and other reporting views?
A: Show answer
A reporting map connects source accounts or dimensions to defined destination categories, such as financial-statement lines, donor-report categories, budget classifications, statutory reports, tax views, management reports, or consolidation structures. Each map should contain stable source and destination identifiers, transformation logic, applicable scope, owner, approval, effective dates, history rules, and test evidence. The mapping specification must explain whether an item maps to one destination or is allocated across several destinations under an approved split rule. A mapping that merely looks plausible is insufficient: the report owner must confirm its purpose and intended accounting meaning. The organization must also distinguish accounting presentation from donor eligibility and tax classification. Those views can draw on the same source transaction but may apply different rules. Before release, all material mapping differences and any unmapped items must be explained and resolved or handled through a formally approved exception where permitted.

Study instruction:
- "Trace sample accounts to each required reporting destination and identify where the underlying classification differs from donor, tax, or budget presentation."
Key point:
- Reporting maps translate controlled ledger classifications into defined reporting views.
- Financial-statement presentation, donor eligibility, budget treatment, and tax treatment must not be conflated.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Chart of Accounts — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
- Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16
19. Q: Why must every used account map to the required reporting view exactly once unless an approved split rule applies?
A: Show answer
Missing, overlapping, or multiple mappings can cause financial statements to omit balances, count them twice, or place them in the wrong category. The specification expects complete mapping coverage and a single applicable destination for each used value in each reporting view unless a documented allocation or split rule justifies a different design. This rule must be interpreted at the appropriate reporting level: an account may legitimately be handled differently across distinct views, but its treatment within a given view must be explicit and testable. The mapping register should identify effective dates so historical transactions are not automatically reclassified using today's rules. Automated tests should detect unmapped accounts, conflicting destinations, expired maps, duplicate assignments, and unexpected changes in the population. Where a split or allocation is authorized, the calculation method, evidence, rounding policy, and reconciliation to the source total must be documented and tested independently.

Study instruction:
- "Create test records for an unmapped account, an overlapping mapping, and a legitimate approved split, then compare the reporting results."
Key point:
- Mapping completeness and non-duplication are critical reporting controls.
- An approved split requires documented logic and reconciliation to the original amount.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
20. Q: How should financial reporting maps be reconciled and their control totals validated?
A: Show answer
The reconciliation process should compare the source population and balances with the results produced by every required reporting map. Tests must establish that all eligible source records are included, no record is inadvertently double-counted, and approved transformations preserve the required total. The supplied specification calls for independent decimal-safe arithmetic and reconciliation of synthetic trial balances and declared reporting views. Decimal-safe calculations are important because binary floating-point representations can produce small rounding discrepancies in monetary calculations. The test should compare source balances, mapped balances, allocation results, and report control totals under the approved currency and rounding conventions. Any difference must be investigated for missing mappings, duplicates, rounding, invalid classifications, transformation errors, or out-of-period assignments. The reviewer must retain the underlying data, transformation version, calculation output, explanations, corrections, approvals, and independent retest results. A reconciliation is complete only when the differences have been resolved or handled through a formally authorized process that does not compromise mandatory reporting requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile an illustrative trial balance to each required report and demonstrate how the control process detects both missing and duplicated mappings."
Key point:
- Report totals must be reconciled to the underlying accounting population.
- Mapping tests need reproducible calculations and retained review evidence.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Reconciliations — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
21. Q: How should changes in account mappings affect historical reports and comparative figures?
A: Show answer
Mapping changes should be version-controlled and effective-dated rather than applied retrospectively by silently overwriting the previous rule. The organization must preserve the mapping that applied during each historical period and assess the consequences of the new mapping for financial statements, donor reports, budget analysis, tax classifications, statutory views, and management reporting. If historical comparatives need restatement or re-presentation, the decision must follow the applicable accounting framework and be approved with clear evidence. A change log should record the previous and new destination, reason for change, affected accounts and periods, expected effects, testing, reviewer, approval, and effective date. Reports should either retain their original historical treatment or be regenerated under a formally authorized revised basis, with the distinction clearly documented. Version history makes it possible to reproduce the original report and explain why a subsequent presentation differs.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an old and new mapping version and prepare an impact assessment for current-period results and prior-period comparatives."
Key point:
- Historical mapping rules must remain recoverable and identifiable.
- Retrospective reporting changes require an approved rationale under the applicable framework.
Source links:
- Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16
22. Q: How should grant recognition, restricted resources, and conditional funding be addressed in accounting policy?
A: Show answer
Grant accounting must be determined from the applicable accounting framework, the substance of the transaction, enforceable agreement terms, relevant conditions or restrictions, repayment obligations, and the organization's rights and obligations. The policy register should explain recognition and measurement decisions for each relevant class of funding, including circumstances in which income, a liability, a receivable, or another balance-sheet item may be appropriate. The source specification expressly warns against assuming that every grant is income, a liability, or a conditional contribution. A grant's label, donor code, cash receipt, or funding restriction alone is not enough to decide accounting treatment. The organization must also distinguish accounting recognition from donor expenditure eligibility and fund-management restrictions. Where a transaction's substance or agreement terms create uncertainty, the Grants and Finance owners should obtain competent review, document the conclusion, and ensure that the appropriate COA and reporting mappings reflect the approved treatment.

Study instruction:
- "Review an actual grant agreement and classify its recognition, measurement, restrictions, and repayment conditions under the organization's approved accounting framework."
Key point:
- Grant recognition depends on the applicable framework and transaction substance, not the grant label alone.
- Donor restrictions, accounting treatment, and expenditure eligibility are related but distinct questions.
Source links:
- Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
23. Q: How should accounting policy address foreign currency and foreign-exchange reporting?
A: Show answer
The policy should establish the functional currency and presentation currency under the applicable accounting framework and document how foreign-currency transactions, period-end balances, realized exchange differences, and unrealized exchange differences are treated. Currency choices cannot simply be inherited from an example workbook. The specification's use of IDR is a fictional assumption rather than an approved determination for the actual entity. The organization must document the relevant currency assessment and applicable translation or remeasurement requirements, including rate sources, dates, calculation methods, permitted exceptions, and the accounts affected. Finance should coordinate these requirements with the general ledger, reporting maps, period-end close procedures, and any statutory or donor reports. Exchange-rate rules and configuration parameters must be tested against independently calculated examples and effective-dated when changed. Any unexplained exchange difference or inconsistent currency treatment should be investigated before the reporting period is certified.

Study instruction:
- "Document the actual functional and presentation currency decisions, then test one foreign-currency transaction and one period-end balance under the applicable framework."
Key point:
- Currency selection and foreign-exchange accounting are framework-dependent decisions.
- Rate sources, transformation rules, journals, and reports must be traceable and independently tested.
Source links:
- IFRS Accounting Standards — issued standards
24. Q: How should materiality, capitalization thresholds, and allocation parameters be established and maintained?
A: Show answer
These parameters should be treated as controlled policy decisions rather than convenient spreadsheet defaults. Each parameter needs an approved value or methodology, currency, effective date, scope, rationale, policy owner, approver, and documented application rules. Depending on the decision, relevant factors may include the applicable accounting framework, expected useful life, transaction nature, reporting materiality, donor agreement, local requirements, or the organization's own approved policy. The source specification deliberately leaves materiality, capitalization, allocation, and delegation thresholds as verification placeholders. An example threshold cannot be copied into the actual policy without authorized assessment. Systems should apply the approved parameter consistently and retain enough information to reproduce the outcome. When a threshold changes, Finance must evaluate affected accounts, transaction populations, prior decisions, report mappings, testing, and training needs. Changes should become effective only on the authorized date and in accordance with applicable accounting requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a threshold approval record and test its behavior before and after the proposed effective date, including transactions close to the boundary."
Key point:
- Materiality and capitalization parameters require authorized, effective-dated decisions.
- Unapproved template defaults must never become organizational policy through accidental use.
Source links:
- DSAK updates and standards effective-date developments — IAI
25. Q: How should prior-period errors, changes in estimates, policy changes, and reclassifications be distinguished?
A: Show answer
These events can have different accounting consequences and should not be grouped under a generic account-mapping correction. A prior-period error, a change in accounting estimate, an authorized accounting-policy change, a presentation reclassification, and a change to a reporting map must be identified separately and assessed under the applicable framework. The organization should document the nature of the event, discovery date, affected period, source requirement, accounts and reports affected, financial effect, proposed correction, approval, and implications for comparative information. A correction to an incorrect account code may leave the transaction's economic substance unchanged, whereas a change in recognition policy may affect the timing or measurement of balances. Changing a map does not automatically correct an underlying accounting error. The organization must preserve the original record and correction history and must not silently rewrite historical policy or reporting logic. Specialists should review matters that could materially change published financial statements or statutory reports.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample events as error correction, estimate change, policy change, reclassification, or mapping change and explain the required evidence and approvals."
Key point:
- Different types of accounting changes require different analyses and treatments.
- Historical records and the rationale for any correction must remain traceable.
Source links:
- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards — IAI
- IFRS Accounting Standards — issued standards
26. Q: How should account restrictions and special posting permissions be designed?
A: Show answer
The account dictionary should identify whether an account accepts ordinary postings, functions as a control account, requires particular dimensions, or is reserved for controlled system processes. Restrictions should be based on an approved accounting and system design, not informal preference. The supplied specification illustrates accumulated net assets as a non-postable control account in its fictional case, while operating cash, payables, and several other accounts permit postings. These settings are examples, not universal requirements for actual organizations. Before activation, the COA owner must establish the relevant account purpose, source policy, posting rules, required dimensions, ledger scope, and reporting destinations. The system should block inappropriate postings and preserve the reason for any authorized exception. Post-release verification must confirm that the production configuration matches the approved version and that invalid postings cannot bypass the controls.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect the posting restrictions for control accounts and ordinary accounts, then perform positive and negative posting tests in a controlled environment."
Key point:
- Posting permissions are part of account governance and must be tested before release.
- Example account settings must not be treated as live configuration instructions.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Account Groups — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
27. Q: What evidence should be retained for account, dimension, and reporting-map changes?
A: Show answer
A complete change file should connect the original request to the approved decision, implementation, verification, and communication. It should contain the request ID, proposed change, supporting source or agreement, analysis of affected policies and reports, reviewer comments, authorized approval, approved version, effective date, system configuration record, positive and negative test outcomes, deployment reference, access checks, rollback preparation, and independent post-release verification. Where mapping changes affect historical periods, the file should also preserve the old and new maps and the analysis of comparative and reporting impacts. The retention schedule must be confirmed against applicable laws, donor terms, contractual requirements, privacy controls, and organizational policy. Evidence should be stored in a controlled repository with appropriate access restrictions rather than scattered across personal folders or email attachments. Changes that cannot be supported by a complete evidence trail should not be treated as fully closed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one change request from initiation through approval, testing, deployment, communication, and archived closure evidence."
Key point:
- An audit trail must show what changed, why, who approved it, and how the change was tested.
- Retention and access rules must follow verified organizational and legal requirements.
Source links:
- General Ledger Process and Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
28. Q: How should accounting-policy and COA changes be tested before they are released to production?
A: Show answer
Changes must be tested in a controlled environment before production deployment. The test plan should include positive cases for valid accounts and dimension combinations, negative cases for prohibited combinations, duplicate and invalid-code checks, effective-date checks, financial-statement mapping coverage, historical-map behavior, total reconciliation, user permissions, and segregation-of-duties enforcement. Changes that affect data migration should also test source and target populations, control totals, duplicate handling, unmatched records, and preservation of historical information. The source specification defines critical tests such as framework-hold behavior, unique COA codes, mapping coverage, decimal-safe total reconciliation, dimension compatibility, approval controls, delegated-authority expiry, and rejection of inactive codes. Each test should have a defined expected result, actual result, evidence reference, tester, reviewer, and pass, fail, or blocked status. A passed test set must apply to the exact approved version proposed for deployment.

Study instruction:
- "Create a test matrix covering valid and invalid account requests, dimension combinations, mapping completeness, historical records, and role conflicts."
Key point:
- Testing must establish both that valid transactions work and that prohibited conditions are blocked.
- Approval and test results must correspond to the same controlled version.
Source links:
- General Ledger Controls and Configuration — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
29. Q: What is the difference between a positive test and a negative test in accounting-system configuration?
A: Show answer
A positive test confirms that a permitted operation succeeds under the approved conditions. For example, an account configured with all required dimensions and a valid effective date should pass the relevant validation checks. A negative test deliberately presents a prohibited condition, such as a duplicate code, an expired grant, an unauthorized account combination, a posting to an inactive code, or an approval performed by an ineligible user. The system should reject or appropriately hold the invalid operation and record sufficient information for investigation. Both test types are required because a process that accepts valid transactions may still fail to enforce restrictions. Results should be compared with explicit expected outcomes rather than judged informally. Where a critical negative test is not performed or cannot be validated, the affected control must remain blocked or unverified rather than being marked as passed merely because no failure has yet been reported.

Study instruction:
- "For each critical COA rule, prepare one valid example and one deliberately invalid example and document the system's actual response."
Key point:
- Positive tests establish expected functionality; negative tests establish that prohibited actions are blocked.
- Missing or unverified critical tests must not be reported as successful.
Source links:
- Financial Accounting Policy and COA Specification — supplied YAML
30. Q: How should production deployment, rollback, and post-release verification be controlled?
A: Show answer
Deployment should begin only after the approved source version, authorized release, required tests, backup or rollback preparation, role checks, and affected-party communication are complete. The implementer should use the approved configuration in a controlled environment and record the deployment ID, objects changed, implementation time, and any errors. An independent verifier should then confirm that the live configuration matches the approved source and that affected mappings, dimensions, posting restrictions, access controls, and report control totals remain correct. If deployment fails, the documented rollback process should restore the approved prior configuration or another authorized safe state while preserving audit history. Emergency changes must be limited in scope and duration, logged, authorized through the designated urgent route, and subjected to independent after-action review. A deployment is not complete merely because the software accepted the changes; the organization must prove that the expected accounting and reporting outcomes remain correct.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a configuration release and failure scenario and verify the required approval, rollback, reconciliation, and post-release evidence."
Key point:
- Production deployment requires approval, testing, controlled implementation, and independent verification.
- Rollback readiness protects the organization against failed or incorrect changes.
Source links:
- Financial Accounting Policy and COA Specification — supplied YAML
31. Q: How should legacy account migration and crosswalks be validated during a system change?
A: Show answer
Migration should preserve the relationship between legacy codes, approved destination accounts, financial dimensions, reporting mappings, and historical transaction records. The migration specification should record the source and target populations, transformation rules, duplicate detection, unmatched codes, retired accounts, valid periods, and any approved one-to-many or many-to-one relationships. The organization should reconcile record counts and monetary totals before and after transformation and document every exception. Historical reporting must remain reproducible using the appropriate effective-dated mapping unless an authorized accounting decision requires a different treatment. Migration testing must also verify that prohibited accounts and dimensions do not become active inadvertently and that the destination configuration preserves required controls. Unresolved mappings or materially unexplained differences should prevent sign-off until corrected or formally handled under an approved procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Design a legacy-to-new COA crosswalk and reconcile account populations, transaction totals, historical mapping, and unresolved codes."
Key point:
- Successful migration requires both accurate data transfer and preservation of historical meaning.
- Unmapped records and unexplained differences must be resolved before migration acceptance.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
32. Q: When may an account or financial dimension be deactivated or retired?
A: Show answer
Deactivation or retirement should follow a documented review of outstanding balances, open transactions, commitments, linked agreements, remaining reporting obligations, system interfaces, and historical records. Inactivity alone does not prove that a value is safe to retire. The organization should identify whether transactions can still be posted to it, whether an approved successor exists, and how historical reports will continue to use the correct former classification. The reviewer should assess whether the change creates duplicate mappings, stranded balances, incompatible dimension combinations, or discrepancies in reports. Retirement approval should identify the effective date, accountable owner, reason, replacement mapping where appropriate, and required system changes. The retirement process must block unauthorized reuse while retaining sufficient history to support audit and reporting. Where the account or dimension remains necessary for unresolved transactions or historical reporting, retirement should be delayed or restricted rather than forced to meet an administrative target.

Study instruction:
- "Assess a proposed account retirement against its balances, open commitments, reporting history, system dependencies, and replacement arrangements."
Key point:
- Retirement requires evidence that operational and historical obligations have been addressed.
- Old master-data values must remain traceable after they cease to be usable for new postings.
Source links:
- Financial Accounting Policy and COA Specification — supplied YAML
33. Q: How should an urgent accounting-policy issue or invalid account combination be handled?
A: Show answer
The organization should log the issue, identify the financial periods and transactions affected, assess the accounting, donor, statutory, security, and operational risks, and appoint a responsible owner. The affected posting or configuration should be held where continuing the process could create an unauthorized or materially incorrect result. The case must be referred to the appropriate Accounting, Legal, Tax, Grants, or system specialist depending on its nature. Any permitted temporary control should have documented authority, limited scope, a defined expiry date, an accountable owner, and independent review. Urgency does not justify silently overriding donor restrictions, statutory requirements, or other mandatory obligations. If the case involves suspected fraud, a data breach, or unauthorized privileged access, the dedicated security or incident-response procedure takes priority over ordinary master-data processing. Closure requires evidence of the decision, correction, communication, testing, and preventive action needed to stop recurrence.

Study instruction:
- "Work through an invalid restricted-fund posting scenario and document containment, specialist escalation, authorized resolution, retesting, and closure."
Key point:
- Exceptions need risk assessment, accountable ownership, and documented resolution.
- Mandatory external requirements cannot be waived through an internal emergency approval.
Source links:
- General Ledger Process and Control — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
34. Q: What should be included in an exception, incident, and corrective-action register?
A: Show answer
The register should capture a unique case ID, discovery date, severity, evidence, affected periods, accounts, dimensions, mappings and reports, estimated financial or donor impact, containment action, assigned owner, deadline, specialist referral, required approval, corrective measure, retest, and verified closure. It should distinguish ordinary data-quality exceptions from significant accounting questions, unauthorized configuration changes, suspected fraud, and security incidents. Repeated errors should be analyzed for root cause rather than treated as isolated operator mistakes. For instance, repeated invalid combinations may indicate incorrect master-data definitions or missing system validation rather than a need for more manual reminders. Management should receive information about overdue cases and recurring causes in accordance with an approved risk-based escalation policy. Closure must be supported by evidence that the problem was corrected and that the revised control operates as intended.

Study instruction:
- "Create a corrective-action record for a recurring mapping error and include root cause, evidence, control redesign, owner, deadline, and independent retest."
Key point:
- The register must support resolution, accountability, root-cause analysis, and prevention of recurrence.
- A case is not closed solely because its status has been changed in a spreadsheet.

- Financial Accounting Policy and COA Specification — supplied YAML
35. Q: How should the organization monitor whether accounting-policy and COA controls remain effective?
A: Show answer
Monitoring should assess the continuing accuracy of master data, account mappings, approval records, effective dates, posting restrictions, source references, and supporting reconciliations. The supplied specification proposes indicators including the percentage of used accounts with approved effective-dated financial-statement mappings, counts of postings to inactive or out-of-period values, invalid account and dimension combinations, reconciliation differences, overdue policy reviews, unresolved applicability decisions, exception recurrence, and verified time to closure. Each indicator needs a defined population, calculation method, owner, frequency, threshold, escalation rule, and retained evidence. For example, a mapping-coverage indicator must use the relevant population of used accounts rather than only accounts that already have mappings. Where the organization has no verified framework or operative mapping decision, the status should clearly reflect that limitation instead of reporting complete coverage as established compliance. Management should review trends and require corrective actions when performance deteriorates.

Study instruction:
- "Define the denominator, calculation, owner, review frequency, and escalation rule for each proposed KPI, then test the indicators against source records."
Key point:
- Monitoring measures must be reproducible and based on a defined, reliable population.
- Dashboard status cannot replace source verification, reconciliation, and independent review.

- Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16
36. Q: How should access control, privileged implementation, and audit logging be designed?
A: Show answer
Access should be assigned according to each person's approved responsibilities and the principle of least privilege. The access register should record the user or role, scope, legal entity, approval, effective dates, and revocation date when access ends. Privileged users who can change accounts, dimensions, mappings, or system rules must not be able to authorize their own high-risk changes without independent control. Access changes should be approved, logged, and reviewed periodically. The audit trail should retain the identity of the actor, timestamp, action, reason, previous and new values, source version, approval, test evidence, deployment reference, and post-release verification. Shared accounts or actions that cannot be reliably attributed to an individual create an accountability weakness and should be escalated. Donor information, employee information, and other sensitive records should be restricted to authorized users and kept out of unnecessary fields in the COA register.

Study instruction:
- "Review actual system roles and sample audit events to verify access authorization, individual attribution, independent approvals, and timely revocation."
Key point:
- Access should reflect authorized duties and be traceable to individual accountability.
- Privileged changes require additional controls and independent review.
Source links:
- General Ledger Process and Control — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
37. Q: How should policy and master-data source changes be monitored over time?
A: Show answer
The organization should assign responsibility for monitoring authoritative standards, legislation, regulator guidance, donor agreements, internal policies, and relevant system changes. Each change assessment should record the source, issue date, effective date, affected entity and transaction classes, interpretation, applicable policy records, accounts, dimensions, reporting maps, tests, training implications, and approval requirements. The specification proposes annual reviews, together with event-triggered reviews when regulations, reporting purposes, donor agreements, audits, or system configurations change. Annual review is only a minimum fallback for routine monitoring, not a substitute for assessing material changes as they arise. A source update should not automatically change an accounting rule: the owner must establish applicability, assess the consequences, and obtain approval before revising operative policy. The organization must also preserve prior source and policy versions needed to explain historical decisions and prior-period reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Select one authoritative-source update and document the impact assessment, applicability decision, policy revision, test plan, approval, and communication."
Key point:
- Changes in authoritative guidance require a documented applicability and impact assessment.
- Source updates must not silently modify approved accounting rules or historic records.

- DSAK standard-setting updates — IAI
38. Q: How should the accounting-policy specification interface with journal, closing, grant, donor-reporting, tax, procurement, and payroll procedures?
A: Show answer
The specification should establish the policy and valid account or dimension rules while leaving transaction execution to the dedicated procedure responsible for that activity. The source identifies interfaces for journal preparation and posting, month-end and year-end close, grant agreement review, donor financial reporting, tax compliance, accounts payable and payments, payroll and employee benefits, and security or incident response. This division prevents the policy document from becoming an uncontrolled substitute for actual workflow procedures. For example, the accounting policy defines the applicable account and reporting treatment, while the journal SOP specifies preparation, review, posting, and evidence requirements. The tax SOP remains responsible for tax conclusions and statutory filings, subject to appropriate tax-owner review. Each interface should identify the receiving procedure, process owner, input and output records, approval boundaries, reconciliation requirements, and escalation route. Any policy change that affects an adjacent process should trigger an impact assessment and necessary procedure updates before implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Build a process-interface matrix identifying the controlling policy, receiving SOP, accountable owner, required evidence, and unresolved dependencies for each interface."
Key point:
- Policy governance and transaction execution are related but distinct responsibilities.
- Adjacent procedures must remain consistent with the approved accounting policy and COA design.

39. Q: What controlled forms and registers should support implementation of accounting policies and the COA?
A: Show answer
The supplied specification identifies a set of required annexes and controlled forms supporting framework decisions, authoritative-source verification, accounting-policy decisions, account creation, financial-dimension management, compatibility rules, reporting crosswalks, approval authority, system configuration, migration, change management, reconciliations, exceptions, training, periodic reviews, audit trails, monitoring, workflow diagrams, and validation tests. Each form or register should have a unique identifier, accountable owner, prescribed data fields, source references, reviewer or approver, applicable version, effective dates, status, and closure evidence. These forms should not be treated as independent documents with conflicting or duplicated rules. Each must connect to the canonical specification and be stored in the approved repository with appropriate access controls and retention settings. Incomplete requests, missing approvals, and unresolved evidence should remain visible in the tracking system rather than being marked complete simply because a form has been submitted.

Study instruction:
- "Inventory the required forms and registers, then check their ownership, required fields, linked controls, and evidence of approval."
Key point:
- Controlled forms turn policy requirements into repeatable, auditable work.

40. Q: How should implementation readiness be assessed before the policy and COA go live?
A: Show answer
Readiness requires more than a completed document or successful code generation. The specification's implementation checklist requires confirmation of the actual legal entity and reporting perimeter, competent review of current authoritative sources, approval of the framework and accounting basis, approval of policy hierarchy and currencies, authorization of the COA and dimensions, valid reporting maps, and a current delegation matrix. The system must then be configured and tested for access controls, duplicate prevention, effective dating, audit logs, posting restrictions, and mapping rules. Positive and negative tests, reconciliation tests, migration checks, historical-map tests, and segregation-of-duties checks must be complete. Before release, the organization must verify the approved configuration, communication and training, rollback arrangements, post-release checks, and retained evidence. Where actual entity requirements remain placeholders or framework applicability remains unverified, implementation must not be declared fully adopted for that entity.

Study instruction:
- "Use the implementation checklist to classify each requirement as complete, partial, or failed and identify the evidence required to close outstanding items."
Key point:

41. Q: Why must the fictional demonstration data remain separate from actual organizational accounting records?
A: Show answer
The specification uses a fictional entity called Harborlight Learning Network and a fictional foundation, offices, program, donor, grant, ERP, source systems, balances, and approval records. These examples demonstrate how a policy decision, account dictionary, financial dimensions, or reconciliation might be represented in a structured specification. They are not evidence that an actual Indonesian foundation exists, that any real donor has approved a restriction, that a given account is authorized, or that the stated balances are genuine. The source explicitly states that its fictional rules, codes, thresholds, interpretations, approvals, and evidence references cannot establish actual organizational requirements. Implementation teams should maintain a clear separation between demonstration fixtures and live master data. Before real-world use, every material field must be replaced or confirmed using the actual entity's documents, authoritative requirements, authorized policies, agreements, and system records. Fictional test results should be labelled as such and must never be reused as proof that production controls have passed.

Study instruction:
- "Identify all fictional entities, codes, balances, approvals, and evidence references in the specification and define how they will be segregated from live records."
Key point:
- Demonstration data helps test the design but does not establish real-world facts or authorization.
- Actual records require independent verification and authorized adoption.

- Financial Accounting Policy and COA Specification — supplied YAML
42. Q: How should the illustrative trial balance be used to test account and reporting controls?
A: Show answer
The supplied specification includes a fictional trial balance in IDR with sample asset, liability, net-asset, income, and expense balances. Its declared control total is zero, illustrating the principle that a balanced double-entry trial balance should have equal total debits and credits under the stated sign convention. A balancing total does not prove that the accounting classification, recognition, measurement, dimensions, or reporting mappings are correct. The test must also verify that every account used is valid, required dimensions are present, incompatible combinations are rejected, and each account has the required effective-dated reporting mapping. The trial balance should be run through the mapping process and the totals compared across source and destination reports. Calculations should use suitable decimal-safe arithmetic and a clearly defined sign convention. Because these numbers are fictional, they must not be used as an organization's opening balances or evidence of a live ledger reconciliation.

Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the fictional trial balance check, validate its account population, and reconcile its mapped totals without treating the sample balances as real."
Key point:
- A balanced trial balance is necessary but not sufficient evidence of correct accounting.
- Classification, dimension validation, mapping coverage, and report reconciliation must also be tested.
Source links:
- General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
43. Q: How should data quality, duplicate prevention, and controlled master-data maintenance be incorporated into the COA process?
A: Show answer
Data-quality controls should operate before activation and continue during periodic reconciliation. The organization should screen account and dimension requests against current, inactive, and retired values; validate code structure and uniqueness; verify authoritative source references; and check compatibility with entity, ledger, account, and reporting scope. Periodically, the live system dictionary should be compared with the approved register so that unauthorized, missing, or out-of-date values are identified. The organization should also review invalid combinations, unapproved posting codes, stale reporting maps, overdue source reviews, and unexplained differences between master data and system configuration. Corrections must be logged, approved where required, and tested independently. A data-quality issue that affects a material balance or reporting output must remain open or block the affected release until it has been addressed through a controlled resolution process. Repeated errors should lead to root-cause analysis and system or process redesign where needed.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the live COA and dimension dictionary to the approved register, then document duplicate, stale, invalid, and unauthorized values."
Key point:
- Master-data integrity requires preventive validation and continuing detective review.
- Data-quality issues must be resolved with evidence and appropriate independent testing.

- General Ledger and Chart of Accounts Controls — United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
44. Q: How should training, communication, and handover be integrated into accounting-policy implementation?
A: Show answer
The implementation process must communicate what has changed, who is affected, when the new rule becomes effective, what actions are required, and where authoritative guidance and procedures are maintained. Training should be tailored to each role, including Finance, Grants, Program, Procurement, Payroll, system administration, and reporting personnel. Users who approve, configure, or validate account data need to understand the relevant responsibilities and escalation routes. The specification includes a training, communication, implementation, and acknowledgment register. That register should document the version communicated, intended audience, training completion, acknowledgment where required, and outstanding issues. Handover must include the current approved policy version, source register, account and dimension dictionaries, mappings, access-control arrangements, known exceptions, release history, and upcoming review obligations. Training records cannot replace testing or approval, but they provide evidence that affected users were informed and prepared to work under the approved process.

Study instruction:
- "Develop an implementation communication plan and training matrix for each affected role, and specify how acknowledgments and unresolved training needs will be recorded."
Key point:
- Adoption requires communication and role-appropriate training alongside technical implementation.
- Handover must preserve current instructions, ownership, and unresolved issues.

45. Q: What acceptance criteria should determine whether the complete policy-generation package is ready for release?
A: Show answer
Acceptance should cover the integrity of the source specification, correctness of the generated documents, business rules, accounting applicability, system configuration, and presentation quality. The supplied specification requires the named output files to exist and contain usable content, but also makes full completion conditional on verified framework applicability, authorized review, resolved references, valid account and mapping structures, successful controls, and reproducible offline generation. It further requires the blank and example documents to match structurally while the example remains clearly fictional. Workflow diagrams must meet the required editable-object specification, and the relevant schema, application, rendering, and visual-inspection tests must be completed where required. Tests whose expected status is blocked or unverified must not be represented as passed. The acceptance record should classify the overall outcome as complete, partial, or failed and preserve evidence supporting each criterion. A package can be technically generated yet remain ineligible for organizational adoption because mandatory accounting, legal, security, or governance decisions are unresolved.

Study instruction:
- "Review the acceptance criteria and assign a supported status to each item, identifying objective evidence for every claimed pass or outstanding condition."

46. Q: How should unresolved framework decisions and missing approvals be represented in policy-generated documents?
A: Show answer
Documents should visibly distinguish approved operative rules, draft controls, fictional assumptions, verification placeholders, and unresolved implementation requirements. A field such as the applicable framework should not be populated with a guessed answer merely to make a document appear complete. Likewise, the existence of an example account mapping, fictional approval, or test result must not be treated as evidence that the actual entity has authorized it. The specification proposes explicit framework-hold and policy-approval gates so that affected rules remain unavailable for operative use until the source, applicability, owner, approval, and effective period are established. Document status and release metadata must accurately reflect the state of the underlying decisions and supporting evidence. A reviewer should be able to determine which requirements have been verified, which remain conditional, and who is responsible for closing each issue. This prevents draft policy from becoming operational through ambiguity, copying, or uncontrolled distribution.

Study instruction:
- "Review a generated policy document and identify every placeholder, draft rule, fictional decision, unverified source, and approval dependency that prevents adoption."
Key point:
- Unknown organizational facts must remain explicit verification requirements.
- A document's professional presentation does not establish its approval or applicability.
