Table 3
Procurement and Supplier Management Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an integrated NGO procurement and supplier management SOP?
A: Show answer
An integrated SOP establishes a controlled, fair, risk-proportionate, and auditable process from identifying a genuine business need through supplier selection, contract execution, delivery, acceptance, and handoff of supporting evidence to Accounts Payable (AP). It defines responsibilities, approval requirements, evidence standards, decision gates, and procedures for handling exceptions. The supplied document is explicitly a fictional demonstration and is not an approved operational policy. Its sample organization, suppliers, transactions, amounts, and outcomes must not be treated as real. Before adoption, the organization must verify its legal status, procurement regime, donor conditions, internal policy, delegated authority, and system configuration.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the purpose of the SOP and distinguish illustrative procedures from controls formally approved for the organization."
Key point:
- Procurement must be traceable from the original need to the completed transaction and archived evidence.
- The supplied SOP is a design reference, not proof of authorization or actual supplier clearance.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement principles and process guidance
2. Q: Which policies and requirements should govern an NGO procurement decision?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying applicable legislation, the organization's approved procurement policy, delegated authority, executed donor or grant agreements, procurement plans, contract conditions, and relevant institutional requirements. Confirm which requirements apply to the particular entity, funding source, transaction, and procurement route. Resolve conflicts through authorized Legal, Compliance, donor, or policy-owner review rather than assuming that a single hierarchy automatically determines every case. In Indonesia, the applicable scope of government procurement regulations must be assessed rather than assumed for every private NGO. Record each source, its version, effective date, applicability decision, and responsible reviewer.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an applicable-source register and identify which rules govern a real procurement case."
Key point:
- The existence of a regulation does not establish that every provision applies to the organization.
- Unresolved legal or donor requirements must block the affected decision until properly assessed.
Source links:
- LKPP — Peraturan Presiden Nomor 46 Tahun 2025
3. Q: Which fundamental principles should guide procurement throughout the lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Procurement should pursue best value for money, fairness, integrity, transparency, effective competition, accountability, and the organization's legitimate needs. These principles require more than collecting quotations: requirements must be defined appropriately, suppliers must receive equitable treatment, evaluation decisions must follow disclosed criteria, and approvals must be supported by evidence. Value for money may involve technical quality, whole-life cost, delivery risk, warranty, implementation requirements, and contractual risk rather than price alone. The organization should translate its applicable principles into concrete rules, review procedures, and documented decisions instead of adopting generic principles without operational controls.

Study instruction:
- "For one purchasing scenario, identify the evidence demonstrating fairness, competition, integrity, and value for money."
Key point:
- Value for money and procedural fairness must be demonstrated, not merely asserted.
- Apply principles through the organization's governing requirements and authorized procurement method.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — value for money, fairness, integrity, and transparency
4. Q: How should the scope of the procurement SOP be defined?
A: Show answer
The scope should cover procurement activities from requisition and method selection through supplier due diligence, solicitation, evaluation, award, contract or purchase-order issuance, receipt, acceptance, supplier performance, and procurement-file closure. It should also define interfaces with budget management, grants, Legal, supplier master-data administration, inventory and asset management, AP, tax compliance, records management, incident response, and IT systems. These interfaces identify where responsibility moves to another approved procedure. For example, procurement can verify that an AP evidence package is complete without acquiring authority to approve the invoice or release payment. Document exclusions and escalation routes explicitly.

Study instruction:
- "Map each procurement stage to its owner, incoming evidence, output, and downstream procedure."
Key point:
- Every handoff must identify the receiving process and decision authority.
- A procurement SOP must not silently absorb responsibilities assigned to Finance, Legal, or other control owners.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement lifecycle
5. Q: Which roles should participate in the procurement process, and what should each role do?
A: Show answer
The operating model should distinguish the requester, budget holder, procurement officer, supplier due-diligence reviewer, conflict reviewer, evaluator, independent reviewer, award approver, Legal or donor specialist, authorized signatory, receiver, technical inspector, AP handoff preparer, records custodian, and independent assurance function. Each role must have defined permissions, prohibited actions, escalation responsibilities, and evidence requirements. For example, the evaluator assesses offers against approved criteria, while the award approver confirms that the recommendation is authorized. Internal Audit should assess control effectiveness independently rather than operate the transactions it later audits. One person may hold multiple roles only where the approved control design permits this and risks are properly mitigated.

Study instruction:
- "Create a responsibility matrix showing who prepares, checks, approves, executes, and independently reviews each major procurement decision."
Key point:
- Responsibilities must be defined by decision rights, not job titles alone.
- Every protected action requires an appropriately authorized person and supporting evidence.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement responsibilities
6. Q: How should segregation of duties be implemented when staffing is limited?
A: Show answer
Identify incompatible combinations, particularly requester and sole approver, evaluator and unresolved conflict reviewer, system implementer and self-approver of a material change, or receiver and required independent inspector. Establish separate review and approval steps wherever practical. If staffing limitations prevent complete separation, document a compensating control such as independent review of evidence, restricted access, post-change testing, or additional supervisory approval. Record who performed the control, what was inspected, the result, and how exceptions were resolved. A control that exists only on paper is insufficient; the organization must verify that it operates in actual transactions.

Study instruction:
- "Test a sample transaction against the segregation-of-duties matrix and document incompatible roles and compensating controls."
Key point:
- A requester must not be the sole approver of their own purchase or exception.
- Compensating controls require documented evidence and an appropriately independent reviewer.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — internal procurement controls
7. Q: How should the total estimated procurement value be calculated?
A: Show answer
Determine the full anticipated commitment using the approved valuation and aggregation method. Depending on applicable rules, this may include related lots, repeat orders, options, renewals, extensions, freight, taxes, and other components of the anticipated purchase. Record the currency, valuation assumptions, aggregation basis, related purchases, and supporting estimate. Do not divide a foreseeable requirement into smaller purchases to avoid a competition method, approval limit, or other control. If the expected total changes materially, recalculate the value and reassess the method, authority, funding, and any required approvals before proceeding.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate one procurement estimate including related lots, options, renewals, and relevant additional costs."
Key point:
- Method selection should use total anticipated value, not an artificially reduced individual order.
- The approved aggregation and valuation rules must be documented and applied consistently.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement planning and methods
8. Q: How should the procurement method be selected for a particular purchase?
A: Show answer
Apply the currently approved procurement-method matrix to the verified aggregate value, purchase category, market conditions, risk, urgency, funding restrictions, and applicable competition requirements. The procedure should specify the evidence and authorization required for quotations, formal tenders, requests for proposals, restricted competition, emergencies, or sole-source routes where permitted. Procurement staff must not invent thresholds or select a method solely because it is administratively convenient. Record the chosen route, rationale, source of the governing parameter, alternatives considered, required approval, and any exception. If required parameters are missing or outdated, hold the decision and refer it to the policy owner.

Study instruction:
- "Classify three hypothetical purchases under an approved method matrix and explain the evidence supporting each decision."
Key point:
- Thresholds and permissible methods must come from verified applicable rules.
- A method decision must be reproducible and independently reviewable.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — solicitation methods
9. Q: What information and evidence should be available before a purchase requisition is approved?
A: Show answer
The requisition should explain the genuine need, expected deliverables, quantity or duration, specification version where applicable, estimated value, currency, funding source, project or donor code, budget evidence, procurement-plan reference, requested delivery date, and requester. It should also identify related orders, relevant risk factors, and required specialist reviews. The budget holder verifies funding and commitment availability but does not thereby authorize the supplier award or payment. Missing information should be returned for correction or formally assessed under an approved exception process. Preserve the approved requisition and its version as the baseline for subsequent procurement decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Review a completed requisition and trace its need, estimate, funding, approval, and specification to supporting records."
Key point:
- A genuine requirement and documented funding precede sourcing commitments.
- Budget availability is not, by itself, authority to select or contract with a supplier.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement planning
10. Q: How can technical specifications be designed without unfairly favouring one supplier?
A: Show answer
Define the operational need, required performance, minimum technical standards, quantities, delivery conditions, compatibility requirements, and acceptance criteria before soliciting offers. Avoid unnecessarily restrictive brand references, qualifications, or requirements that benefit a particular supplier without a documented and authorized justification. Where a brand or existing system reference is genuinely necessary, explain the reason and evaluate whether an equivalent solution is permissible under the applicable rules. Have an appropriate technical reviewer challenge requirements that could unnecessarily restrict competition. Freeze the approved specification version before evaluating offers, and control any subsequent change through an authorized procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Review a sample specification for unnecessary supplier-specific requirements and document justified amendments before solicitation."
Key point:
- Specifications should describe the legitimate requirement rather than predetermine the winner.
- Changing requirements during evaluation requires controlled review and may require restarting the process.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — competition and solicitation
11. Q: What should supplier onboarding and due diligence establish?
A: Show answer
Supplier onboarding should establish the supplier's identity, relevant legal and registration status, capability, ownership information where required, contact details, applicable tax information, and other checks determined by risk and context. Additional checks may address integrity, sanctions or exclusions, safeguarding, security, privacy, technical capacity, licensing, references, and donor-specific requirements. Each result should identify the supplier, check performed, source, date, reviewer, evidence reference, finding, scope, conditions, and next review trigger. Due diligence should support an authorized, documented decision rather than rely on informal assurance or incomplete onboarding records.

Study instruction:
- "Build a supplier onboarding checklist and distinguish mandatory evidence from risk-dependent additional checks."
Key point:
- Due diligence must be proportionate to risk and applicable requirements.
- A supplier record is incomplete until required checks and the authorized disposition are evidenced.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — Policies and Regulations
12. Q: How should supplier risk tiers influence due diligence?
A: Show answer
Use approved risk criteria to classify suppliers into defined tiers and assign the necessary checks and refresh intervals. A lower-risk supplier may require proportionate identity, capability, and relevant tax or contract checks. Higher-risk suppliers may require additional ownership verification, references, licensing, conflict checks, safeguarding or security assessments, and other applicable specialist reviews. The actual criteria and refresh intervals must be approved and tailored to the organization. Record the basis for the tier, checks required, outstanding conditions, reviewer, decision authority, and reassessment triggers. Risk classification should guide the scope of due diligence without bypassing mandatory requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Classify three hypothetical suppliers and demonstrate how the approved risk criteria change their evidence requirements."
Key point:
- Risk tiers determine proportional additional scrutiny, not automatic approval or rejection.
- Required legal, donor, and policy checks remain mandatory regardless of a general risk rating.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — supplier policies and requirements
13. Q: How should supplier identity, ownership, and duplicate records be controlled?
A: Show answer
Establish a unique supplier identifier and verify the legal name and other relevant identity attributes against reliable evidence. Where required, assess beneficial ownership, registration, licensing, and authorized representatives. Compare new onboarding submissions with existing supplier records to identify possible duplicates, alternate spellings, shared addresses, or related entities. Similarity is an investigation signal, not automatically proof of fraud or common ownership. Resolve discrepancies through an authorized review and preserve the evidence, decision, and master-data changes. Restrict sensitive ownership and identity information to users who need access for legitimate procurement and compliance purposes.

Study instruction:
- "Review sample supplier records with similar names or identifiers and document how duplicate candidates are investigated."
Key point:
- Supplier identity must be verified before reliance on the record.
- Duplicate indicators require investigation and documented disposition, not silent deletion or automatic rejection.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — supplier registration and policies
14. Q: How should supplier bank-detail changes be verified?
A: Show answer
Treat changes to supplier bank instructions as controlled master-data events separate from ordinary procurement approval. Verify the request through an independently established contact channel or another authorized verification method. Confirm the supplier identity, change request, authorized requester, supporting evidence, independent approval, system update, and post-change review. The person entering the change should not independently authorize their own change or payment instruction. If the request is unusual, the contact details have changed unexpectedly, or the evidence conflicts, place the change on hold and escalate it under the organization's fraud-prevention procedure. Never rely solely on an email requesting urgent payment to a new account.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a bank-detail change request and identify the independent verification and approval evidence required before activation."
Key point:
- Bank-detail changes require independent verification and traceable authorization.
- An unresolved supplier-master discrepancy must block affected transactions.

15. Q: What should happen when supplier screening identifies a possible match?
A: Show answer
Treat a possible screening match as an unresolved signal that requires authorized identity and applicability review. Verify the identifiers used, source, search date, matching information, and applicable restriction before reaching a conclusion. A possible match is not, by itself, proof of identity, misconduct, or legal exclusion. Assign an authorized reviewer and restrict progression or access where the applicable policy requires it. Record whether the case is pending, cleared, cleared with conditions, held, or rejected, along with the supporting rationale and authorized decision. Escalate ambiguous or material findings to the designated Compliance or Legal function.

Study instruction:
- "Use a hypothetical screening match to distinguish a preliminary alert from a verified finding and an authorized disposition."
Key point:
- Possible matches require proportionate verification rather than automatic clearance or automatic rejection.
- Supplier eligibility decisions must be supported by authorized evidence and applicable rules.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — Policies and Regulations
16. Q: When should supplier due diligence be refreshed?
A: Show answer
Refresh due diligence according to the approved risk-based schedule and when material events occur. Potential triggers include contract renewal, significant changes in ownership or legal status, adverse information, updated screening requirements, licensing changes, new risk exposure, or a material change in the proposed purchase. Reassess which checks apply to the current supplier, transaction, location, funding source, and contract. Preserve prior results and dates so reviewers can understand the evolution of the decision. If a mandatory check has expired or a material issue is unresolved, hold affected activity until an authorized disposition permits resumption.

Study instruction:
- "Design a supplier refresh schedule with event-based triggers and test how an expired check affects a pending purchase."
Key point:
- Supplier clearance is time- and scope-dependent, not a permanent guarantee.
- Expired or materially changed information must be reassessed under approved requirements.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — supplier requirements
17. Q: How should conflicts of interest be disclosed and resolved?
A: Show answer
Require relevant participants to disclose actual, potential, or apparent conflicts before accessing restricted offers or participating in evaluations, awards, contract variations, and supplier decisions. An independent reviewer should assess the declaration and determine appropriate mitigation, such as recusal, restricted access, replacement of an evaluator, or additional independent review. Disclosure alone does not constitute clearance. The person whose conduct or interests are being assessed must not independently approve their own mitigation. Record the declaration, assessment, decision, restrictions, reviewer, and conditions for resumption. New information should trigger reassessment where it could affect the fairness or validity of earlier decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a scenario involving a staff member's personal relationship with a bidder and record the authorized resolution."
Key point:
- Conflicts must be identified, assessed independently, and controlled before restricted participation.
- Mitigation must address access and influence, not only the final vote.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — supplier conflicts and ethical conduct
18. Q: How should gifts, hospitality, and other supplier benefits be handled?
A: Show answer
Establish an approved gift and hospitality policy that defines prohibited conduct, disclosure requirements, thresholds or restrictions where appropriate, reporting channels, independent review, and disciplinary or contractual consequences. Procurement personnel should not invent monetary limits or rely on informal customary practices. Assess the circumstances, timing, value, source, purpose, and relationship to pending decisions. A suspected attempt to influence a supplier decision should be documented and referred to the authorized integrity or compliance process. Requirements applicable to UN suppliers, including the UN's stated position on gifts and hospitality, should not automatically be represented as the policy of every NGO; the organization's own applicable rules must be verified.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate hypothetical supplier invitations before a tender award and identify the declaration, review, and participation restrictions required."
Key point:
- Supplier benefits must not compromise actual or perceived independence.
- Gift parameters and escalation procedures must be formally approved.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — Ethical Behavior of Suppliers and Contractors
19. Q: How should suspected fraud, bribery, collusion, or retaliation be escalated?
A: Show answer
Establish a protected reporting route for integrity concerns and restrict case information to authorized personnel. Record the concern, date, affected case, evidence location, immediate control risks, referral, and authorized next steps without presenting an allegation as a proven finding. Procurement staff should not conduct investigations outside their authority or expose complainants and whistleblowers unnecessarily. The designated investigation, Compliance, Legal, safeguarding, or other competent function determines the appropriate response. Preserve evidence, prevent unauthorized alteration, and document any hold, recusal, suspension, or resumption decision. Applicable reporting duties and legal requirements must be assessed for the actual situation.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a suspected bid-rigging or bribery concern and map its confidential referral and evidence-preservation path."
Key point:
- Concerns must reach the designated protected process without prejudging the outcome.
- Confidentiality and evidence integrity must be maintained during escalation.
Source links:
- Undang-Undang Nomor 31 Tahun 1999 tentang Pemberantasan Tindak Pidana Korupsi — applicability and current text require legal review
20. Q: How should confidential bids and supplier personal data be protected?
A: Show answer
Classify supplier records, bid documents, personal data, conflicts, screening results, complaints, and bank details according to sensitivity. Apply role-based access, secure storage and transfer, controlled disclosure, and access logging. Share bid information only with authorized participants and preserve confidentiality until disclosure is permitted by applicable rules. Personal data processing should have an identified purpose and appropriate legal basis, access controls, retention arrangements, and handling procedures for incidents and data-subject requests where applicable. Assess cross-border transfers and any relevant contractual or legal restrictions. Do not place credentials, passwords, or unnecessary sensitive information in shared procurement workbooks or general-purpose archives.

Study instruction:
- "Assign access classes to a procurement file and identify which users may access bid, identity, conflict, and bank data."
Key point:
- Access must follow legitimate need and applicable privacy requirements.
- Confidential procurement evidence requires controlled handling and traceable access.
Source links:
- Undang-Undang Nomor 27 Tahun 2022 tentang Pelindungan Data Pribadi
21. Q: What should the solicitation package contain before it is issued to suppliers?
A: Show answer
Prepare the approved specification, scope of work, quantities or deliverables, contract terms, response instructions, deadlines, eligibility conditions, mandatory requirements, evaluation criteria, scoring weights where applicable, pricing structure, submission method, clarification procedure, and award conditions. Confirm that the correct procurement method and approving authority have been used. Record the solicitation ID, case ID, document version, criteria version, approval, issue timestamp, invited suppliers, and closing time. Requirements and criteria should be complete enough for suppliers to prepare comparable offers. Any material change after issue must follow the approved amendment and communication process.

Study instruction:
- "Use a solicitation checklist to test whether a sample package is complete, approved, consistently versioned, and ready for issue."
Key point:
- Suppliers should compete against clear and approved requirements.
- The solicitation and evaluation criteria must have traceable versions.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — solicitation and competition
22. Q: How should clarification questions and procurement communications be handled fairly?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled communication channel and a log of supplier questions, answers, dates, decisions, and document changes. Provide material clarifications to all affected participants on an equivalent basis when required by the solicitation and applicable rules. Do not give one supplier confidential competitor information, undisclosed criteria, or selective access to material information that could influence its offer. Where clarification changes scope or conditions, obtain the required approval and amend the solicitation formally. Preserve evidence of dispatch and receipt and extend the deadline where required by the applicable procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Review a clarification log and identify any unequal disclosure or uncontrolled change in requirements."
Key point:
- Consistent communication protects competition and the credibility of the process.
- Material clarifications and amendments must be approved, versioned, and communicated appropriately.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — fairness and solicitation
23. Q: How should supplier offers be received and their custody documented?
A: Show answer
Record the offer identifier, supplier, solicitation, receipt date and time with the appropriate time zone, receipt channel, receiving person, file version, and custody or access history. Apply the prescribed deadline and opening procedure consistently. Restrict access until the authorized stage, and retain evidence showing that submissions were not improperly altered, replaced, or selectively disclosed. A late submission, missing record, conflicting timestamp, or suspected alteration should be handled under an approved rule and documented as an exception. Do not adjust timestamps or silently substitute a revised offer to make a submission appear compliant.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical offer from receipt through controlled access, opening, evaluation, and archival evidence."
Key point:
- Offer timing, version, and custody are essential audit evidence.
- Exceptions must follow predefined rules rather than informal discretion.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — solicitation and evaluation controls
24. Q: Why should mandatory eligibility and pass/fail requirements be checked before weighted scoring?
A: Show answer
Where the approved solicitation establishes mandatory eligibility conditions, assess those conditions before applying weighted technical or commercial scoring. Define the evidence needed for each condition and record the result and rationale for each supplier. A strong score should not compensate for a mandatory failure where the solicitation requires a pass/fail gate. Apply the same criteria consistently and use an authorized clarification process if the rules permit clarification. Where no supplier is eligible, do not manufacture a price benchmark or winner from ineligible offers; apply the approved cancellation, hold, or re-solicitation procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Review a simulated evaluation containing one mandatory eligibility failure and determine whether weighted scoring may proceed."
Key point:
- Eligibility gates take precedence where the approved process makes them mandatory.
- An empty eligible set must trigger a controlled decision, not an artificial award.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — evaluation principles
25. Q: How should value for money be evaluated instead of selecting the lowest price automatically?
A: Show answer
Apply the evaluation method disclosed and authorized before offer opening. Depending on the purchase, value for money may involve technical quality, evaluated price, whole-life cost, delivery feasibility, implementation risk, warranty, support, compliance, and contractual exposure. Ensure that all evaluators assess the same approved criteria and retain individual scores, consensus rationale, supporting evidence, and conflict declarations. The lowest price may be the preferred result when the method and requirements support it, but it must not override a mandatory failure or an approved best-value methodology. Document why the recommended offer satisfies the organization's need and offers the best result under the applicable rules.

Study instruction:
- "Compare two hypothetical suppliers with different technical scores, total costs, and delivery risks using the disclosed evaluation criteria."
Key point:
- Best value depends on the authorized evaluation method and documented evidence.
- Evaluators must not introduce undisclosed preferences after offers are received.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — best value for money
26. Q: How should weighted scores and evaluated prices be calculated and verified?
A: Show answer
Document the approved scoring formula, criteria weights, pricing basis, currency conversion method and source, rounding precision, and formula version. Recalculate scores independently and test for missing, malformed, zero, negative, or non-finite price inputs, unverified conversion rates, and inconsistent units or totals. Confirm that only eligible offers are included in price comparisons when required by the methodology. Retain source inputs, calculation workpapers, formula settings, reviewer results, and explanations of discrepancies. Any change to weights or calculation rules after offer access must follow the applicable authorized process; an evaluator must not silently alter the methodology to obtain a preferred result.

Study instruction:
- "Independently recompute a sample evaluation and test how the process handles invalid prices and currency conversions."
Key point:
- Calculations must be reproducible from approved inputs and formulas.
- A spreadsheet result is not reliable until its logic and source data have been validated.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — evaluation and award
27. Q: How should tied scores or an absence of eligible offers be handled?
A: Show answer
Define tie-break procedures in the approved solicitation or governing procurement policy before offers are opened. Apply the stated criteria consistently and document the calculation, decision, and approval. Do not invent a new tie-breaker after seeing which suppliers benefit. If no supplier passes mandatory eligibility, follow the authorized hold, cancellation, or re-solicitation route. Record the reasons, treatment of submitted offers, approval, communication to relevant parties, and any conditions for restarting. An inability to identify an eligible supplier is a procurement decision requiring formal handling, not a justification for bypassing the eligibility criteria.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate tied scores and a case where all offers fail eligibility, then apply the pre-approved decision rules."
Key point:
- Tie-break rules must be established before the outcome is known.
- No eligible offer means no automatic award or valid price ranking under a mandatory eligibility gate.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — evaluation decisions
28. Q: What should the award recommendation contain before approval?
A: Show answer
The recommendation should identify the procurement case, approved method, eligible suppliers, evaluation criteria and version, results, evaluated prices, material risks, conflicts and their dispositions, funding confirmation, specialist reviews, and the proposed supplier, value, scope, and currency. Explain significant deviations, exceptions, conditions, and the basis for the recommended outcome. Attach the evaluation record and independent recalculation where required. The award approver must determine whether the recommendation meets the applicable requirements and falls within valid delegated authority. Record approval, rejection, or hold with the rationale, date, reviewer, supporting version, and required next actions.

Study instruction:
- "Review an award paper and verify that its recommendation is fully supported by the evaluation and required approvals."
Key point:
- Award approval requires evidence of eligibility, evaluation, funding, and authority.
- A recommendation is not itself an authorized award.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — award decisions
29. Q: How should delegated authority and approval limits be verified?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled delegation matrix specifying approval roles, limits, procurement categories, permitted exceptions, signature powers, conditions, effective dates, and any required committee involvement. Confirm that the approving person held the relevant authority at the time of the decision and that the procurement is within the approved scope and limit. Check for expired delegations, restricted authority, missing approvals, and conflicts with other required controls. Preserve the delegation version and approval evidence used for the decision. If authority cannot be verified, hold the procurement rather than relying on seniority, job title, or informal verbal instruction.

Study instruction:
- "Select an award and verify the approver's authority, scope, limit, effective date, and required independent approvals."
Key point:
- Approval authority must be current, documented, and applicable to the transaction.
- An unauthorized approval does not become valid merely because the person holds a senior position.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement governance and authority
30. Q: What is the difference between award approval and contract or purchase-order execution?
A: Show answer
Award approval is the authorized decision to select a supplier and approve the relevant value and scope. Contract or purchase-order execution is a separate legal or operational act that must be completed by an authorized signatory or issuing role. Before execution, confirm that all required legal and donor conditions are cleared, the instrument matches the approved award, and the signatory has valid authority. Do not start from the assumption that an approved recommendation creates a binding contract. Record the executed version, signatories, timestamps, effective term, and any outstanding conditions permitted under the governing rules.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one procurement from recommendation approval to signed contract and identify the evidence required at each gate."
Key point:
- Award approval does not automatically confer signature authority or create a valid executed contract.
- Contract issuance must be independently checked against the approved award and legal requirements.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — contract award and administration
31. Q: What should be checked when preparing a contract or purchase order?
A: Show answer
Match the instrument to the approved supplier, award reference, scope and specification version, quantities, deliverables, total value, currency, delivery location and milestones, acceptance criteria, warranty or service levels, tax-treatment status where relevant, confidentiality and security requirements, and contract term. Verify all necessary legal and donor conditions and the signatory's current authority. Preserve the final version, approval references, execution timestamps, and any required audit evidence. If a material discrepancy exists between the draft instrument and the award, stop issuance and obtain an authorized correction or reapproval as required.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a contract-to-award comparison and record all mismatches before a purchase order is issued."
Key point:
- The contract or purchase order must accurately reflect the authorized decision.
- Material discrepancies cannot be hidden through informal edits or backdated documents.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — contract administration
32. Q: How should contract variations, extensions, and renewals be controlled?
A: Show answer
Require a documented change request explaining the need, original terms, proposed revision, cost and schedule impact, cumulative value, funding availability, supplier implications, and relevant contract or donor conditions. Recalculate the aggregate commitment using the approved method and reassess whether the original procurement route, competition rules, and approval authority remain appropriate. Obtain required approvals before implementing the change, and preserve the old and revised versions. Renewals should not be automatic merely because a contract contains an extension option; check the option terms, performance, funding, eligibility, and applicable approvals. Emergency changes also require evidence and subsequent review where prescribed.

Study instruction:
- "Review a contract extension with a significant price increase and identify the required value, funding, authority, and competition checks."
Key point:
- Variations and renewals can change the cumulative procurement exposure.
- Required approval must precede commitment or implementation unless a formally authorized exception applies.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — contract amendments and administration
33. Q: How should emergency or sole-source procurement be handled?
A: Show answer
Use an emergency or sole-source route only where the applicable policy permits it and the documented facts satisfy its requirements. The record should explain the urgency or lack of viable alternatives, why ordinary competition is unsuitable, the supplier and value rationale, market considerations where feasible, the funding position, the approving authority, and any required donor or Legal review. Urgency caused by avoidable poor planning should not be used automatically to justify bypassing controls. Record the scope and duration of the exception and complete any mandated post-event review. If the grounds or authority are not established, hold the exception and use the approved escalation route.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate an urgent purchase and distinguish a supported emergency exception from an avoidable planning failure."
Key point:
- Exceptions require explicit grounds, evidence, and valid authority.
- An exceptional route does not eliminate contract, supplier, funding, or integrity controls.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement methods and exceptions
34. Q: When should Legal, donor, tax, technical, security, or safeguarding specialists review a purchase?
A: Show answer
Define specialist-review triggers based on the actual transaction and governing requirements. Triggers may include unusual contractual terms, donor prior approval, regulated goods or services, sensitive personal data, security exposure, safeguarding risks, complex tax treatment, specialized technical requirements, or material supplier-integrity concerns. Each review should identify the authorized reviewer, scope, evidence, date, decision, conditions, and any restriction on next steps. A pending mandatory review must block the affected action. Procurement should not infer approval from a specialist's silence or extend a reviewer's authority beyond their remit. Conditional approval is valid only within its recorded boundaries.

Study instruction:
- "Create a specialist-review matrix and identify which decisions remain blocked until each required clearance is complete."
Key point:
- Specialist approvals depend on defined authority and documented evidence.
- A conditional clearance must retain its conditions and permitted-action boundaries.
Source links:
- LKPP — Peraturan Presiden Nomor 46 Tahun 2025, subject to applicability review
35. Q: How should receipt and inspection of goods or services be documented?
A: Show answer
The receiver should record what was actually delivered, including the date, location, quantity or milestone, supplier reference, condition, and supporting evidence. An authorized inspector or service owner then compares the delivery with the executed contract or purchase order and its acceptance criteria. Record accepted quantities separately from items not yet inspected or accepted. The receiving record must not certify delivery that did not occur, and material exceptions should trigger the designated discrepancy process. Required inventory, asset-custody, tagging, or service-management records should be completed under the relevant downstream procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a sample delivery record with the purchase order and identify missing evidence or acceptance discrepancies."
Key point:
- A delivery record documents physical or service receipt; it does not automatically prove acceptance.
- Acceptance requires evidence against the agreed requirements and authorized responsibility.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — contract administration and performance
36. Q: How should partial delivery, defects, and discrepancies be handled?
A: Show answer
Record each affected line or milestone, the contract requirement, quantity delivered, quantity accepted, defect description, evidence, impact, responsible owner, and required corrective action. Where authorized and permitted, accept compliant portions separately while leaving defective, incomplete, or unverified portions unresolved. Do not label partial delivery as full acceptance or silently amend the purchase order to hide a mismatch. Assess whether the discrepancy affects payment eligibility, contract remedies, delivery milestones, project timing, or future supplier performance. Track the issue until an authorized disposition and independent verification have been completed where required.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a shipment with a shortage and damaged goods and prepare the discrepancy record and follow-up actions."
Key point:
- Accepted quantities must be supported by inspection evidence.
- Material discrepancies must be resolved or formally held before the affected AP handoff proceeds.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — supplier and contract performance
37. Q: What evidence is required before a procurement case can be handed over to Accounts Payable?
A: Show answer
The AP handoff package should contain the approved award, executed or properly issued contract or purchase order, current applicable supplier status, invoice reference, coding and funding information, and required receipt and acceptance evidence. Confirm that material discrepancies and blocking conditions have been resolved or formally treated under a permitted rule. The handoff record should identify the case, supplier, package version, preparer, independent checker, date, and evidence locations. AP then applies its own invoice-validation and payment-authorization procedures. A complete procurement package is not approval to pay and must never be treated as bank-release authority under the procurement SOP.

Study instruction:
- "Assemble an AP handoff package and test it against the checklist and downstream responsibility boundaries."
Key point:
- Evidence completeness is a prerequisite to handoff where required.
- AP invoice approval, payment authorization, and disbursement remain separate controlled activities.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement completion and contract administration
38. Q: How should supplier performance be monitored during and after contract delivery?
A: Show answer
Monitor performance against defined contractual and operational measures such as delivery timeliness, quality, completeness, responsiveness, warranty support, service levels, compliance, and resolution of defects. Record performance evidence, reporting period, responsible contract owner, supplier response, assessment, corrective actions, deadlines, and review outcome. Separate facts from opinions and retain supporting records for material negative findings. Use the approved assessment criteria consistently across comparable suppliers and consider risk and context when interpreting results. Supplier performance information may inform future sourcing, renewals, remediation, or escalation only through the authorized decision process.

Study instruction:
- "Build a supplier performance scorecard and demonstrate how documented delivery failures lead to corrective actions."
Key point:
- Supplier ratings need defined criteria and evidence.
- Performance monitoring supports future decisions but does not automatically authorize exclusion or suspension.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — contract and supplier performance
39. Q: How should supplier renewal, suspension, or rejection decisions be controlled?
A: Show answer
Assess contract performance, current due diligence, unresolved defects, integrity concerns, capability, funding, market conditions, renewal options, and applicable policy and contract requirements. Distinguish a routine performance issue from a material compliance or integrity concern requiring specialist referral. Record the evidence, decision grounds, authorized reviewer, affected scope, effective date, notification requirements, and any restrictions on future procurement. Suspension or rejection must not be imposed solely through an informal spreadsheet status or an unverified allegation. For renewals, confirm that the continuation is contractually and procedurally permitted and appropriately approved before commitment.

Study instruction:
- "Review a proposed supplier renewal with unresolved delivery issues and identify the evidence and authority needed for the decision."
Key point:
- Renewal and suspension require documented, authorized decisions.
- Supplier status must reflect the current scope, evidence, and applicable restrictions.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — Policies and Regulations
40. Q: How should procurement exceptions and complaints be managed?
A: Show answer
Register each exception or complaint with a unique identifier, date, procurement case, issue type, source, severity, impact, responsible owner, due date, current status, and evidence. Investigate the issue within the assigned authority, preserve relevant records, and refer protected allegations or legal matters to the designated function. Define corrective action, reviewer, completion criteria, and independent retesting where necessary. An item should move to closed status only when the required evidence and closure authority are present. If new facts emerge, reopen the case and reassess previous decisions that may have been affected.

Study instruction:
- "Create an exception register and demonstrate the evidence required to close and independently verify one issue."
Key point:
- Every exception needs an owner, response deadline, action, and evidence-based disposition.
- Closure is a controlled decision, not simply a change of status.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — ethical conduct and prohibited practices
41. Q: How can an NGO ensure procurement records support an effective audit trail?
A: Show answer
Maintain a procurement file index linking the requisition, specifications, valuation, method decision, funding checks, supplier due diligence, conflict declarations, solicitation, offer custody, evaluation, approvals, executed contract or purchase order, receipt, acceptance, AP handoff, supplier performance, and exception records. Each record should identify the procurement case, document type, version, date, owner, reviewer where required, approval, and controlled storage location. Preserve the evidence supporting important decisions as well as the final outcome. A reviewer should be able to trace both from the final award back to the original requirement and from the requirement forward to delivery and handoff.

Study instruction:
- "Select one completed case and test whether each material decision can be traced to its source evidence and authorization."
Key point:
- A defensible audit trail connects the entire lifecycle, not only the signed contract.
- Decisions, changes, approvals, and exceptions must remain traceable after closure.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — documentation and accountability
42. Q: How should procurement retention, archival, and secure disposal be controlled?
A: Show answer
Establish a retention schedule using verified applicable legal, donor, contractual, audit, privacy, and organizational requirements. Define the record classes, retention trigger, applicable period, storage location, access permissions, legal-hold procedure, backup arrangements, and authorized disposal method. Do not invent retention periods or delete records subject to an investigation, audit, complaint, or legal hold. Keep final evidence distinct from drafts and preserve required historical versions. Test backup restoration and confirm that archived documents remain readable and retrievable for authorized review. Disposal should be documented when required by the approved schedule and applicable rules.

Study instruction:
- "Create a retention and archive matrix for bids, contracts, supplier personal data, conflicts, and complaints."
Key point:
- Retention periods depend on verified requirements rather than convenience.
- Legal holds and restricted records must be respected throughout the archive lifecycle.
Source links:
- Undang-Undang Nomor 27 Tahun 2022 tentang Pelindungan Data Pribadi
43. Q: What conditions should be met before a procurement file is formally closed?
A: Show answer
Confirm that the file index is complete, required contract and delivery records are present, acceptance and AP handoff or another authorized disposition are evidenced, open complaints and exceptions have been resolved or formally retained with approval, and the correct retention classification is recorded. Check that all referenced documents are accessible and that final versions can be identified. Reconcile any unresolved quantities, milestone status, or outstanding deliverables to the authorized disposition. File closure must not conceal incomplete work or imply that a separate AP payment process has finished. Record the closure decision, responsible owner, reviewer where necessary, date, and outstanding obligations that continue beyond procurement closure.

Study instruction:
- "Test a proposed file closure against the approved checklist and identify all unresolved blockers."
Key point:
- Closure requires evidence completeness and an authorized disposition of open issues.
- Closing a procurement file does not automatically close downstream financial obligations.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement records and contract administration
44. Q: How should procurement workflow statuses and decision gates be designed?
A: Show answer
Use controlled status domains for requisitions, supplier checks, conflicts, solicitation, evaluation, awards, contracts, delivery, specialist reviews, exceptions, AP handoff, and file closure. Define permitted transitions, responsible roles, prerequisites, mandatory evidence, blocked outcomes, and correction routes. For example, an award must not advance to contract execution if a required approval remains pending. A case with missing receipt or unresolved material discrepancies must not be considered ready for AP handoff where those items are mandatory. A conditional approval should preserve its conditions and permitted actions. Transitions after correction or reopening should require revalidation and reapproval where applicable.

Study instruction:
- "Build a status-transition table and test whether a case can reach a protected status while a mandatory prerequisite remains incomplete."
Key point:
- Status labels must represent evidenced conditions rather than user assumptions.
- Workflow rules should prevent invalid transitions and preserve a traceable correction path.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement control framework
45. Q: How should procurement-system access and configuration changes be governed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a role-based access register identifying users, privileges, the business justification, approving authority, effective date, and removal date. Restrict supplier-master updates, bank-detail changes, bid access, evaluation permissions, award approval, and privileged configuration changes according to the segregation-of-duties design. Require authorized approval before changes to formulas, procurement thresholds, routing rules, required fields, or status transitions. Test material configuration changes independently before releasing them. Record the change request, old and new settings, test evidence, approver, implementation date, and post-release checks. Access should be removed when no longer required and reviewed periodically.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the actual user-access list against the approved role matrix and test one proposed configuration change."
Key point:
- System access must reflect authorized business responsibilities.
- Configuration and privileged-access changes require independent verification and traceable release evidence.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — Policies and Regulations
46. Q: How should procurement thresholds, evaluation weights, and other configuration parameters be maintained?
A: Show answer
Store each controlled parameter with a unique identifier, description, value or approved source, effective date, expiry or review trigger where applicable, owner, approver, and version. Parameters can include method thresholds, competition requirements, supplier risk criteria, due-diligence refresh intervals, evaluation weights, currency rules, and retention settings. Keep unresolved values explicitly marked as requiring configuration rather than supplying arbitrary defaults. Changes should be supported by the relevant approved policy, contract, donor rule, or legal review. Test the effect of a change on existing and new cases before authorized implementation, and retain historical versions to explain decisions made under earlier settings.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect the parameter register and identify values that are missing, expired, unsupported, or not yet approved."
Key point:
- Critical parameters must have an authoritative basis and a controlled effective date.
- Missing configuration must block affected decisions instead of silently defaulting to a value.
Source links:
- LKPP — Peraturan Presiden Nomor 46 Tahun 2025, subject to applicability review
47. Q: How can data validation and automation improve procurement controls without replacing human judgment?
A: Show answer
Use standardized forms, unique case and supplier IDs, controlled status lists, required-field checks, version references, duplicate detection, date and amount validations, and cross-references between documents. Automated tests can identify missing approvals, unresolved holds, invalid transitions, mismatched values, or incomplete file indexes before a case advances. Power Query, formulas, or controlled workflow tools can assist with data cleansing, reconciliation, and exception reporting. However, a flagged duplicate is not necessarily fraudulent, a possible screening match is not proof, and a completed field does not establish substantive approval. Authorized reviewers must assess material exceptions and document the resolution. Changes to rules and formulas must be tested before use.

Study instruction:
- "Test a procurement tracker with deliberately missing approvals, duplicate suppliers, invalid prices, and unresolved holds."
Key point:
- Automation detects conditions and supports repeatable controls.
- Professional decisions, source verification, and authorized approvals remain necessary.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement processes and controls
48. Q: How should management monitor procurement performance and control effectiveness?
A: Show answer
Define measurable indicators such as cycle time, on-time delivery, incomplete procurement files, exceptions by severity, overdue corrective actions, supplier performance, competition outcomes, contract variations, and the percentage of cases with completed mandatory checks. Each KPI needs a definition, source, calculation method, reporting period, owner, target, and escalation rule. Validate automated metrics against underlying records and distinguish clean cases from held cases, test-only records, and unresolved exceptions. Review trends, identify root causes, and assign actions to the responsible owners. A dashboard should help management identify risk and bottlenecks, not be treated as a substitute for detailed review or evidence of compliance.

Study instruction:
- "Build a procurement dashboard and reconcile each KPI to the underlying case register and supporting evidence."
Key point:
- Performance indicators need stable definitions and verifiable data.
- Exception trends and control failures should lead to documented corrective action.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement performance and accountability
49. Q: How should an NGO test a proposed procurement SOP before formally adopting it?
A: Show answer
Conduct an end-to-end walkthrough using representative cases and verify that the written procedure matches actual responsibilities, evidence, systems, and applicable requirements. Test ordinary purchases, higher-risk suppliers, conditional approvals, delivery discrepancies, material exceptions, and cases where mandatory checks fail. Confirm that forms, statuses, decision rules, source references, and approval requirements operate consistently. Include negative tests to establish that prohibited actions, unsupported approvals, invalid status transitions, missing evidence, and unresolved holds are rejected or blocked as designed. Record each test, expected result, actual result, defect, corrective action, and retest. Adoption requires authorized approval and completion of applicable legal, donor, policy, training, system, and release gates.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a test register with positive and negative cases and demonstrate that each material defect is corrected and independently retested."
Key point:
- A drafted procedure is not an operationally validated control system.
- Formal approval and organizational adoption require separate evidence from technical testing.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement Manual — procurement governance and controls
50. Q: What must be completed before the fictional SOP can be converted into an approved procedure for a real NGO?
A: Show answer
Verify the actual entity and legal status, determine the applicable procurement regime, review current legislation and consolidated regulations, obtain the approved procurement policy and delegation matrix, and load actual donor, grant, and contract requirements. Approve the method and threshold matrix, supplier risk criteria, due-diligence and screening parameters, conflict and gift rules, specialist-review gates, retention schedule, and required system permissions. Confirm interfaces with supplier master data, AP, receiving, inventory and assets, tax, privacy, incident response, and records management. Replace fictional data, test required procedures and system controls, complete staff training, resolve critical defects, and obtain formal approval from the authorized governing body or delegate. Until these steps are completed and evidenced, the SOP must remain a draft and must not be represented as adopted or approved for operational use.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption-readiness checklist identifying each missing legal, policy, donor, authority, system, training, and approval requirement."
Key point:
- Organizational adoption requires verified requirements, configured controls, tested procedures, and authorized sign-off.
- Fictional supplier records, thresholds, approvals, and test results must never be represented as real procurement evidence.
Source links:
- United Nations Procurement — Policies and Regulations
