Table 4
Journal Entry Preparation, Review and Posting Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of a controlled journal-entry preparation, review, and posting process?
A: Show answer
A controlled journal-entry process ensures that every eligible general-ledger transaction is supported by reliable evidence, classified under an applicable accounting framework, calculated correctly, assigned to valid accounts and dimensions, reviewed independently, authorized by an appropriately delegated person, posted to the correct entity and accounting period, reconciled, and archived. The process must distinguish the existence of a business event from the accounting decision made about it. A balanced entry does not automatically demonstrate valid recognition or classification, and an approval does not prove that the entry reached the ledger. The source SOP example describes these controls using a fictional Indonesian foundation; its organization, transactions, account codes, approvals, and posting references are illustrative only. An actual NGO must establish its own accounting framework, chart of accounts, delegations, system procedures, and effective policies before adoption.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one journal from its original business event through preparation, independent review, authorization, posting, reconciliation, and archival."

Key point:
- A journal must be valid, supported, authorized, posted, and reconciled.
- The fictional source document is a reference example, not an approved organizational policy.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
2. Q: What qualifies as an eligible manual general-ledger journal?
A: Show answer
An eligible manual journal is a transaction whose nature, accounting requirements, and system design permit direct general-ledger processing. Eligibility must be determined before the preparer creates journal lines. Some transactions belong in dedicated processes such as accounts payable, payroll, banking, fixed assets, inventory, grants, advances, leases, or tax administration. Those processes can maintain important supporting records, approvals, subsidiary balances, and settlement information that a standalone journal would bypass. Where a source module is authoritative, the preparer should refer the transaction to its owner and retain a linked referral record. A manual journal should not be used merely because the correct source-system transaction is inconvenient or delayed. The organization's documented accounting policy, system configuration, and applicable rules determine which routes are permitted.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample transactions by source module and document why each may or may not be posted through a manual general-ledger journal."

Key point:
- Determine the permitted accounting route before preparing the entry.
- A manual journal must not bypass mandatory source-module controls.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and subsidiary-ledger interfaces
3. Q: Which rules and documents govern the accounting treatment of a journal entry?
A: Show answer
The preparer must identify the applicable legal requirements, accounting framework, effective organizational accounting policies, executed contracts, donor agreements, approved delegations, and system requirements relevant to the transaction. These authorities serve different purposes. Accounting standards govern applicable recognition, measurement, classification, presentation, and disclosure decisions. Donor agreements may establish funding restrictions or reporting conditions, while laws and regulations may impose additional mandatory requirements. Internal policies establish approved operating procedures within the scope permitted by those authorities. An ERP default, historical practice, or account previously used by a colleague is not sufficient evidence that a treatment is correct. If requirements conflict or the applicable framework is uncertain, the journal should be held while an authorized specialist resolves the issue. The resolution should identify the governing authority and the accounting consequence rather than merely stating that management approved the treatment.

Study instruction:
- "Take a sample transaction and list the accounting authority, applicable organizational policy, contractual or donor conditions, and approvals needed before posting."

Key point:
- Accounting treatment must follow the applicable authority and approved policies.
- Unresolved framework or contractual questions require documented review before posting.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
4. Q: How should a journal request be classified and routed before preparation begins?
A: Show answer
The requester and preparer should identify the business event, source record, transaction category, responsible business owner, intended accounting period, and permitted processing route. Typical categories include routine manual journals, recurring entries, accruals, deferrals, foreign-currency adjustments, corrections, reversals, interoffice adjustments, specialist entries, and controlled migration journals. These categories are not interchangeable. For example, a recurring template may be approved for a period, but an individual occurrence still needs current data and validation. A correction must be linked to the original entry and preserve the original evidence. A migration journal may require control totals and dedicated testing instead of routine manual-journal approval. Each route should define required evidence, applicable decision gates, specialist reviews, and the system or team responsible for final processing.

Study instruction:
- "Map five different journal requests to their permitted processing routes, required evidence, specialist reviews, and expected completion states."

Key point:
- Classification determines the applicable controls and processing route.
- Every journal category needs explicit entry and completion requirements.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processing and journal categories
5. Q: Why should requesting, preparing, reviewing, approving, posting, and reconciling be separated?
A: Show answer
Separating responsibilities reduces the risk that an unsupported or incorrect journal passes through the entire process without independent challenge. The requester explains the business purpose and supplies source evidence. The preparer determines the proposed accounting treatment and calculates the journal. The reviewer independently tests evidence, classification, coding, mathematics, period, duplication, and authorization requirements. The approver decides whether the exact journal version may proceed under the current delegation. The posting operator submits only the authorized version, while the reconciler independently confirms the ledger outcome against source records and control totals. A person may hold several responsibilities in a small NGO, but self-approval and other mandatory incompatible duties must remain prohibited. Where complete separation is impracticable, documented compensating controls should be approved and tested. Examples include independent post-posting review, restricted system access, and review by an authorized finance manager who did not prepare the entry.

Study instruction:
- "Create a responsibility matrix for one journal and identify incompatible duties and the compensating controls required for a small finance team."

Key point:
- Independent review and authorization should challenge the preparer's work.
- Staff limitations require documented compensating controls, not informal self-approval.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger roles and workflows
6. Q: What evidence is needed to demonstrate that journal approval is valid?
A: Show answer
The organization should maintain a current delegation register identifying the authorized person or position, legal entity, applicable journal categories, monetary limits, currencies, effective dates, restrictions, and substitute arrangements. Approval evidence should identify the approver, date, decision, journal reference, version or hash, and relevant authority. An approval from a person who has exceeded their limit, acted outside the approved scope, or used an expired delegation is not sufficient. The approval record must relate to the exact version reviewed, including its lines, amounts, entity, period, currency, and dimensions. If those details change materially, the relevant checks and approval must be repeated. A name typed into a spreadsheet does not independently establish that approval occurred through an authorized process. Evidence should be verifiable through the controlled workflow, approved records, or authenticated system audit trail.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect three journal approvals and verify the approver's delegated scope, effective date, journal identity, amount, and decision evidence."

Key point:
- Authority must be current, appropriate, and traceable.
- Approval applies to the exact version that was reviewed.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processing and approval workflows
7. Q: What makes supporting evidence sufficient for a journal entry?
A: Show answer
Evidence must support the particular decision being made, rather than merely exist in the journal folder. Depending on the transaction, it may include an original contract, supplier invoice, service-completion record, payroll report, calculation sheet, approved estimate, funding agreement, source-system record, bank statement, or specialist assessment. The preparer should be able to establish the event's nature, period, amount, counterparty, authorization, and relevant accounting dimensions. Reviewers should examine authenticity, completeness, consistency, dates, version, and the link between the document and the journal line. A screenshot that omits relevant context or a file that cannot be connected to the source transaction may be insufficient. Missing or conflicting evidence should result in a hold or documented escalation, not a fabricated reference. Evidence standards should be defined by journal category and the applicable accounting and organizational requirements.

Study instruction:
- "For an accrual, a reclassification, and a correction, identify what evidence supports the event, amount, period, and authorization in each case."

Key point:
- Evidence must substantiate the specific accounting decision and amount.
- Missing or contradictory support must be resolved or formally escalated.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger documentation and processing
8. Q: What information should a journal request contain before the preparer starts accounting analysis?
A: Show answer
A controlled request should identify the requester, business owner, legal entity, transaction date, proposed accounting period, business purpose, source document references, origin system, journal category, expected amount and currency, required dimensions, urgency, and supporting files. It should also disclose whether similar entries already exist, whether the transaction is part of a recurring schedule, whether specialist review may be needed, and whether any source-module process already owns the event. The preparer should confirm that mandatory fields are complete and that the request describes the underlying event rather than simply demanding a debit to one account and a credit to another. Incomplete requests should be returned or placed on hold with an assigned owner and specific information required. This prevents accounting analysis from being built on unverified assumptions.

Study instruction:
- "Design an intake form and test it against a complete routine journal request and an incomplete request with missing source evidence."

Key point:
- Intake controls establish the facts needed for correct classification.
- Missing information should generate a visible exception before preparation progresses.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processes and journal documentation
9. Q: How should the applicable accounting framework and chart of accounts be established before posting?
A: Show answer
The organization must determine the framework and approved accounting policies applicable to its financial statements before selecting accounts or accounting treatments. It should then use an authorized chart of accounts with descriptions, account types, valid posting dates, permitted journal sources, required dimensions, and restrictions. Account mappings should be documented and maintained under controlled change procedures. A code found in a fictional SOP or an example ledger must not be copied into a real accounting system without validation. The preparer should check whether the intended account is active, appropriate for the transaction, available to the relevant entity, and permitted for manual posting. Where a reconciliation account is controlled by a subsidiary ledger, direct manual postings may be prohibited. Uncertain accounts require review by the chart-of-accounts owner and, when necessary, the accounting-policy owner.

Study instruction:
- "Select several accounts from the organization's approved chart and verify their descriptions, permitted journal sources, required dimensions, and effective dates."

Key point:
- Use approved, effective-dated account mappings.
- An example account code is not authority for an actual posting.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger accounts and subsidiary-ledger controls
10. Q: Why must entity, fund, project, donor, office, and cost-centre dimensions be validated separately from the GL account?
A: Show answer
An appropriate account code does not guarantee that a transaction is assigned to the correct reporting dimension. A salary expense, for example, may be posted to the correct expense account but charged to an incorrect project or donor. The resulting general ledger might remain balanced while grant expenditure and project reports become inaccurate. Required dimensions depend on the accounting system, organizational structure, grant conditions, and reporting requirements. The preparer should select dimensions from approved master data and verify consistency with the source transaction, approved budget, funding restriction, and relevant allocation policy. The reviewer should investigate missing, inactive, conflicting, or prohibited combinations. Changes to donor or project coding after approval require impact assessment and renewed authorization where material.

Study instruction:
- "Take a journal involving two projects and verify its entity, office, fund, project, donor, and cost-centre assignments against approved master data."

Key point:
- Account validity and dimension validity are separate controls.
- Incorrect dimensions can misstate donor and project reporting even when debits equal credits.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger classification and reporting dimensions
11. Q: Why is a zero debit-credit imbalance not proof that a journal is correct?
A: Show answer
Double-entry arithmetic requires total debits to equal total credits, but a balanced journal may still contain an invalid account, incorrect period, duplicate transaction, unsupported estimate, wrong project, inappropriate tax treatment, unauthorized posting, or incomplete transaction population. For example, a journal debiting an incorrect project and crediting the same liability account can balance perfectly while misrepresenting project performance. The preparer should validate both arithmetic and accounting substance. The reviewer should separately test recognition, measurement, classification, evidence, period, authorization, source routing, duplication, and required dimensions. Balance checks are essential because they detect numerical imbalance, but they are only one component of the control framework. Unexplained plugs or unsupported rounding adjustments should not be used to force a journal to balance.

Study instruction:
- "Construct a balanced journal with an incorrect project dimension and explain why arithmetic validation alone cannot approve it."

Key point:
- Balanced means arithmetically equal, not necessarily accurate or authorized.
- Substantive and procedural checks must be completed independently of the balance test.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processing and controls
12. Q: How should journal calculations involving allocations, foreign currency, tax, or estimates be controlled?
A: Show answer
Each material calculation should show its inputs, formula or methodology, units, source documents, assumptions, rounding convention, preparer, and independent verification. Foreign-currency calculations should identify the applicable rate source, date, currency direction, conversion basis, and the policy or rule governing the rate. Allocations should disclose the allocation basis, eligible population, method, and supporting data. Tax-sensitive entries require a separate assessment of the relevant tax rules and the transaction facts; account classification alone does not determine the tax outcome. Estimates should have an identifiable event, calculation method, assumptions, owner, and review date. Reviewers should independently recalculate material amounts and investigate differences rather than merely confirm that the preparer’s spreadsheet displays a plausible result. Any change to an input or calculation after review must be assessed for impact on approval and posting.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a sample foreign-currency journal and an allocation independently, documenting sources, formulas, assumptions, and differences."

Key point:
- Calculations must be reproducible from documented inputs and methods.
- Tax and other specialist conclusions require the relevant expertise and authority.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and period-end adjustments
13. Q: What should an independent reviewer test before recommending approval?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should examine the business purpose, eligibility of the general-ledger route, supporting evidence, applicable accounting policy, account and dimension coding, calculations, currency, posting period, transaction completeness, duplicate risk, approval authority, and consistency with related records. The reviewer should confirm that the journal description explains the economic substance rather than simply repeating an account name. Where applicable, review should include tax, donor, legal, grant, specialist, or system-owner clearance. The reviewer should also consider unusual amounts, unexpected period movements, manual overrides, unsupported assumptions, and changes from previously approved treatment. Each review test needs an explicit outcome, such as passed, failed, or held, and evidence of how any exception was resolved. A verbal assurance or unexplained tick mark should not replace the evidence required by policy.

Study instruction:
- "Apply a reviewer checklist to a complete journal and retain evidence of each test, exception, resolution, and final recommendation."

Key point:
- Review must test substance, evidence, coding, calculations, authority, and risk.
- Unresolved material exceptions prevent routine approval.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processing and review
14. Q: How should approval thresholds and delegated authority be incorporated into journal workflows?
A: Show answer
The organization should establish documented approval rules appropriate to journal type, financial exposure, risk, entity, currency, and any applicable special requirements. Thresholds should be based on the approved delegation framework rather than improvised by the preparer. The workflow should verify that the selected approver is active, authorized for the transaction category, within the applicable limit, and not prohibited from approving the entry due to a conflict or incompatible duty. A journal exceeding a limit may require escalation to a higher authority or additional review. Splitting a journal merely to circumvent an approval threshold must be prohibited. Delegations should be reviewed when staff change roles, leave the organization, or receive revised authority. Approval records should be retained with the version they authorize.

Study instruction:
- "Test several approval scenarios, including an expired delegation, an over-limit journal, and a request to split a transaction to avoid a threshold."

Key point:
- Approval must follow current documented authority and applicable limits.
- Workflows should detect self-approval, expired authority, and attempts to bypass thresholds.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger roles and approvals
15. Q: What is the difference between a journal that is drafted, approved, posted, and reconciled?
A: Show answer
These statuses describe different stages and must have separate completion evidence. Draft means the entry is being prepared and may still change. Approved means an authorized person has accepted a specific version for the defined purpose. Posted means the official ledger or relevant accounting system has recorded the expected transaction and provides identifiable posting evidence. Reconciled means an independent check has established that the posted result agrees with the registered entry, source records, relevant subledger, and control totals, with any differences explained or resolved. A workflow must not advance a journal to a later status merely because the previous step appears complete in a spreadsheet. For example, an approved journal whose posting failed remains unposted. A posted journal with an unexplained variance remains unreconciled. The status model should define permitted transitions, responsible actors, prerequisites, evidence, and exception handling.

Study instruction:
- "For a journal with approval evidence but a failed posting, identify its correct status and the evidence required before it can be marked posted or reconciled."

Key point:
- Approval, posting, and reconciliation are distinct control outcomes.
- Each status requires its own criteria and supporting evidence.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processing and document status
16. Q: How should the posting date and accounting period be controlled?
A: Show answer
The posting date determines the period in which the journal is recorded under the system's rules, while other transaction dates may establish the business event or the period to which the accounting relates. The preparer must determine the appropriate period using the applicable framework and policy, document the reasoning, and validate that the target period is open and permitted. The system date should not silently replace the justified accounting date. Entries requested after a close may require an authorized late-entry procedure, impact assessment, additional approval, or treatment in a subsequent period depending on the facts and applicable requirements. Backdating or reopening a closed period without authority must be prohibited. Period owners should maintain a close calendar and ensure that approved exceptions remain visible until properly resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the service date, invoice date, preparation date, approval date, and intended posting date for a transaction spanning month-end."

Key point:
- The posting period must be supported by the applicable accounting treatment and cut-off rules.
- Closed-period entries require an authorized and documented route.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and period-end processing
17. Q: What evidence proves that a journal has actually been posted to the ledger?
A: Show answer
Posting evidence should come from the authoritative accounting system and identify the transaction sufficiently to link it to the approved journal version. Relevant fields may include the official document number, entity, ledger, posting date, period, account lines, currency and amounts, dimensions, system status, and batch or interface reference. The evidence should demonstrate that the expected lines reached the ledger rather than merely that the user submitted an upload or clicked a posting button. Where a posting batch processes multiple journals, the preparer or posting operator should confirm the result for each individual journal and investigate rejected lines. A spreadsheet-generated number or locally assigned reference is not equivalent to an official ledger document number. If the system result is uncertain, the journal must remain in an unresolved state until the actual ledger position is checked.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an approved journal file with its official ledger extract and verify the document number, amounts, dimensions, and posting period."

Key point:
- The authoritative ledger provides evidence of actual posting.
- Submission or local workbook status alone does not prove successful posting.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger documents and reports
18. Q: What should happen when a posting attempt fails or only some journal lines are posted?
A: Show answer
The posting operator should preserve the system response, batch identifier, timestamp, approved payload version, rejected lines, and any available error details. Before retrying, the operator must query the actual ledger state and determine which lines were posted, rejected, duplicated, or left uncertain. Resubmitting the entire journal without checking can create duplicate debits, credits, or liabilities. The recovery plan should describe the intended final ledger state, the correction method, the person authorizing recovery, and the checks needed to avoid duplication. If recovery changes the original approved lines, the affected review and approval gates must be repeated. The attempt register should retain the failed attempt and its resolution. A technical error should not be cleared merely by changing the workbook status to successful.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a partial posting, compare expected and actual ledger lines, and design a duplicate-safe recovery plan."

Key point:
- Check the actual ledger state before retrying an uncertain posting.
- Preserve failed attempts and reapprove materially changed recovery entries.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger posting and document processing
19. Q: Why should accounts-payable, payroll, banking, fixed-asset, inventory, grant, and advance transactions follow their designated source processes?
A: Show answer
Source processes commonly maintain operational details that a general-ledger-only entry does not capture. Accounts payable may maintain supplier identity, invoice status, payment allocation, and vendor balances. Payroll maintains employee and payroll-period details. Fixed-asset processes maintain asset identification, cost, useful life, depreciation, and disposal information. Inventory and advance processes require their own quantity, custody, settlement, or supporting-document information. Posting around these processes can leave subsidiary records inconsistent with the general ledger, duplicate liabilities or expenses, or bypass authorization controls. The correct procedure is to refer a source-owned event to its responsible module or process owner and link the referral to the journal request. A specialist-approved manual adjustment may be permitted for a defined reason, but the applicable policy and required control account must first be confirmed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a supplier invoice from receipt through AP processing and compare it with the outcome of an incorrectly prepared direct GL entry."

Key point:
- The GL journal process does not replace source-module controls.
- A manual adjustment must follow an authorized route and preserve subledger integrity.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and subsidiary ledgers
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
20. Q: How should recurring journal templates and individual monthly occurrences be controlled?
A: Show answer
A recurring journal template defines an authorized pattern for a particular type of transaction, but approval of the template does not prove that every future occurrence is appropriate. Each occurrence must be validated against the relevant period, current source information, account and dimension mappings, amount calculation, validity dates, previous occurrence, and duplicate checks. The organization should maintain a template owner, approval reference, effective period, expiry or review trigger, calculation basis, reversal method where applicable, and occurrence register. A template must be suspended when the underlying contract, allocation basis, service pattern, accounting policy, or authorization changes. Reusing a prior-month amount without checking current conditions can produce unsupported expenses or liabilities. Failed or missing occurrences should appear in the close-exception report until resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Review a recurring journal template and independently test one monthly occurrence against its current source data and schedule."

Key point:
- Template approval and occurrence approval are separate controls.
- Each occurrence requires current validation and duplicate detection.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Recurring journal documents and general-ledger processing
21. Q: How should accrual journals be supported when an invoice has not yet arrived?
A: Show answer
An accrual may be appropriate when a qualifying expense or obligation relates to the reporting period even though the supplier invoice or final settlement evidence is not yet available. The preparer must establish the relevant event, service or obligation, period, approved accounting basis, estimation method, assumptions, calculation, and supporting evidence. Depending on the facts, evidence may include a contract, service report, milestone confirmation, usage statement, delivery record, or reliable operational data. The estimate should have a responsible owner and a plan for confirmation, settlement, reversal, or adjustment. An accrual must not be used to create an unsupported reserve or to conceal missing documentation. The applicable recognition requirements and organizational accounting policies must be checked before the entry is approved. Subsequent information should be used to compare the estimate with the actual amount and investigate material differences.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an illustrative accrual from service-period evidence and document the estimation basis, assumptions, review, and expected settlement process."

Key point:
- An accrual requires a supportable event, period, and measurement basis.
- Missing invoices do not remove the need to assess recognition, but estimates must remain evidence-based.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Accrual entries and reversals
22. Q: How should an accrual be settled or reversed when the actual invoice arrives?
A: Show answer
The responsible preparer should locate the original accrual, confirm whether it was actually posted, review the approved reversal method, and compare the accrual with the subsequent invoice. The resulting accounting depends on the original entry, the actual service and invoice, the period concerned, and the applicable policy. A subsequent invoice that differs from the accrual requires a documented variance analysis; the difference should not be concealed through an unexplained journal. The procedure should prevent the same expense or liability from being recognized twice. If the original accrual has already been reversed or settled through another route, the preparer must account for that fact before preparing additional entries. Review, approval, posting, and reconciliation evidence should link the original accrual, settlement or reversal, and any necessary adjustment.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a hypothetical accrual of IDR 750,000 with a later invoice of IDR 730,000 and document the variance, reversal state, settlement, and required review."

Key point:
- Confirm the original ledger state before settling or reversing an accrual.
- Actual invoice differences require transparent analysis and correct follow-up.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Accrual entries and reversals
23. Q: How do prepayment and deferral journals differ from ordinary expense recognition?
A: Show answer
A prepayment or deferral journal allocates an amount across periods when the applicable accounting treatment supports recognition of an asset or liability and subsequent release. A payment date alone does not determine whether the whole payment should be expensed immediately. For example, advance payment for a service may require analysis of the contractual rights, service period, and applicable policy before the initial entry and subsequent releases are determined. The preparer should retain the underlying contract, payment evidence, allocation basis, dates, calculation schedule, account mapping, and approval. Each subsequent release must use current dates and the remaining balance, and must not exceed the amount available for release. The organization should review expired schedules, cancelled services, changes in contract terms, and stale balances. The accounting result depends on the actual event and applicable framework, not a generic spreadsheet formula.

Study instruction:
- "Build an illustrative monthly release schedule for an advance-paid service and reconcile the closing balance to the original payment and releases."

Key point:
- Prepayment and deferral entries require an approved recognition and allocation basis.
- Periodic releases must reconcile to the remaining supportable balance.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and period-end adjustments
24. Q: What is the difference between a correction, reversal, reclassification, and prior-period error?
A: Show answer
These terms describe different accounting purposes and should not be treated as interchangeable workflow labels. A reversal counteracts an earlier entry under an approved reversal procedure; eligibility depends on whether the original entry was posted and whether it was already reversed. A correction addresses an identified error in the original transaction or record and should link to the original entry and explain the required remedy. A reclassification moves an amount between accounting categories when evidence supports the revised classification; it should not be used to obscure a recognition or measurement error. A prior-period error requires assessment under the applicable accounting framework, including the nature, period, materiality, and relevant reporting requirements. A change in an estimate or accounting policy may require different treatment from correction of an error. The preparer must identify the substance of the issue before selecting the procedure and journal type.

Study instruction:
- "Classify four sample cases as reversal, correction, reclassification, or potential prior-period error and state what authority and evidence are needed."

Key point:
- Choose the procedure based on the substance and history of the issue.
- Prior-period errors and changes in estimates or policies require separate assessment.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger adjustments
25. Q: Why must the original journal and its supporting evidence be preserved after a correction?
A: Show answer
Preserving the original record allows reviewers and auditors to reconstruct the initial decision, identify the error, understand the approved correction, and verify its financial effect. The correction record should include the original journal reference, actual posting state, description of the problem, root cause, affected accounts and dimensions, proposed remedy, approval, replacement or adjustment journal, and reconciliation outcome. The original entry should not be deleted or overwritten merely to make the current report appear correct. Where the system provides reversals or linked correction documents, those facilities should be used according to the authorized process. The file archive and audit trail must retain the original and subsequent versions under the relevant retention policy. The organization must also evaluate whether the error affects other reports, donor submissions, tax filings, or management decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a fictional correction from the original entry to the approved replacement and identify all evidence required to reconstruct the sequence."

Key point:
- Corrections must preserve the original record and the complete audit link.
- The impact of a correction extends beyond the journal when other reports or filings are affected.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Correction of errors
26. Q: How should journal entries be reconciled to source records, subledgers, and the general ledger?
A: Show answer
Reconciliation should compare the registered journal population with actual ledger entries and the relevant source records, subsidiary ledgers, batch totals, and control accounts. It should establish that expected entries are complete, actual entries are not duplicated, and posted amounts, dimensions, and periods agree with the approved records. The reconciler should investigate missing entries, unexpected lines, amount differences, incorrect periods, duplicate postings, and unmatched source-module balances. A zero difference does not itself establish that reconciliation is complete; the source population and method must also be reliable, and an independent person must review the result. Each difference should have a cause, owner, resolution plan, due date, and supporting evidence. Material unresolved discrepancies must remain open or be escalated rather than marked reconciled to meet a close deadline.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a journal register with an official GL extract and source records, including at least one missing-entry case and one duplicate-posting case."

Key point:
- Reconciliation tests completeness and agreement, not merely final arithmetic.
- Unexplained differences require investigation and documented resolution.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliations
27. Q: How should journals be incorporated into the monthly and year-end close process?
A: Show answer
The close process should begin with a defined population of expected journal activity, including manual requests, recurring occurrences, accruals, reversals, specialist entries, corrections, and relevant source-system interfaces. The period owner should monitor intake completeness, review and approval status, failed or partial postings, late entries, unprocessed referrals, and outstanding reconciliations. Accruals and other period-end adjustments must follow the applicable recognition and measurement policy. The close checklist should identify responsible owners, due dates, evidence, blockers, and escalation points. Before certification, the period owner should confirm that material journal populations and interfaces have been assessed, required adjustments are complete, and unresolved exceptions have been properly controlled. The close should be locked only through the authorized procedure after the required sign-offs. Year-end procedures may involve additional disclosures, reporting adjustments, and specialist reviews that cannot automatically be inferred from monthly practice.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a journal close checklist and identify the controls that prevent an unapproved, unposted, or unreconciled item from being treated as complete."

Key point:
- Journal management is a controlled component of period-end close.
- Close certification requires population completeness, exception control, and authorized period closure.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and period-end activities
28. Q: How should journal exceptions be recorded, prioritized, and escalated?
A: Show answer
An exception register should identify the journal or source transaction, issue type, date detected, value and affected reporting areas where known, severity, owner, due date, required action, escalation path, current status, and resolution evidence. Examples include missing documents, unsupported calculations, an inactive account, a wrong period, a suspected duplicate, an expired approval, a failed posting, or a material reconciliation difference. Priority should reflect the potential financial, compliance, operational, donor, fraud, and reporting impact, together with the deadline and age of the issue. The process should distinguish a routine administrative correction from a serious control failure requiring immediate escalation. Exceptions must not disappear from reporting when they are moved to another period or reassigned to another employee. Closure requires evidence that the underlying problem was resolved and that any affected accounting records or reports were corrected appropriately.

Study instruction:
- "Create an exception register for three journal issues and explain how severity, owner, escalation, and closure evidence are determined."

Key point:
- Exceptions require clear ownership, deadlines, and evidence-based closure.
- High-risk issues must be escalated according to approved criteria.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger controls and reconciliation
29. Q: How should tax, donor, legal, and specialist accounting questions affect the journal approval process?
A: Show answer
A journal should be held when a material accounting, tax, contractual, donor-eligibility, legal, or specialist question cannot yet be resolved using reliable evidence and applicable authority. The workflow should identify the specific question, facts, relevant documents, specialist responsible, required decision, and evidence needed to clear the hold. Tax classification must not be assumed from an account name or nonprofit status alone. Donor eligibility must be assessed using the applicable executed award and cost rules, while accounting recognition must follow the applicable accounting framework. These are related but distinct judgments. A specialist's advice must be reviewed and recorded within the relevant delegation and governance arrangements. If a decision changes the journal lines, account, amount, dimensions, or period, the changed version must undergo the applicable validation and approval gates again.

Study instruction:
- "Review a hypothetical journal with an unresolved tax and donor issue and list the facts, agreements, specialist conclusions, and approvals needed before release."

Key point:
- Accounting treatment, tax classification, and donor eligibility are separate decisions.
- An unresolved material hold must block unauthorized approval or posting.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger controls and processing
30. Q: What additional checks are required for foreign-currency journal entries?
A: Show answer
The preparer should identify the original currency and amount, the accounting purpose of the conversion, the applicable rate methodology, the appropriate rate date, the currency direction, and the policy governing conversion or remeasurement. The calculation should show the rate source, rate precision, rounding method, resulting functional-currency amount, and relevant source balances. Reviewers should distinguish transaction conversion from period-end remeasurement because different accounting events may require different rate treatments under the applicable framework. A rate copied from a prior entry or an unrelated exchange-rate website is not sufficient merely because the resulting amount appears reasonable. Exchange differences should be evaluated under the relevant accounting requirements and organizational policy. If the rate source or methodology is uncertain, the entry should be held for clarification and independent calculation.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a sample foreign-currency journal and explain the evidence needed for the rate, date, conversion method, and accounting treatment."

Key point:
- Currency calculations require an authorized methodology and verifiable rate evidence.
- Conversion, remeasurement, and exchange-difference treatment must not be conflated.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and period-end revaluation
31. Q: How should intercompany, interfund, interoffice, or interagency journals be controlled?
A: Show answer
The preparer must establish the relationship between the parties, the nature of the balance, the relevant legal entities or funds, the underlying agreement or policy, and the permitted accounting route. Where reciprocal entries are required, the organizations or offices should agree on the amount, currency, transaction reference, accounting period, and settlement or elimination status. Differences can arise from timing, foreign-currency conversion, inconsistent coding, or transactions recorded by only one party. Those differences should be identified and investigated rather than forced into a balancing account without evidence. The reviewer should confirm that the journal does not misclassify an internal movement as external revenue or create an unsupported receivable or payable. Consolidation and reciprocal-clearing requirements depend on the actual entity structure and applicable accounting framework.

Study instruction:
- "Compare reciprocal balances between two offices and document the source of any differences before proposing an adjustment."

Key point:
- Reciprocal entries require consistent counterparty, amount, period, and reference data.
- Differences need evidence-based resolution, not unsupported balancing entries.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation procedures
32. Q: How should restricted funding and project allocations be reflected in journal entries?
A: Show answer
The preparer must distinguish the accounting treatment of a transaction from the funding source and the reporting dimensions used to track its use. Donor restrictions, eligible-cost rules, budget limitations, contractual obligations, and approved organizational policies may affect how a transaction is authorized and reported, but they do not replace the applicable accounting framework. The source and purpose of funding should be documented, together with the basis for any allocation across projects, donors, or cost centres. The reviewer should verify that allocation percentages, shared-cost methods, and project assignments are supported, consistently applied, and reconciled to the source amount. Changes that affect donor reporting may require additional approval or notification. A journal must not be used to move expenditure between projects solely to improve reported budget performance or conceal an ineligible cost.

Study instruction:
- "Trace an illustrative shared-cost allocation to its approved method, funding restrictions, source total, and resulting project balances."

Key point:
- Accounting recognition and donor/project allocation are related but distinct decisions.
- Allocations must be evidence-based, consistent, and reconciled to the source transaction.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and financial reporting dimensions
33. Q: What system-access and audit-trail controls should protect the journal-entry process?
A: Show answer
The organization should provide users with permissions appropriate to their responsibilities and restrict access to journal creation, approval, posting, period management, configuration, and administration. The system should record material actions, including creation, editing, review, approval, posting attempts, reversals, changes to mappings, period changes, and access administration, with identifiable users and timestamps where supported. Access should be reviewed periodically and removed promptly when duties change or end. Privileged users should not grant themselves unreviewed access or bypass mandatory approval controls. Audit logs must be protected from unauthorized alteration and retained under an approved policy. Shared credentials reduce accountability and should not be used as a substitute for role-based permissions. When the system cannot provide a complete audit trail, compensating records and monitoring should be documented and independently reviewed.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect the available journal audit logs and compare user access with the current responsibility and delegation matrix."

Key point:
- Permissions should follow job responsibilities and approved authority.
- Audit trails must make consequential changes and system actions traceable.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger roles and system processes
34. Q: How should journal records be archived while protecting confidentiality and preserving audit evidence?
A: Show answer
The archive should link the original request, evidence, calculation workbook, reviewer notes, approval record, exact approved version, posting confirmation, reconciliation, exception resolution, and related correspondence. Consistent naming, unique transaction identifiers, controlled folder structures, and an index make retrieval faster and reduce the risk of using obsolete documents. Final records must be distinguished from drafts and superseded versions. Access should be restricted according to business need, especially where evidence contains employee, supplier, donor, bank, or other sensitive information. Retention periods should follow applicable law, donor or contractual obligations, organizational policy, and any relevant litigation or investigation hold. Backup arrangements should be tested by retrieving and opening stored evidence rather than merely confirming that a backup job completed. Deletion or alteration of required audit evidence should follow an authorized retention process.

Study instruction:
- "Retrieve one completed journal package from the archive and verify that all evidence, approvals, versions, and posting references can be opened and traced."

Key point:
- A complete journal archive supports reconstruction and independent review.
- Access, privacy, retention, and recovery require explicit controls.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger records and reporting processes
35. Q: How can management monitor whether journal-entry controls are operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include the number and value of journals posted after cut-off, rejected or partially posted entries, approval exceptions, journals lacking required evidence, duplicate candidates, unresolved reconciliation differences, aging of open exceptions, correction frequency, and average time from request to reconciliation. Each measure needs a definition, source, calculation method, reporting frequency, owner, target where appropriate, and escalation threshold. For example, approval timeliness should be measured against the full population of journals requiring approval, not only those already approved. A high volume of manual journals may indicate a legitimate process need or a source-module weakness, so trends must be interpreted in context. Management should review recurring causes and assign corrective actions with due dates. Automated dashboards should be reconciled to authoritative records before their results are used for decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a journal-control dashboard and validate each KPI against the journal register, system posting records, and reconciliation exceptions."

Key point:
- Effective monitoring needs clear definitions and reliable source data.
- Trends should lead to documented root-cause analysis and corrective action.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger controls and reporting
36. Q: What must be completed before a fictional journal-entry SOP can be adopted as an actual NGO policy?
A: Show answer
The organization must replace fictional names, entity identifiers, account codes, transaction amounts, rates, source systems, approval records, and posting references with verified organizational information. It must establish the applicable accounting framework and current policies; validate the chart of accounts and source-module routes; confirm legal, tax, donor, contractual, and reporting requirements; assign policy ownership; and approve role delegations and segregation-of-duties controls. It must also confirm the forms, registers, workflow statuses, error-handling procedures, record retention, and escalation arrangements that staff will actually use. The draft should be reviewed by the relevant finance, tax, legal/compliance, grants, and system owners, with the appropriate governing or delegated authority approving release. Testing should include normal cases, exceptions, failed postings, changed versions, and unresolved holds. Document adoption, effective date, training, and review triggers before describing the SOP as operational.

Study instruction:
- "Create an adoption checklist that distinguishes verified organizational facts, policy decisions, system tests, external requirements, and approvals still outstanding."

Key point:
- A fictional example cannot establish actual policy or prove operational readiness.
- Adoption requires entity-specific verification, testing, and authorized approval.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual — Official accounting guidance
37. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, or VBA support journal preparation without replacing accounting judgment?
A: Show answer
Excel can maintain controlled journal forms and calculation schedules; Power Query can import, standardize, and combine source data; and VBA can automate repeatable tasks such as preparing journal batches, validating required fields, generating exception reports, exporting approved reports, and recording workflow events. These tools can help identify missing references, inactive codes, duplicate candidates, unbalanced entries, unusual amounts, and incomplete approvals. However, software cannot independently establish that an accounting treatment is permitted or that a donor cost is eligible. Accounting policy and authorized professional judgment must govern the rules encoded in the workbook. Formula, query, mapping, and macro changes should be version-controlled, tested on representative data, independently reviewed, and approved before release. The organization should retain refresh logs, input totals, output versions, error results, and reconciliation evidence so automated outputs can be reproduced and audited.

Study instruction:
- "Design a controlled journal-preparation workbook and test it with missing evidence, invalid dimensions, duplicate candidates, and unbalanced entries."

Key point:
- Automation improves repeatability but does not establish accounting validity.
- Formula and workflow changes require testing, authorization, and traceable outputs.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger controls
- Excel VBA Reference — Microsoft Learn
38. Q: How should journal templates, account mappings, and workflow configurations be changed safely?
A: Show answer
A controlled change begins with a documented request describing the problem, proposed amendment, affected journals or reports, business reason, owner, and intended effective date. The change owner should assess implications for accounting policy, account availability, required dimensions, source-module routing, approval thresholds, existing recurring templates, reporting, and historical comparability. Technical changes must be tested using representative data and negative cases, including invalid accounts, incompatible dimensions, changed approval versions, duplicate attempts, and closed-period entries. An independent reviewer should assess the test results and verify that the change does not bypass required controls. Production release should be authorized, versioned, communicated to affected users, and supported by a rollback or recovery plan where practicable. Historical records must retain their original context so a future reviewer can establish which configuration applied when an entry was created and posted.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a change-control record for a revised account mapping and verify impact assessment, test results, approval, release, and rollback arrangements."

Key point:
- Configuration changes can affect accounting classification and control effectiveness.
- Changes require documented ownership, testing, approval, and controlled release.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processes
- Getting Started with VBA in Office — Microsoft Learn
39. Q: How should a no-entry decision be documented when a requested journal is not appropriate?
A: Show answer
A request that should not produce a journal must be resolved explicitly rather than silently ignored. The reviewer or authorized decision-maker should record the underlying facts, reason no entry is required, applicable policy or accounting rationale, relevant source evidence, decision date, responsible person, and any follow-up action. Examples might include a duplicate request, an event that has not met the applicable recognition criteria, a transaction already recorded through a source module, or a request lacking an identifiable business event. The conclusion must be supported by the facts and applicable requirements; the term "no entry" is not a shortcut for avoiding difficult accounting questions. The request should remain traceable and should be closed only after required review and communication. If new evidence changes the conclusion, the request should be reopened under the controlled workflow.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a no-entry decision record for a duplicate request and another request with insufficient evidence, distinguishing the grounds for each decision."

Key point:
- A documented no-entry decision is different from an omitted transaction.
- Each decision requires an evidence-based rationale and appropriate review.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processing and controls
40. Q: How can an NGO demonstrate that journal-entry controls are complete, tested, and audit-ready?
A: Show answer
The organization should maintain a controlled inventory of journal categories, workflows, decision gates, roles, forms, master-data checks, status transitions, tests, and evidence requirements. Each control should specify its owner, objective, frequency or triggering event, expected result, evidence, escalation route, and closure criteria. Testing should cover both successful transactions and deliberately invalid cases, such as self-approval, missing evidence, inactive accounts, unauthorized period changes, changed approved versions, failed postings, duplicate retries, and unresolved reconciliation differences. A test inventory records what should be tested; it does not prove that a test was executed or passed. Actual test results must identify the input, expected result, observed result, tester, date, defects, and corrective action. Independent reviewers should distinguish local spreadsheet validation, system integration testing, package validation, and live ERP testing. The final SOP should state which acceptance checks were performed and which remain unverified before organizational release.

Study instruction:
- "Select the mandatory controls in a journal-entry workflow and prepare a test register that records expected outcomes, actual results, evidence, defects, and approval."

Key point:
- A control design is not proof that the control operates effectively.
- Audit readiness requires executed tests, reliable evidence, and transparent reporting of unresolved limitations.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processes and controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors

Table 5
Month-End and Year-End Financial Closing Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an integrated month-end and year-end financial closing process?
A: Show answer
A financial close is a controlled sequence of activities used to ensure that an organization's accounting records are complete, accurate, classified correctly, reconciled, reviewed, and ready for financial reporting. It brings together source-data certification, transaction cut-off, accruals, estimates, prepayments, asset schedules, foreign-exchange revaluation, journal posting, balance-sheet reconciliations, trial-balance review, specialist clearances, management certification, and period locking. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Chapter 18 describes general-ledger processes, period-end procedures, reconciliation and adjustment, reporting, and period closure within the United Nations' own accounting environment. For an NGO, the precise procedure must be adapted to its applicable reporting framework, accounting policies, legal requirements, donor conditions, systems, and delegated authority. The supplied SOP 005 is explicitly a fictional demonstration: the named entity, dates, schedules, amounts, approvals, source extracts, and reported outcomes must not be treated as facts about an actual organization. A successful close requires evidence for each material control, not merely a balanced trial balance or completed checklist.

Study instruction:
- "Map the complete financial-close process from source-data collection through reconciliation, certification, and period locking. Distinguish demonstrated controls from verified organizational requirements."
Key point:
- A close is complete only when required accounting, review, evidence, and authorization gates have been satisfied.
- An illustrative SOP is not proof that a real financial period has been closed correctly.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Period-End Procedures
2. Q: What is the difference between month-end closing and year-end closing?
A: Show answer
Month-end closing establishes reliable financial information for an individual reporting month. It normally includes source-data completeness checks, transaction cut-off, recurring accruals, depreciation or amortization where applicable, foreign-exchange revaluation, journal processing, balance-sheet reconciliations, analytical review, and monthly reporting. Year-end closing includes the normal monthly procedures plus additional activities needed to finalize the annual reporting period. These may include annual estimates, asset and liability confirmations, provisions and contingencies, events after the reporting date, annual grant and statutory reporting, financial-statement disclosures, audit schedules, and approval of the annual financial statements. Some activities overlap; year-end does not eliminate the need for reliable monthly records. The applicable framework determines which additional recognition, measurement, presentation, and disclosure requirements apply. NGOs should document which procedures are monthly, which are annual, which occur only under particular conditions, and which belong to separate operational SOPs. A calendar-year entity must not automatically assume that every donor or statutory reporting deadline coincides with its financial year-end.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a comparison of monthly and annual close activities, identifying recurring controls, additional year-end requirements, responsible owners, and required evidence."
Key point:
- Year-end extends the ordinary closing process with additional annual accounting and reporting requirements.
- The entity's reporting calendar and applicable framework determine the actual closing obligations.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End and Year-End Procedures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
3. Q: How should the applicable accounting framework and reporting requirements be established before closing begins?
A: Show answer
The finance function should document the legal entity, reporting purpose, reporting period, applicable accounting framework, relevant accounting policies, statutory requirements, donor obligations, and reporting perimeter before operational close instructions are issued. For a nonprofit organization, nonprofit status alone does not establish which accounting standards govern its financial statements. The organization must examine applicable law, its governing documents, contractual obligations, authorized accounting policy, funding arrangements, and reporting commitments. Mandatory legal requirements and the applicable accounting framework govern within their respective scopes; donor agreements may impose additional reporting and eligibility requirements without automatically replacing statutory accounting requirements. If the framework or an accounting interpretation is uncertain, the issue should be recorded, assigned to a qualified reviewer, and escalated before the affected conclusion is finalized. Historical practice, a system default, or an example journal is not sufficient authority for adopting an accounting treatment. The selected framework and policy version should be recorded in the close instruction and retained as evidence of the basis used for reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reporting-framework decision record and identify the evidence needed to establish the entity's accounting, statutory, and donor-reporting obligations."
Key point:
- Accounting treatment must be based on the applicable authority and verified facts, not on an untested template.
- Unresolved framework questions must remain open until an authorized decision is documented.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Official Chapter Directory and Accounting Guidance
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
4. Q: Which responsibilities should be assigned to the preparer, reviewer, approver, and close coordinator?
A: Show answer
The close coordinator maintains the calendar, monitors dependencies, tracks outstanding tasks, consolidates exceptions, and prepares the overall status report. Source-system owners provide complete, period-specific data and certify its population, version, record counts, and control totals. Preparers construct calculations, journal requests, analytical schedules, and reconciliations. Independent reviewers challenge assumptions, recalculate important figures, inspect supporting documentation, and return incomplete work. Authorized approvers decide whether the applicable requirements have been met and whether a task can proceed within their delegated authority. Journal operators execute approved postings under the applicable journal procedure, while system administrators perform authorized period-locking or reopening operations. Specialist owners address matters such as tax, grants, legal compliance, assets, and foreign currency within their assigned competence. These roles should be documented in a responsibility matrix that includes effective dates, monetary limits where applicable, substitutes, and escalation routes. The person performing a control must not be assumed to have authority to approve the same work merely because they hold a senior position or have system access.

Study instruction:
- "Build a responsibility matrix for one close cycle and test whether every material activity has a preparer, independent reviewer, authorized approver, and evidence owner."
Key point:
- Task ownership, technical access, and approval authority are different responsibilities.
- Delegations must be current, documented, and appropriate to the action being approved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes and Controls
5. Q: Why must segregation of duties be enforced throughout the financial close?
A: Show answer
Segregation of duties reduces the risk that one person can initiate, prepare, approve, execute, and conceal an inappropriate or incorrect transaction. During closing, this principle applies to source-data certification, accrual calculations, asset adjustments, foreign-exchange revaluation, journal approval, reconciliation clearance, certification, and period locking. For example, the person who prepares a material reconciliation should not be its sole independent reviewer, and an administrator who executes a period reopening should not independently authorize that reopening. An NGO with limited staffing may be unable to separate every activity completely. In that situation, management should document compensating controls such as independent recalculation, approval by a different authorized person, post-transaction review, restricted system access, and periodic review of system logs. Compensating controls must be proportionate to the risk, time-limited where appropriate, and supported by evidence. A deadline does not justify bypassing mandatory approval or treating self-review as independent verification.

Study instruction:
- "Identify conflicting duties in the close workflow and design a documented compensating control for each conflict that cannot be eliminated."
Key point:
- Independent review is a substantive control, not simply another signature.
- Staffing constraints require documented alternative controls rather than silent bypasses.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Posting Procedures
6. Q: How should a financial-close calendar be designed and maintained?
A: Show answer
A close calendar should identify the reporting period, legal entity, reporting currency where relevant, task owner, preparer, reviewer, approver, dependencies, internal deadline, expected evidence, status, and escalation route. It should distinguish the financial close deadline from statutory, tax, audit, and donor-reporting deadlines. Those external deadlines must be checked against current authoritative requirements rather than copied from prior months or previous years. The calendar should allow sufficient time for late submissions, corrections, independent review, and escalation. If a prerequisite is delayed, the coordinator should identify downstream tasks that are affected, revise the forecast transparently where permitted, notify responsible owners, and retain the original due date and reason for the change. A task should not be marked complete simply because a scheduled date has passed. The completion status must be supported by the applicable output or control evidence. For year-end, additional milestones should cover annual estimates, confirmations, subsequent-events assessments, disclosure schedules, and audit requests.

Study instruction:
- "Construct a close calendar with dependencies and evidence requirements, then simulate a late payroll or bank file and trace which downstream tasks must be held or reforecast."
Key point:
- The calendar is a dependency-management control as well as a deadline tracker.
- Completion and delay statuses must reflect evidence and actual progress, not planned dates alone.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Month-End Closing Process
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
7. Q: How should the reporting perimeter and completeness of the close population be determined?
A: Show answer
Before closing, finance should establish which legal entities, offices, funds, projects, bank accounts, ledgers, subsidiaries, branches, and reporting dimensions are within scope. It should determine the source system of record for each population and identify any legitimate exclusions or separate processing arrangements. Completeness testing should reconcile the in-scope register to source-system records, opening balances, transaction reports, subsidiary ledgers, and other control totals as appropriate. A report generated successfully does not prove that the correct entity, period, or population was selected. Similarly, a balanced trial balance cannot reveal every omitted transaction or incorrect classification. The scope should be documented alongside the source extract version, extraction timestamp, filters, currencies, record counts, control totals, and any exclusions. Where more than one office or entity is involved, interoffice or intercompany balances may require matching and consolidation controls. Changes to scope must be authorized, recorded, and reflected in affected reconciliations and reports.

Study instruction:
- "Create a reporting-perimeter register and demonstrate how source populations and total controls establish completeness for one reporting period."
Key point:
- Close accuracy depends on both correct processing and a complete, correctly scoped population.
- Scope changes and exclusions require documented authorization and traceable impact assessment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Procedures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Reporting
8. Q: How should source-system owners certify the completeness and reliability of closing data?
A: Show answer
Each source owner should provide a period-specific extract and a certification showing the system or register used, reporting dates, extraction timestamp, applied filters, record count, control totals, file version, known limitations, and status of relevant approvals. The records may originate from accounts payable, payroll, bank and cash systems, procurement, grants, project expenditure, assets, inventory, or other operational applications. Finance should compare the submission to independent evidence such as subsidiary-ledger totals, system control reports, opening balances, or known transaction populations. It should investigate missing files, unexplained reductions in records, duplicate submissions, inconsistent currencies, incomplete periods, and changes made after the initial certification. A certification by the source owner is evidence of accountability, but it does not eliminate independent review. Updated extracts should retain version identity so that reviewers can determine which information was used in calculations, journals, and reports.

Study instruction:
- "Design a source-completeness form and test it against extracts from at least three systems, including one file that is deliberately incomplete."
Key point:
- Received files must be reconciled to evidence of the expected population.
- Source certifications should identify scope, version, limitations, and control totals.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Data and Reconciliation Controls
9. Q: How should transaction cut-off be reviewed at month-end and year-end?
A: Show answer
Cut-off review determines which reporting period should contain a transaction under the applicable accounting requirements and the facts of the transaction. Finance should examine goods received, services delivered, payroll earned, invoices received late, commitments, accruals, prepayments, transactions recorded after closing, and other events around the reporting date. The invoice date or cash-payment date does not always establish the period of expense recognition. For example, a supplier invoice received in July for services completed in June may require examination for a June accrual. Conversely, a payment made in June for services relating to future months may represent a prepayment if recognition criteria and organizational policies support that treatment. Different taxes, donor reports, and statutory obligations may use different triggering events or deadlines, so accounting cut-off must not be substituted for a tax-law conclusion. Finance should document the relevant dates, evidence, period selected, applicable policy, review decision, and any unresolved exception.

Study instruction:
- "Review transactions immediately before and after period-end and document the evidence and accounting rationale for each cut-off decision."
Key point:
- Recognition timing must follow the applicable accounting requirements and the underlying transaction facts.
- Accounting cut-off, payment timing, and statutory reporting triggers must be distinguished.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and Period Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Accruals and Closing Procedures
10. Q: How should accruals for services received but not yet invoiced be prepared and reviewed?
A: Show answer
An accrual may be necessary when an expense has been incurred during the reporting period but the invoice or payment has not yet occurred, subject to the applicable recognition requirements. Finance should obtain evidence of the goods or services received, service period, purchase order or contract, delivery or service acceptance, agreed price, available invoice information, and any relevant estimates. The preparer calculates the amount and records the assumptions, period, account, project dimensions, supporting references, and proposed journal. The reviewer challenges whether the obligation relates to the current period, whether the quantity and rate are reasonable, whether the amount overlaps with an existing payable, and whether another process has already recognized the expense. Under an accrual-basis approach, an illustrative entry may debit the appropriate expense and credit an accrued liability, but the actual accounts and recognition basis must follow the entity's authorized policies. If the estimate is uncertain, the uncertainty and method should be documented rather than hidden in a round-number adjustment.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate an accrual from evidence of services delivered, compare the estimate with a subsequent invoice, and document the approved accounting adjustment."
Key point:
- Accruals recognize qualifying current-period obligations even when invoicing or payment occurs later.
- Evidence, assumptions, duplication checks, and independent review support the estimate.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Accrual Entry and Period-End Procedures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and Recognition
11. Q: How should financial estimates and provisions be distinguished from ordinary accruals?
A: Show answer
An ordinary accrual generally addresses a qualifying expense or liability whose timing or final invoice differs from the reporting period. A provision involves a separate assessment of whether a liability with uncertainty in timing or amount meets the recognition requirements of the applicable framework. Finance should not use a provision as a general reserve for future spending, anticipated budget pressure, or unexplained differences. It should identify the underlying event, available evidence, nature of the obligation, estimation basis, uncertainty, expected timing, approval, and any required reassessment or disclosure. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Chapter 15 addresses provisions, contingent liabilities, contingent assets, and events after the reporting date.
