Whether a particular claim, dispute, warranty, employee matter, or other obligation qualifies must be determined against the entity's applicable framework and circumstances. When a provision is no longer supported, any release or adjustment must be justified and approved under the applicable requirements. A provision must not be used to offset unrelated expenditure or conceal a reconciliation difference.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an unbilled-service accrual, a qualifying provision, and a possible contingent liability. State what evidence would support each conclusion."
Key point:
- Accruals, provisions, and contingent liabilities have different recognition considerations.
- Uncertainty must be assessed under the applicable accounting framework and documented evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
12. Q: How should prepayments, deferred balances, and advances be reviewed during closing?
A: Show answer
A closing review should examine opening balances, additions, payments, releases, settlements, adjustments, aging, and closing balances for each material account. Prepayments generally require evidence that the payment relates to goods or services whose benefit extends into a future period. The qualifying balance is released according to the relevant benefit period and authorized accounting policy. Advances should remain separately identifiable until supported expenditure, recovery, refund, or another valid settlement event is documented. Deferred income or similar balances must be assessed against the applicable accounting requirements, contract terms, and grant conditions rather than automatically released according to cash receipts. For each schedule, finance should reconcile the calculated closing balance to the general ledger and investigate negative amounts, overdue items, missing end dates, unused advances, expired contracts, and unexplained movements. Donor-specific eligibility and release requirements must be assessed separately when applicable. A balance should not be cleared solely to finish the close or because a supporting document has been requested but not received.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a roll-forward for a prepayment, an advance, and a deferred balance, then reconcile each closing total to the general ledger."
Key point:
- Each balance requires a documented explanation of its opening amount, movements, and closing position.
- Releases and settlements must be supported by the relevant recognition rules and evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Closing Schedules
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
13. Q: How should payroll and employee-benefit balances be included in the monthly and annual close?
A: Show answer
Finance should reconcile payroll registers, approved payroll changes, employee movements, payments, payroll liabilities, relevant benefits, and general-ledger balances. It should assess whether services performed or benefits earned before the reporting date require recognition even if payment is scheduled later. At year-end, additional employee-benefit estimates or valuations may be required under the applicable framework and the entity's actual arrangements. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Chapter 6 addresses employee benefits, including liabilities and timing differences affecting earned and unpaid benefits. For each calculation, the organization should document the population, underlying period, methodology, source reports, assumptions, calculation version, reviewer, and journal linkage. Finance should investigate differences between payroll expense, cash paid, liabilities, withholding amounts, and benefits schedules instead of assuming that those totals must always be identical. Tax calculations and statutory payroll reporting also require their own current-law validation and should not be inferred solely from financial-accounting treatment.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile payroll expense, amounts paid, unpaid benefits, payroll liabilities, and relevant tax schedules for one completed period."
Key point:
- Employee costs may belong to the period in which qualifying service or benefit obligations arise, not necessarily the payment period.
- Annual benefit valuations and local payroll-tax duties require their own applicable-authority review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Employee Benefits
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Accruals and General-Ledger Closing
14. Q: What fixed-asset controls are necessary before the period can be certified?
A: Show answer
The asset close should establish the completeness and accuracy of the fixed-asset register, additions, disposals, transfers, construction or development balances where applicable, assets placed in service, depreciation or amortization calculations, and related general-ledger balances. Finance should confirm that the asset register and ledger cover the same entity, reporting period, currency, and population. Changes to asset cost, useful life, residual value, capitalization thresholds, or disposal status require authorization under the applicable policy. Depreciation or another applicable allocation method should be based on the asset's approved parameters and the relevant recognition requirements. An unexplained difference between the asset subledger and the general ledger must be investigated, not cleared through an unsupported journal. Donor-funded equipment also needs appropriate tracking where the entity's rights, control, policy, or reporting obligations require it. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Chapter 8 addresses property, plant and equipment, but the actual entity's framework and capitalization policy must be verified before applying a particular treatment.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a fixed-asset register to the general ledger, independently recalculate depreciation for a sample, and investigate one disposal or in-service-date exception."
Key point:
- Asset-register completeness and ledger agreement are separate controls that both require evidence.
- Asset accounting parameters cannot be changed solely to eliminate a period-end variance.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangible Assets
15. Q: How should lease-related balances and schedules be considered during year-end closing?
A: Show answer
Lease-related balances should be reviewed using the applicable accounting framework, the executed contract, any approved lease assessment, amendments, payment schedules, and relevant operational facts. Finance should identify lease arrangements within the reporting perimeter, verify that the lease inventory is complete, and reconcile the balances and movements required by the applicable framework. Depending on the governing rules and the arrangement, the review may need to consider recognition, measurement, interest or depreciation calculations, payments, modifications, reassessments, and disclosures. The close team should not assume that every rental payment has identical accounting treatment or that a contract labelled as a service cannot contain a lease arrangement. Procurement or facilities staff may provide contract evidence, but the accounting conclusion should be made by the authorized technical owner. Any unresolved contract interpretation or policy question should remain a recorded hold if it affects material recognition, measurement, presentation, or disclosure.

Study instruction:
- "Select a sample of rental and equipment contracts, identify the information needed for the applicable lease assessment, and reconcile the approved schedule to the ledger."
Key point:
- Contract descriptions and payment patterns alone do not establish the accounting treatment.
- Lease conclusions depend on the applicable framework, contract facts, and approved accounting policy.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Official Accounting Guidance and Chapter Directory
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Closing Procedures
16. Q: How should inventory and consumable balances be verified at period-end?
A: Show answer
Inventory close controls should establish that the recorded population and valuation reflect the applicable accounting policy and the actual stock position. Finance should coordinate with storekeepers and operational owners to obtain controlled stock counts or approved movement reports, receipts, issues, transfers, write-offs, adjustments, and the closing inventory valuation. It should reconcile quantities and values to the relevant inventory records and general-ledger accounts, investigate negative stock or unexplained movements, and determine whether damaged, obsolete, missing, or unusable items require assessment under the applicable policy. Consumables purchased in bulk may require an accounting assessment of whether items remain on hand or have already been consumed; the correct treatment depends on the entity's policy and materiality considerations. Differences must not be eliminated by changing quantities or posting unsupported adjustments. The responsible operational owner should document physical evidence and Finance should independently review material variances and resulting accounting entries.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile opening stock, receipts, issues, adjustments, and closing stock, then investigate a material difference between the physical count and the inventory ledger."
Key point:
- The purchase register and the closing stock position answer different questions.
- Physical evidence, valuation, and ledger reconciliation are distinct controls.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Official Chapter Directory, Including Inventory Guidance
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Reconciliation and Adjustment
17. Q: How should foreign-currency monetary balances be revalued at period-end?
A: Show answer
The close team should identify foreign-currency monetary balances in scope, verify the applicable reporting currency and approved exchange-rate policy, obtain the authorized period-end rate source, and record the currency pair, quotation direction, rate date, timestamp, and any approved override. The calculation should use the appropriate rate methodology for the applicable accounting framework and the particular balance. Finance must distinguish period-end remeasurement from the original foreign-currency transaction amount and from later cash settlement. The result should be independently recalculated, reconciled to the ledger, and reviewed for unusual movements and missing rates. If a rate source is unknown, a currency direction is reversed, or the approved method is unclear, the affected result should remain on hold pending resolution. Accounting translation, statutory tax conversion, and donor-reporting conversion may use different rules; one spreadsheet rate should not automatically be applied to all three purposes. Any journal must be prepared and posted through the authorized process with evidence of the source and calculation version.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a sample of foreign-currency balances, verify the rate source and quotation direction, and reconcile the approved result to the ledger."
Key point:
- Exchange-rate source, period, quotation direction, and applicable accounting policy must be documented.
- Financial-reporting revaluation must be distinguished from tax and donor-specific currency conversion requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Revaluation and General-Ledger Procedures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
18. Q: What controls are required when preparing and posting month-end adjustment journals?
A: Show answer
Every proposed journal should identify the entity, reporting period, accounts, debit and credit amounts, currency, relevant fund or project dimensions, supporting calculation, source references, preparer, reviewer, approver, and purpose. The journal must follow the applicable chart of accounts, accounting policy, delegated authority, and journal-processing procedure. The preparer should demonstrate the calculation and intended accounting effect; the reviewer should examine recognition, classification, period, completeness, reasonableness, and supporting evidence. Approval must be linked to the precise version being processed. After submission, Finance should inspect the actual system status and capture the posting identifier or rejection details. A balanced journal is not necessarily a correct journal, and a submission confirmation is not proof of posting. The close schedule should link each adjustment to the applicable reconciliation and report. Rejected, duplicated, partially processed, or unexpectedly modified requests must be investigated before another attempt is made.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a proposed adjustment from its supporting workpaper through review, approval, actual posting evidence, and the resulting ledger balance."
Key point:
- Journal approval and actual system posting are separate events.
- Every material adjustment must be supported, authorized, linked to a reporting purpose, and traceable to the final ledger.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Journals and Closing Entries
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
19. Q: When is a journal reversal appropriate, and how should the reversal be controlled?
A: Show answer
A reversal may be appropriate where the original accounting entry is designed to be reversed in a later period, such as certain accrued expenses that will be replaced by an actual invoice or another properly recorded transaction. Whether reversal is appropriate depends on the nature of the entry, accounting policy, source-process design, and the possibility of duplicate recognition when the subsequent transaction is recorded. The schedule should record the original journal identifier, approved reversal date or rule, reason for reversal, and evidence that the reversal occurred. Before processing a correction or reversal, Finance should verify the original document and circumstances, identify any subsequent settlement, and determine whether a reversal would create a new error. The UN Finance Manual describes specific reversal procedures in its own system, but those procedures should not be copied mechanically into an NGO's ERP. If a reversal fails, remains pending, or is posted twice, the team should retain the actual status evidence and route the issue through the authorized journal and incident process.

Study instruction:
- "Review an accrued expense and its later invoice, determine whether reversal is appropriate, and trace the original and subsequent entries to their system statuses."
Key point:
- A reversal is a controlled accounting event, not a general way to erase an error.
- The original and reversing records must remain linked and independently verifiable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reversals and Accrual Entries
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Corrections and Accounting Errors
20. Q: How should balance-sheet reconciliations be completed and reviewed?
A: Show answer
Each material balance-sheet or control account should have a named owner, defined reconciliation frequency, approved reconciliation method, source-of-truth records, and independent reviewer. The reconciliation should identify the entity, period, currency, general-ledger balance, supporting balance, difference, reconciling items, aging, explanation, owner, target date, and closure evidence. Relevant accounts may include cash, receivables, payables, advances, prepayments, deferred balances, employee obligations, inventory, fixed assets, tax balances, and interoffice balances. Reviewers should verify the completeness of the compared populations, the treatment of timing differences, and the validity of adjustments. A zero difference is not sufficient if the supporting records are incomplete or the reconciliation omits relevant transactions. Where differences remain, the preparer should determine their causes and whether correction, reclassification, recovery, or further investigation is necessary. Material or overdue unresolved items must remain visible in the close report and may prevent certification under the entity's approved close policy.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reconciliation for one material control account and have another person independently trace selected items from the ledger to source evidence and back."
Key point:
- Reconciliation establishes whether independent records agree and whether differences are justified.
- Zero balances or zero differences do not replace completeness testing and substantive review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Adjustment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation
21. Q: How should unresolved reconciling items be investigated and cleared?
A: Show answer
Every unresolved item should have a unique identifier, date first identified, account, amount, currency, relevant source references, root-cause hypothesis, risk assessment, named owner, target date, and status. The owner should determine whether the item results from a timing difference, omitted transaction, duplicate, classification error, unmatched payment, incorrect opening balance, unsupported posting, data-conversion problem, or other cause. Evidence should establish both the original discrepancy and the proposed resolution. An approved correcting journal may be necessary, but a journal should not be used merely to force a reconciliation to zero without understanding the cause. Items may require recovery from another party, correction in the source system, a reclassification, or escalation to a specialist. The independent reviewer should confirm that the correction resolves the underlying problem and that the revised balance and relevant downstream reports agree. The close team should preserve the original explanation and correction history rather than overwriting the previous record.

Study instruction:
- "Select three aged reconciling items, establish a root cause for each, and document the resolution evidence and independent retest."
Key point:
- A reconciling item is closed only when its cause and resolution are supported by evidence.
- Unsupported balancing entries can conceal misstatement and prevent effective review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Reconciliations and Adjustments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Errors and Corrections
22. Q: Why is a balanced trial balance insufficient evidence that the close is correct?
A: Show answer
A trial balance demonstrates that the debit and credit totals in the included ledger population agree according to the report's scope. It does not establish that all transactions have been recorded, that the correct period was used, that accounts are correctly classified, or that balances are supported by valid evidence. A transaction can be omitted entirely without making the remaining trial balance unequal. The same is true of an entry posted to the wrong expense account or donor dimension if the debit and credit sides remain balanced. Reviewers must therefore assess source completeness, material cut-off, journal validity, control-account reconciliations, unusual or negative balances, movement analysis, account mappings, and reporting tie-outs. Any trial-balance extract must also be identified by entity, period, ledger, currency, generation time, and version. If journals are posted after the extract is produced, the report may no longer reflect the current ledger and affected checks must be repeated.

Study instruction:
- "Construct examples of an omitted transaction and a wrong-account posting that leave the debit and credit totals balanced, then identify the controls that would detect each."
Key point:
- Arithmetic balance is necessary but does not prove completeness, recognition, classification, or accuracy.
- Trial-balance review must be supported by reconciliations, analytics, and source-level evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Trial Balance and Period-End Review
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statements and Disclosure
23. Q: How should analytical review and account mapping support the financial close?
A: Show answer
Analytical review looks for relationships and movements that require explanation, such as unexpected month-to-month expense changes, unusually large balances, negative asset balances, unexplained fluctuations in payroll costs, or differences between project reporting and general-ledger totals. Thresholds for investigation should be documented and appropriate to the organization; illustrative values in a template must not be treated as approved materiality limits. Finance should compare actual figures with prior periods, budgets, forecasts, operational drivers, and relevant source schedules. Account mapping should be checked to ensure the general ledger feeds the correct financial-statement line, donor report, fund report, or management category. The review should distinguish legitimate changes in activity from classification errors, incomplete postings, duplicate records, changes in estimates, and other issues. Every material variance should have a documented explanation, supporting evidence, reviewer disposition, and corrective action where needed. Changes to mappings must be authorized and tested because a mapping change can alter reports without changing the ledger balance itself.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a monthly variance analysis, investigate one material movement, and trace the affected account mapping into the final reporting pack."
Key point:
- Analytical review identifies risk indicators that transaction-level controls may miss.
- Account mapping and reporting outputs require version control and independent validation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Reports and Closing Controls
24. Q: How should grants, donor restrictions, and project reporting be incorporated into the close?
A: Show answer
Finance should reconcile relevant general-ledger balances with approved grant records, project expenditure, donor schedules, advances, receipts, and reporting commitments. The team should identify the executed agreement, applicable period, permitted uses, reporting currency, allowable-cost conditions, funding restrictions, and donor deadlines. Accounting recognition must follow the applicable framework and actual grant terms; receipt of cash or the existence of a donor code does not by itself establish the correct revenue recognition treatment or prove that costs are eligible. Donor reporting rules may differ from statutory accounting presentation and should be reconciled rather than silently substituted for it. Where a cost is shared across projects, the allocation basis must be supportable and the total allocation must agree to the original cost population. Material variances, questioned costs, expired agreements, incomplete partner reports, and potential recoveries should be recorded as exceptions with owners and deadlines. The close should preserve evidence connecting the accounting result to grant terms, source transactions, approvals, and the report submitted to the donor.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a grant and reconcile its opening balance, receipts, qualifying expenditure, advances, reported use, and closing position against the agreement and ledger."
Key point:
- Donor eligibility, accounting recognition, and cash movement are related but distinct questions.
- Project and donor reports must be reconciled to controlled accounting records and applicable grant terms.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
25. Q: How should tax, statutory, and other specialist obligations be incorporated into close certification?
A: Show answer
The close coordinator should obtain the status of relevant specialist obligations from the responsible tax, legal, compliance, grants, or statutory-reporting owners. Each applicable task should identify the legal entity, obligation, period, authoritative deadline, required calculation or report, review and approval requirements, submission evidence, and unresolved issues. The financial close must not infer that a tax return has been submitted merely because a provision or tax journal has been recorded. Likewise, a donor report is not automatically correct because the general ledger balances. Specialist teams should confirm the actual status and provide evidence that the relevant requirement has been met or identify a formal hold. If specialist obligations are outside the scope of the close, the exclusion and responsible procedure should be documented rather than silently omitted. The finance team must distinguish the accounting treatment of a liability from the separate legal or statutory filing obligation. When a material compliance issue remains unresolved, the approver should assess its financial-reporting implications before certification.

Study instruction:
- "Create a specialist-clearance register listing applicable tax, statutory, donor, and legal tasks with owners, deadlines, required evidence, and escalation criteria."
Key point:
- Accounting entries do not prove that an external filing or statutory obligation has been completed.
- Material unresolved compliance matters must be assessed and escalated before certification.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Procedures
26. Q: What additional controls are required for the year-end close beyond routine monthly procedures?
A: Show answer
Year-end planning should extend the normal close calendar to cover annual estimates, asset and liability confirmations, inventory counts where required, payroll and employee-benefit assessments, grant and partner reconciliations, provisions, contingencies, subsequent events, tax and statutory obligations, and financial-statement disclosures. It should also identify audit requests, reporting deadlines, board or committee approval requirements, and any applicable special procedures. The annual reporting perimeter and the applicable accounting framework must be confirmed; an NGO must not adopt the dates or processing arrangements of the illustrative United Nations financial system without checking its own requirements. The year-end overlay should specify additional tasks, owners, reviewers, evidence, deadlines, and approval gates, while retaining the controls already required for ordinary month-end closing. Any material unresolved difference should remain visible in the year-end status report. Final annual reporting should not be released solely because the final month's ordinary checklist has been completed.

Study instruction:
- "Build a year-end overlay on top of the ordinary monthly checklist and explain why each additional task is required."
Key point:
- Year-end closing includes both recurring close controls and additional annual reporting requirements.
- Annual obligations depend on the actual entity, framework, agreements, and legal reporting calendar.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Year-End Closing Procedures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Annual Financial Statements and Disclosures
27. Q: How should events occurring after the reporting date be assessed at year-end?
A: Show answer
The year-end process should identify relevant events occurring between the reporting date and the date the financial statements are authorized for issue, or the equivalent date established by the applicable framework. The review should distinguish events that provide additional evidence about conditions existing at the reporting date from events that indicate conditions arising afterward. Under the applicable reporting requirements, adjusting events may require changes to recognized amounts, while material non-adjusting events may require disclosure rather than adjustment. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Chapter 15 addresses events after the reporting date. Finance should coordinate information requests with legal, HR, procurement, grants, treasury, and operational owners to identify claims, settlements, major losses, funding changes, asset damage, and other potentially material matters. The date, facts, assessment, supporting documents, accounting conclusion, reviewer, and resulting journal or disclosure should be recorded. An event should not be classified solely by the date it was discovered or paid; its relationship to the reporting-date conditions is critical.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate two subsequent-event scenarios and document whether each affects reporting-date measurements, disclosure, or neither under the applicable framework."
Key point:
- Subsequent-event accounting depends on the conditions indicated by the event and the applicable reporting rules.
- The year-end evidence trail must show the date, analysis, conclusion, and authorization.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Events after the Reporting Date
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Disclosure Requirements
28. Q: How should year-end financial-statement disclosures and audit schedules be prepared?
A: Show answer
Disclosure preparation should be planned as a controlled reporting activity, not postponed until the trial balance is finalized. Finance should identify applicable disclosure requirements, assign owners, define the required information, establish evidence sources, and track completion dates. Supporting schedules may be needed for assets, liabilities, employee benefits, provisions, contingencies, commitments, grant balances, related parties, foreign-currency exposure, and other areas required by the selected framework. The team should reconcile disclosure amounts to the final trial balance and relevant supporting records, ensuring that narrative explanations agree with the underlying facts. Where estimates, uncertainty, or policy judgments are material, the basis and approval should be retained. Auditors may request supporting files, but a completed audit request list does not establish that an accounting issue has been resolved or that the financial statements are correct. The entity must distinguish its own preparation and approval responsibilities from an external auditor's independent work. Disclosure completeness should be reviewed before financial statements are authorized for release.

Study instruction:
- "Create a disclosure checklist linked to ledger accounts, source schedules, supporting evidence, preparers, reviewers, and final financial-statement references."
Key point:
- Financial-statement disclosures require evidence, accurate tie-outs, and review under the applicable framework.
- Audit readiness is not equivalent to an audit opinion or authorization to issue financial statements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provisions, Contingencies and Subsequent Events
29. Q: What evidence should be included in a close-certification package?
A: Show answer
The certification package should contain the approved close calendar and reporting perimeter, source-completeness confirmations, material cut-off decisions, closing schedules, journal requests and actual posting statuses, balance-sheet reconciliations, analytical reviews, specialist clearances, unresolved-exception register, and final reporting tie-outs. It should identify the version and timestamp of each major extract or workpaper so the approver knows exactly what has been reviewed. The approver should assess the nature and materiality of open items, the evidence supporting their disposition, the authority of the person signing, and the implications for financial statements and external reports. Certification should state the entity, period, reporting scope, evidence version, approver, approval date, and any permitted limitations. If a material matter is still on hold, the package should show the reason and blocked activities rather than presenting a misleading completion status. An approval record proves a decision was made; it does not itself prove that all underlying accounting work is correct.

Study instruction:
- "Assemble a sample close-certification pack and verify that each major closing assertion links to an independent source, reconciliation, review, or authorized disposition."
Key point:
- Certification must be based on a defined evidence package and delegated authority.
- A completion percentage or signed checklist cannot substitute for required accounting and control evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Closing and Reporting Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
30. Q: Under what conditions may a financial period be locked after closing?
A: Show answer
Period locking should occur only after the entity's required close gates have been satisfied and authorized approval has been obtained. The prerequisites normally include source-completeness review, material accounting adjustments, relevant reconciliations, financial-reporting review, specialist clearance, exception disposition, and required certification. The actual requirements depend on the authorized policy and system design. The operator should execute the lock within the approved scope, preserve the system event or transaction identifier, record who performed it and when, and retain evidence of the resulting period status. An independent reviewer should verify the actual locked state rather than assume that an instruction or successful system message proves the period is locked everywhere required. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes period-control procedures in its own financial systems; an NGO must configure and test its own system-specific process. A lock should never be used to conceal incomplete work or prevent legitimate review of unresolved accounting issues.

Study instruction:
- "Design a period-lock checklist and test that every prerequisite is cleared before execution and independently verified afterward."
Key point:
- Period locking is a controlled system action that follows authorization and close certification.
- Approval to lock and proof of actual lock are separate evidence requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Closing the Posting Period
31. Q: How should post-close adjustments and requests to reopen a locked period be handled?
A: Show answer
A reopening request should identify the error or new information, affected entity and period, accounts and reports involved, amount and materiality assessment, reason the correction cannot be handled through another approved route, and potential donor, tax, statutory, or management-reporting consequences. The requester must provide source evidence and a proposed corrective action. An authorized approver independent of the requester should assess the request and approve or reject it within the documented delegation. Only the approved scope should be reopened by the authorized system operator, with the original close snapshot and audit history preserved. Any necessary journal must follow normal preparation, review, approval, and posting controls. The affected schedules, reconciliations, trial-balance review, disclosures, and external reports must then be reassessed. Finance should obtain fresh certification where the correction affects prior approval and relock the period after the required checks are complete. The original version must not be overwritten or silently backdated.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a material error discovered after period lock and document the approval, affected-report assessment, corrective posting, recertification, and relock evidence."
Key point:
- Reopening must be authorized, scoped, traceable, and independently verified.
- Corrections require assessment of downstream reporting effects, not just the original journal.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Errors and Changes in Accounting Estimates
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Journal and Posting Controls
32. Q: How should close exceptions be prioritized and escalated?
A: Show answer
Each exception should record its type, affected period, amount and currency where known, risk, root-cause hypothesis, responsible owner, due date, required approval, and evidence needed for closure. Priority should consider not only monetary size but also legal deadlines, donor restrictions, potential fraud, reporting consequences, recurrence, age, and impact on other close tasks. Missing evidence for a material liability or an unresolved specialist determination may prevent certification even when the monetary amount is not yet known. Automated or manual status labels should reflect documented criteria such as open, under review, correction required, pending approval, verified, or closed. Escalation should identify the recipient, date, required decision, and consequences of delay. An item must not disappear from the register when transferred to another period or owner. It may be closed only when the approved resolution and independent review evidence exist, or when an authorized disposition explicitly permitted by policy has been completed.

Study instruction:
- "Build an exception register and rank sample issues by financial, compliance, donor, fraud, and reporting risk rather than amount alone."
Key point:
- An exception is a controlled issue requiring ownership, a deadline, evidence, and an explicit disposition.
- Moving a problem to the next period does not constitute resolution.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation, Adjustment, and Closing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Errors and Corrections
33. Q: How should recurring closing errors be analyzed to prevent them from happening again?
A: Show answer
The finance team should distinguish the immediate error from its underlying cause. A missing invoice, for example, may be the direct cause of an incorrect accrual, but the root cause might be a poor receiving process, unclear submission deadline, incomplete supplier register, or lack of escalation. The issue log should capture what happened, when it happened, the affected balances and reports, why existing controls failed, the required correction, the preventive or detective control change, its owner, and the target date. Corrective action may involve process redesign, clearer responsibilities, better source-data certification, new validation rules, improved system access, staff training, or revised close-calendar dependencies. The responsible reviewer should test that the action addresses the original cause and confirm whether similar errors recur in later periods. A training reminder alone may be inadequate where the root cause is a system deficiency or unclear authorization rule. Lessons learned should be incorporated into controlled SOP revisions and communicated to affected teams.

Study instruction:
- "Select a recurring close error, use a root-cause analysis to identify the control failure, and design a corrective action with an effectiveness test."
Key point:
- Correcting the accounting result does not necessarily correct the underlying process weakness.
- Preventive actions must be assigned, implemented, and tested for effectiveness.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
34. Q: How should audit trails and financial-close evidence be maintained?
A: Show answer
Evidence should show what information was used, what action occurred, who performed and reviewed it, when it happened, what decision was made, and how the outcome affected the accounts or reporting. Records should link source extracts, calculations, journal requests, approvals, posting confirmations, reconciliation versions, exception decisions, final reports, and lock or reopening events. The organization should maintain a consistent naming convention, controlled folders, access permissions, retention rules, and a documented approach to preserving superseded versions. Evidence must remain readable and retrievable for the period required by applicable law, donor agreements, contracts, audit needs, and organizational policy. A file's presence in an archive does not by itself prove authenticity or approval; the relevant status and history must also be identifiable. Financial evidence should not expose passwords, authentication tokens, or unnecessary personal data. Backup and restoration controls should be tested to ensure records remain available when needed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a material adjustment from its original supporting document to its final ledger posting, approval, reconciliation, reporting output, and archived evidence."
Key point:
- Audit trails should connect actions, decisions, evidence, and accounting outcomes.
- Retention, access, privacy, and restoration must be governed by approved requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Documentation and Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
35. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, and VBA improve financial-close automation without replacing accounting judgment?
A: Show answer
Power Query can import data from approved source files, standardize column names and formats, combine data from different systems, and flag potential duplicates, missing codes, inconsistent periods, or unusual values. Excel formulas and structured tables can support calculations, roll-forwards, reconciliations, variance analysis, and controlled schedules. VBA can automate repetitive tasks such as generating close packs, refreshing approved inputs, recording workflow statuses, exporting reports, or producing exception listings. These capabilities should operate within a documented process with approved accounting rules, clear data ownership, independent validation, and controlled outputs. Software does not determine whether an expense belongs in a period, whether a liability meets recognition criteria, or whether a donor cost is eligible. Those conclusions depend on the applicable accounting framework, agreements, law, and authorized organizational policy. Changes to formulas, mappings, queries, or macros should be versioned, tested using representative cases, approved, and logged. Automated output must be reconciled with actual system records before it is used for certification or posting.

Study instruction:
- "Automate one reconciliation using standardized source tables, then test omitted rows, duplicate records, invalid mappings, changed source formats, and incorrect period dates."
Key point:
- Automation can improve consistency and exception visibility, but cannot establish accounting validity on its own.
- Query logic, formulas, mappings, and output versions require controlled testing and review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Accounting and General-Ledger Controls
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
36. Q: What data-quality controls should be applied when combining close files from multiple systems?
A: Show answer
Before combining data, finance should confirm that each source has a known owner, reporting period, intended population, standard field structure, currency, and approved version. Transformations should standardize formats and field names, but must not silently remove source records or alter values in ways that affect accounting. Control totals and record counts should be captured before transformation and compared with the processed output. Duplicate detection should use appropriate combinations of transaction references, supplier or employee identifiers, dates, amounts, currencies, and document types rather than a single reference field alone. Missing project codes, invalid accounts, incompatible dates, unexpected blank fields, and inconsistent currencies should be reported as exceptions. Legitimate transactions should not be deleted simply because they resemble another record. Any record exclusions or transformations that affect the population should have documented rules and an audit trail. The resulting data should be reconciled to source and ledger evidence before calculation or certification.

Study instruction:
- "Create a repeatable data-quality check with pre- and post-transformation control totals and verify that known invalid and duplicate records are flagged for review."
Key point:
- Data transformation should preserve traceability and explain every material change in the population.
- Potential duplicates and invalid classifications require verification rather than automatic removal.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Data and Reconciliation Controls
- Microsoft Learn — Power Query Data Transformation Interface
37. Q: How should close dashboards and key performance indicators be designed for management review?
A: Show answer
A close dashboard should present the status of material activities and risks, such as source completeness, outstanding journals, unreconciled balances, aged exceptions, overdue dependencies, specialist holds, approval status, and evidence of period locking. Each indicator needs a precise definition, source, owner, reporting timestamp, calculation method, threshold, and escalation route. For example, the proportion of tasks completed should use a clearly defined task population and must not imply that every completed task passed the required accounting review. An unresolved material item may be more significant than dozens of minor administrative tasks. The dashboard should distinguish planned dates from actual completion dates and approved status from system-submission status. Finance should reconcile key dashboard measures to their underlying registers and ensure that manual overrides are documented. Management should use the dashboard to make decisions and prioritize remediation, while retaining detailed workpapers and independent review for material accounting matters.

Study instruction:
- "Design a dashboard with measurable definitions for completeness, timeliness, reconciliation quality, and exception aging, then validate each indicator against its source register."
Key point:
- Dashboard status is only reliable when definitions, source data, and evidence are controlled.
- High completion percentages do not establish that material accounting risks have been resolved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
38. Q: How should bank, cash, and treasury reconciliations be incorporated into the close?
A: Show answer
The close team should verify that bank and cash records cover the relevant accounts and period, reconcile the bank statements to the books, and investigate outstanding transfers, unpresented payments, unidentified receipts, bank charges, foreign-currency balances, and other reconciling items. The reconciliation must distinguish valid timing differences from errors, unauthorized transactions, duplicate entries, and items requiring follow-up with the bank or another office. Evidence should identify the statement version, account, currency, closing date, ledger balance, adjusted statement balance, reconciling items, preparer, reviewer, and resolution status. For multi-office NGOs, interoffice transfers may require matching entries and settlement evidence across offices. A bank payment marked successful does not independently establish that the related accounting entry is correctly classified or that a liability is fully settled. Finance must also verify any exchange-rate or treasury data used in period-end valuation under the applicable policy.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a bank reconciliation, trace outstanding items to subsequent clearing evidence, and investigate a transfer recorded differently by two offices."
Key point:
- Bank reconciliation must prove the relationship between external bank records and internal cash records.
- Unidentified or aged items require assigned follow-up, not unsupported adjustment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Reconciliation and General-Ledger Controls
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Official Accounting Guidance
39. Q: How should interoffice balances and related-party transactions be reviewed at closing?
A: Show answer
An NGO operating through multiple offices may have transfers, recharges, shared supplier payments, interoffice receivables, interoffice payables, and other balances that need consistent identification and settlement tracking. The close team should match counterpart records by entity or office, transaction reference, date, currency, amount, and underlying purpose. A difference may reflect timing, exchange rates, omitted entries, incorrect account mapping, or a transfer that one office treated incorrectly. Finance should identify the responsible counterpart, obtain supporting documents, explain material differences, and reconcile balances to the relevant ledgers. Internal transfers should not automatically be classified as external income, and payments made for another office should not automatically be charged to the paying office's own project. The correct accounting presentation depends on the reporting entity, applicable consolidation rules, and organizational policy. Related-party disclosures or other reporting requirements should be assessed under the actual framework where relevant.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile an interoffice balance from both counterpart records and document the treatment of a transfer, a shared supplier payment, and an unsettled difference."
Key point:
- Counterpart agreement is necessary to establish the completeness and accuracy of interoffice balances.
- Classification and consolidation treatment depend on the reporting entity and applicable accounting policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable and Financial Instruments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Reconciliation
40. Q: How should opening balances and brought-forward amounts be validated at the start of a new reporting period?
A: Show answer
Opening balances should be linked to the prior period's approved closing records and the actual system carry-forward process. Finance should verify the relevant entity, ledger, currency, accounts, control totals, and applicable opening-balance adjustments. It should investigate unexplained differences, changes in account mapping, missing balances, unexpected carry-forwards, and records that were posted after the prior period was approved. The next period's workpapers should identify whether prior accruals have been reversed, whether remaining balances continue to be valid, and whether previous reconciling items have been settled or formally carried forward. Carrying forward a balance does not establish its validity; the balance must remain supported and subject to the required review. Changes in accounting policy, errors, or estimates may require special treatment under the applicable framework rather than a routine opening entry. The opening trial balance and relevant subledger totals should be documented and reviewed before subsequent closing calculations rely on them.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the prior approved closing trial balance with the next period's opening balances and investigate differences and unresolved brought-forward items."
Key point:
- Opening balances inherit the need for evidence and reconciliation from the prior reporting period.
- A balance carried forward without review can perpetuate an earlier error.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger and Period-End Procedures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
41. Q: What should happen when the ledger, subledger, asset register, or source schedule does not agree?
A: Show answer
The first step is to confirm that both records represent the same entity, period, population, currency, status, and accounting scope. Finance should compare control totals and transaction populations, identify omitted or duplicated items, inspect mapping and timing differences, and review any recent corrections or source-system changes. The owner should establish whether the difference results from a legitimate timing issue, an unsupported source record, a missing journal, an unauthorized parameter change, a failed interface, or an accounting error. If a correction is required, it should be processed at the appropriate source or journal level under the authorized procedure. The reviewer should confirm that the correction resolves the cause and that the affected reports reconcile afterward. The team should not simply overwrite the subledger, alter the general ledger, or book a balancing amount to achieve agreement. Material unresolved differences must remain open and be escalated according to the close policy.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a deliberate subledger-to-ledger mismatch and document the source population, root cause, approved correction, and independent retest."
Key point:
- Reconciliation differences must be explained at the transaction and process level.
- Corrections must resolve the underlying cause without destroying original evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger Reconciliation and Adjustments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Errors and Corrective Accounting
42. Q: How should materiality and risk influence close review and escalation?
A: Show answer
Materiality and risk help determine the depth of review, order of investigation, and level of escalation, but they do not authorize the omission of mandatory procedures. The organization should establish the applicable materiality approach and investigation thresholds under its authorized policies and reporting requirements. Reviewers should consider monetary value alongside qualitative factors such as fraud indicators, donor restrictions, statutory obligations, management sensitivity, related-party concerns, repeated errors, and the risk of misleading external reports. An issue of comparatively small value may still require urgent action if it affects legal compliance or signals a control failure. The close coordinator should record the basis of the assessment, the people who made the decision, the affected financial statements or reports, and the required corrective action. Any permitted decision not to adjust an amount should be supported under the applicable framework and delegated authority. The team should not import materiality thresholds from a fictional example or an unrelated organization without formal approval.

Study instruction:
- "Rank a sample of closing issues by monetary materiality and qualitative risk, then justify the review depth and escalation route for each."
Key point:
- Materiality informs review decisions but does not override mandatory requirements or authorized controls.
- Thresholds and dispositions must be evidence-based, approved, and appropriate to the entity.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Estimates and Errors
43. Q: How should changes in accounting policies, estimates, or previously recorded errors be reflected in the close?
A: Show answer
Finance must distinguish a change in accounting policy from a change in estimate and the correction of an error. These matters can have different recognition, measurement, presentation, and disclosure consequences under the applicable framework. For each issue, the team should document the facts, reason for change or correction, relevant accounting authority, applicable period, calculation method, affected balances, approval, and potential impact on previously issued or distributed reports. A new estimate should not automatically be treated as an error simply because it differs from an earlier reasonable estimate; conversely, a mistake caused by incorrect data or an inappropriate method should not be relabelled as an estimate change to avoid correction requirements. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Chapter 17 provides guidance on accounting policies, changes in estimates, and errors. The entity must verify which provisions apply to its reporting framework and whether retrospective adjustments, comparative disclosures, or other actions are required.

Study instruction:
- "Classify three scenarios as a policy change, an estimate change, or an error, then determine the evidence and review needed for each."
Key point:
- Different types of accounting changes have different consequences under the applicable framework.
- The rationale, timing, approval, and reporting impact must be documented before the close is finalized.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
44. Q: How should a financial-close process be tested before a new SOP or automated workflow is adopted?
A: Show answer
Testing should demonstrate that the process works under normal conditions and handles realistic exceptions without silently bypassing controls. The team should define acceptance criteria for source completeness, calculations, cut-off, journal approval, reconciliation, reporting tie-outs, audit-trail creation, and period-lock authorization. Test cases should include missing source files, duplicate transactions, incorrect periods, unapproved accounting parameters, foreign-exchange mismatches, incomplete reconciliations, failed journal submissions, unauthorized approvals, and reopening requests. Expected results should be documented before testing and compared with actual outcomes. Synthetic calculations should be independently recomputed, while workflow states and document references should be checked against the intended controls. Technical validation of a Word file or an automated model does not prove the accounting framework has been correctly applied or that an organization's staff has completed the required approval. Adoption should require both technical acceptance and authorized organizational approval, with unresolved issues documented as holds rather than assumed to have passed.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a test register covering normal processing and negative scenarios, record expected and actual results, and define which failed tests block adoption."
Key point:
- Acceptance testing should prove that controls work, including when inputs, approvals, or system actions fail.
- Technical integrity, accounting validity, and organizational adoption are separate acceptance gates.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Closing Procedures and General-Ledger Controls
45. Q: How should financial-close procedures be maintained when staff change or a key person becomes unavailable?
A: Show answer
The close process should be documented so that its critical steps, evidence, control logic, and approval requirements do not depend on one individual's memory. Each task should identify the process owner, backup person, relevant systems, source files, approved calculation or procedure, escalation route, and required output. Handover records should explain open items, pending approvals, upcoming deadlines, recurring problems, and locations of controlled evidence. Substitute personnel must receive appropriate training and access through the authorized access-management process; credentials should never be shared. The close coordinator should confirm that deputies understand the duties and limits of their authority. Periodic walkthroughs and practical exercises help identify instructions that are incomplete or inconsistent with actual operations. Changes in staff or system roles should trigger a review of access, delegations, and workflow assignments. Procedures should be updated through version-controlled approval, not through uncontrolled edits to the only copy.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a handover pack for a close coordinator and test whether a trained substitute can identify the outstanding tasks, evidence, approvals, and escalation requirements."
Key point:
- Documentation and cross-training support continuity without weakening authorization controls.
- Staff substitutions require appropriate access and documented delegated authority.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General-Ledger and Period-End Procedures
46. Q: How should management decide whether an organization is ready to adopt a proposed month-end and year-end closing SOP?
A: Show answer
Adoption readiness requires more than a polished document or a successful demonstration using fictional data. Management should confirm that the correct legal entity and reporting framework have been established, the process has been mapped against actual systems and responsibilities, the calendar uses verified deadlines, accounting policies are approved, source owners and reviewers are assigned, and the required controls are operational. Representative walkthroughs should show how real evidence flows from source data to calculations, postings, reconciliations, reports, and approval. Open issues affecting law, accounting treatment, donor eligibility, tax status, system access, or material financial balances should be formally resolved or retained as explicit adoption holds. Technical validation should confirm that templates and workflow identifiers work as intended, while operational testing should establish that users can execute the process consistently. The final approval record should identify the authorized approver, document version, effective date, scope, unresolved limitations, and review date. The fictional SOP provided as the source must remain illustrative unless the actual organization independently validates, adapts, approves, and adopts it.

Study instruction:
- "Complete an adoption-readiness assessment covering framework applicability, operational walkthroughs, control testing, evidence quality, authorization, and remaining holds."
Key point:
- A proposed SOP becomes operational only after entity-specific validation and authorized adoption.
- Fictional examples, successful arithmetic, and technically valid documents do not prove live organizational compliance.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Official Accounting Guidance
