Table 6
Balance Sheet Reconciliation Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose and intended use of a balance sheet reconciliation SOP?
A: Show answer
A balance sheet reconciliation Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) establishes a consistent process for proving that recorded asset, liability, and other balance-sheet accounts are complete, accurate, supported, independently reviewed, and resolved or transparently carried forward. It defines the account population, source evidence, reconciliation method, preparation responsibilities, review requirements, reconciling-item aging, escalation, correction referrals, closure criteria, and document retention. The supplied document, SOP 006, is explicitly a fictional training example and has not been adopted by a real organization. Its sample entities, balances, approvals, account references, test results, and events cannot be used as evidence of actual balances or approval authority. An implementing NGO must verify its legal identity, accounting framework, policies, source systems, delegated authority, and applicable requirements before approving the procedure. The SOP governs the reconciliation control process; it does not independently authorize journals, write-offs, payments, or changes to source records.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the purpose, scope, ownership, and adoption limitations of a reconciliation SOP, distinguishing a training example from an approved operational policy."
Key point:
- Every applicable balance must be supported, independently reviewed, and traceable to its underlying records.
- The supplied SOP is a fictional template, not evidence of real organizational activity or approval.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
2. Q: Why is reconciliation necessary even when the general ledger balance appears correct?
A: Show answer
An apparently correct balance may contain offsetting errors, duplicate transactions, omitted activity, incorrect classifications, unsupported journals, incomplete source populations, or errors that happen to produce a zero difference. Reconciliation therefore requires more than comparing two totals. The preparer must establish that the sources are complete, the accounting period and reporting perimeter agree, the selected method is appropriate, and the balance can be independently reproduced from the underlying evidence. Unusual movements, stale items, contradictory documents, and suspicious transactions also require investigation. The reviewer must challenge whether the apparent agreement reflects the underlying economic events rather than an accidental mathematical result. For NGO reporting, this protects the reliability of project balances, donor-funded resources, liabilities, advances, and other financial statement amounts. A zero difference is a useful result, but it is not sufficient evidence that the account is correct.

Study instruction:
- "Construct an example in which two incorrect balances agree and identify the independent evidence needed to detect the underlying errors."
Key point:
- Mathematical agreement does not establish completeness, validity, or correct accounting treatment.
- Independent evidence and risk-based review are essential to reliable reconciliation.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
3. Q: What document-control information must be verified before the SOP can become effective?
A: Show answer
The controlled document record should identify the legal entity, registration details where relevant, SOP number, version, policy owner, independent reviewer, authorized approver, applicable accounting framework, effective date, next review date, approval reference, release version, and authoritative storage location. Each field must be completed from approved evidence rather than copied from an example. The document must distinguish a draft, a document under review, an approved policy, and a superseded version. An approval reference must be traceable to an actual decision by an authorized person or governing body. The effective date must not be treated as operational merely because a date has been entered. Changes must be recorded with their reasons, owners, review and approval evidence, retesting results, and archive references. Until the applicable authority and adoption gates are satisfied, the procedure must remain a controlled draft.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a document-control register and identify which fields require independent evidence before the SOP can be issued."
Key point:
- Version numbers and approval references are not substitutes for valid authorization.
- The effective status must agree with the actual approval record.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
4. Q: How should the applicable accounting framework and governing requirements be established?
A: Show answer
Begin by verifying which accounting framework, statutory requirements, organizational policies, donor agreements, contractual provisions, and reporting instructions apply to the actual entity. Use those requirements to determine accounting recognition, measurement, classification, presentation, disclosure, and reporting obligations. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual is a valuable reference for reconciliation workflows and UN accounting-system practices, but its entity-specific procedures must not automatically be treated as mandatory policy for every NGO. Where the organization uses IPSAS, identify the relevant standards and implementation guidance; where it uses another framework, apply the appropriate authoritative requirements. Document source applicability separately from source existence: locating a guidance document does not prove that it governs the organization. Assign an owner to resolve uncertainties, obtain technical review where necessary, and maintain an authoritative register of the decisions and parameters used by the SOP.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an authority register linking each important reconciliation rule to its applicable accounting framework, organizational policy, contract, or regulatory requirement."
Key point:
- Applicability must be established before guidance is used as a binding rule.
- UN implementation guidance should be distinguished from requirements governing the actual NGO.
Official links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual
5. Q: How should responsibilities, competence, and decision-making authority be assigned?
A: Show answer
Define separate responsibilities for reconciliation preparation, source-data provision, technical accounting advice, independent review, approval, correction processing, escalation, system administration, and record retention. Each role should have explicit responsibilities, authority limits, required competence, a reporting line, and an appropriate substitute when the assigned person is unavailable. The preparer is responsible for assembling the evidence, performing the reconciliation, explaining differences, and recording unresolved items. The reviewer must independently challenge the evidence, calculations, classification, and conclusions. The approver authorizes decisions within the approved delegation, while the relevant source-process owner carries out corrections in their own system. A consultant may provide analysis or assistance but does not automatically replace the organization's authorized decision-maker. The responsibility matrix should be checked against actual user permissions and current delegations, not merely against job titles.

Study instruction:
- "Create a responsibility matrix for preparation, review, approval, correction, escalation, and retention, then compare it with actual system permissions."
Key point:
- Each control activity needs a clearly accountable and appropriately authorized owner.
- Responsibility descriptions and actual permissions must be consistent.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes
6. Q: How can the organization prove that the account population selected for reconciliation is complete?
A: Show answer
Build the reconciliation population from the approved chart of accounts, general ledger account balances and activity, relevant subledger populations, prior-period account roster, and records of new, reactivated, suspended, or closed accounts. Consider all accounts that remain in scope because of activity, reporting requirements, control significance, or a continuing balance, including accounts with zero ending balances but relevant transactions during the period. Compare the population across these independent or differently generated records and explain omissions, additions, code changes, and scope differences. Each account should have an assigned owner, reviewer, reconciliation method, required source evidence, reporting frequency, and risk classification. An account should not be removed from the population simply because it is difficult to reconcile, overdue, dormant, or temporarily inactive. Completeness must be supported by actual entity data; a successful test against a fictional or sample account list cannot certify the real population.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the approved account master to the GL, prior-period roster, and relevant subledgers, documenting every new, missing, reactivated, or closed account."
Key point:
- The reconciliation population must be independently established and explainable.
- Sample data cannot prove that the actual organization's population is complete.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Subsidiary Ledgers
7. Q: How should a reconciliation method be selected for each account?
A: Show answer
Select a method that reflects the nature of the account, how transactions arise, and what independent evidence is available. A bank account normally requires a bank-statement bridge; a control account supported by a subledger requires comparison with the matching subledger population; prepayments and fixed assets may need a schedule roll-forward; high-volume clearing accounts may require transaction-level matching and aging; and reciprocal interoffice balances may require bilateral confirmation. Physical or quantity-controlled assets also require evidence of existence and valuation, not just arithmetic agreement. Some accounts require a composite method because more than one source population contributes to the balance. Record the method, equation, expected evidence, source owner, matching keys, currency, sign convention, and relevant review tests in the reconciliation register. Changing the method must be justified and reviewed because it can alter what errors the procedure detects.

Study instruction:
- "Assign an appropriate reconciliation method to bank, receivable, accrual, advance, fixed-asset, inventory, and reciprocal balance examples."
Key point:
- A method must fit the account's underlying activity and available evidence.
- One universal reconciliation formula cannot provide adequate control for every account class.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Reconciliation
8. Q: What evidence proves that source reports are complete, reliable, and suitable for reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Record the source-system name, report identifier, report parameters, legal entity, reporting period, currency, extraction date and time, extraction user, run or batch reference, row count, control total, interface status, and evidence location. Where available, retain report hashes or other identifiers that support integrity and reproducibility. Verify that the source population includes the required accounts, transactions, statuses, and dates, and investigate incomplete interfaces, rejected records, unexpected row-count changes, duplicate reports, and missing periods. A report generated from the same GL being reconciled is not necessarily independent evidence of the underlying transaction or external balance. For example, a ledger cash figure alone does not replace a bank statement. If critical source evidence is missing, inconsistent, or not independently corroborated, place the reconciliation on hold or apply the approved exception route. Do not substitute assumed completeness for evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Select a source report and test its period, parameters, row count, control totals, interface status, and independence from the balance being reconciled."
Key point:
- Reliable reconciliation depends on complete and appropriately independent evidence.
- Missing source evidence must trigger a controlled response rather than an unsupported sign-off.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Data Processes
9. Q: How should the monthly reconciliation calendar be organized?
A: Show answer
Establish a controlled timetable covering report availability, account assignment, preparation, source-data follow-up, independent review, correction referrals, resubmission, approval, close-readiness assessment, and archival completion. Each step needs an owner, due date, dependency, expected deliverable, evidence requirement, and escalation route. Schedule the work to leave enough time for investigation and correction before the financial close deadline. Carry-forward items must remain visible and retain their original age and action history; a new reporting period does not automatically make an old issue new. Late source reports and unavailable reviewers should activate predetermined escalation and substitute arrangements. Dates, reporting frequency, tolerances, materiality thresholds, and escalation limits must be established from approved local policy rather than invented from this training example. The calendar should distinguish preparation completion from independent review and authorized close approval.

Study instruction:
- "Build a monthly calendar with dependencies, owners, deadlines, substitute arrangements, and escalation points for overdue reconciliations."
Key point:
- A reconciliation is not complete merely because preparation has finished.
- Deadlines and escalation thresholds require valid organizational approval.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Period-End Processes
10. Q: How is a bank reconciliation prepared and independently verified?
A: Show answer
Obtain the appropriate bank statement or independently confirmed bank balance for the correct account and reporting date. Compare it with the cash general ledger and identify deposits in transit, outstanding payments, bank charges, direct debits, interest, unidentified transactions, and other timing or recording differences. Use a consistent sign convention and document each reconciling item with its reference, amount, date, explanation, supporting evidence, original age, owner, and expected resolution. The bank-to-book bridge should reconcile to the adjusted book balance once separately authorized book corrections are reflected. A difference caused by an unrecorded bank charge is not the same as a valid timing difference; it may require a journal through the authorized transaction process. Investigate unexpected debits, duplicate payments, restricted-cash discrepancies, and suspected fraud promptly. Review the statement balance, ledger balance, equation, open items, subsequent clearing evidence, and final conclusion independently.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a bank reconciliation using a statement and cashbook, then trace every reconciling item to evidence or an authorized correction."
Key point:
- Bank and ledger balances must be reconciled through documented timing items and verified corrections.
- Unidentified or suspicious transactions cannot be dismissed as ordinary timing differences.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
11. Q: How should a GL control account be reconciled to its subsidiary ledger?
A: Show answer
Extract the control account balance and the corresponding subledger population for the same entity, period, currency, accounting scope, and relevant account or dimension. Verify that the subledger total is complete and that the account mapping is correct. Calculate the difference using a documented sign convention, then investigate unposted interfaces, manual journals, duplicate items, timing differences, incorrect account assignments, and transactions missing from one side. Some integrated systems restrict direct postings to reconciliation accounts because subledger activity is expected to update the GL automatically. Where such a restriction applies, follow the actual system configuration and approved accounting procedure rather than posting directly to force agreement. Record legitimate differences separately and correct errors through the authorized process. The reconciliation should show the control balance, detailed population total, differences, explanations, referrals, and independently verified final status.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an accounts-payable or accounts-receivable subledger total with its GL control account and investigate each unmatched amount."
Key point:
- The GL control balance should be supported by the complete, correctly mapped subsidiary population.
- System-integrated accounts may require corrections through the originating subledger rather than direct GL posting.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation Accounts and Subsidiary Ledgers
12. Q: How should a schedule roll-forward substantiate an account balance?
A: Show answer
A roll-forward explains the movement from the opening supported balance to the closing supported balance. Its basic structure is opening balance plus additions or charges, less settlements, releases, disposals, write-offs, and other applicable reductions, adjusted for authorized transfers and other relevant movements. Each component must be traced to the correct source population and accounting period. Examples include staff advances, partner advances, prepayments, accrued liabilities, fixed assets, and other schedule-based accounts. Verify the opening amount against the previously approved reconciliation or an authorized restatement. Check additions against transaction evidence, reductions against settlements or approved adjustments, and closing balances against the detailed register and GL. Investigate missing movements, duplicated additions, unexplained transfers, and unsupported opening amounts. Recognition and measurement must still follow the applicable accounting policy; a mathematically balanced roll-forward does not independently establish that the balance is correctly classified or valued.

Study instruction:
- "Build a roll-forward for one advance or prepayment account and tie each movement and the closing balance to supporting records."
Key point:
- Every closing balance should be explainable through a supported sequence of movements.
- A balanced schedule must also comply with the applicable accounting policy.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
13. Q: When is transaction-by-transaction matching more appropriate than comparing summary totals?
A: Show answer
Transaction-level matching is useful for high-volume clearing accounts, bank activity, settlements, and other populations in which individual transactions should correspond to identifiable source records. Define reliable matching keys such as transaction reference, source document, counterparty, currency, amount, date, and relevant account or project dimension. Matching only on amount or reference can produce false matches when different transactions share an amount or document number. Identify fully matched, partially matched, unmatched, duplicate-candidate, and ambiguous records separately. The matched and unmatched populations must together account for the complete source population, and the signed unmatched amounts must reconcile to the remaining difference. Do not automatically discard suspected duplicates or close partial matches without checking the underlying evidence. Document matching rules, thresholds, exceptions, and any approved manual overrides so the results are reproducible.

Study instruction:
- "Test a transaction-matching schedule containing duplicated references, equal amounts, partial matches, and unmatched records."
Key point:
- Matching keys should identify the transaction reliably, not merely produce a convenient tie.
- Full-population coverage and exception review are necessary to support the reconciliation result.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Transaction Matching
14. Q: How should reconciling items be aged and monitored until resolution?
A: Show answer
Maintain a uniquely identified record for every outstanding item, including account, originating transaction, amount and currency, discovery date, original age anchor, due date, reason, risk classification, owner, follow-up actions, evidence, and target resolution date. Distinguish the transaction date, accounting date, statement date, discovery date, and correction date because they serve different purposes. Calculate age from the approved original anchor and an explicitly identified reporting date. Carry-forward, reassignment, partial settlement, extension, and reopening must not reset the original age or erase prior explanations. Update the remaining exposure as supported settlements occur, while retaining the transaction history. Apply approved aging and escalation rules, considering both elapsed time and the underlying risk. A recently discovered item may still be serious, while an old item cannot be treated as harmless solely because it has been carried forward repeatedly.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an aging register and test that reassignment, partial settlement, carry-forward, and reopening preserve the original item history and age."
Key point:
- Original age and open exposure must remain visible throughout the item's life cycle.
- Extensions and reassignment do not replace evidence of actual resolution.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable and Receivable Assessment
15. Q: What controls are needed for foreign-currency reconciliation and revaluation?
A: Show answer
Preserve the original currency and amount, the functional or reporting currency, the exchange rate, the rate's source and date, its direction, the calculation, the rounding convention, and the accounting period. Distinguish the original transaction amount from subsequent currency retranslation or settlement effects. Reperform the calculation independently using the approved rate and method, and retain sufficient precision in intermediate calculations to avoid unexplained rounding differences. Investigate discrepancies caused by inconsistent rate direction, incorrect dates, incorrect currency codes, missing exchange differences, or unintended conversion of an already translated amount. The applicable accounting policy must determine the relevant rate and recognition of exchange differences. An illustrative calculation or a rate embedded in a spreadsheet is not evidence that the rate or accounting treatment is authorized. Any parameter change requires documented ownership, review, testing, and effective-date control.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a foreign-currency balance using the approved rate source, rate direction, rounding convention, and original currency amount."
Key point:
- FX reconciliation requires transparent rate evidence and reproducible calculations.
- The selected rate and accounting treatment must follow the applicable policy.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Processes
16. Q: How should period-end cut-off and opening-balance continuity be controlled?
A: Show answer
Confirm that the reconciliation covers the intended entity and period, uses the correct report dates, and includes transactions that belong in the reporting period under the applicable accounting rules. Review late invoices, subsequent receipts and payments, reversals, unprocessed interfaces, transfers near period-end, and post-close entries that may change the balance or the explanation of an outstanding item. Tie the opening balance to the prior approved close or an authorized restatement and document any difference. Distinguish the accounting date from the transaction, settlement, or evidence-receipt date. A transaction entered after closing may require investigation into the period to which it belongs; the posting date alone does not resolve the accounting question. If new information materially affects a previously approved reconciliation, assess whether re-review or reopening is required under the organization's close policy.

Study instruction:
- "Select transactions around month-end and determine whether their timing, evidence, and subsequent posting affect the correct reporting period."
Key point:
- Opening balances and cut-off decisions require documented continuity and supporting evidence.
- New information or material post-close changes may invalidate earlier approval.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
17. Q: What additional evidence is required for different classes of balance-sheet accounts?
A: Show answer
Evidence must reflect the underlying nature of each balance. Bank accounts need external statements and documented clearing items. Receivables and grant balances need detailed debtor or grant records and, where applicable, supporting agreements, invoices, settlements, and aging information. Payables and accruals need supplier or service evidence, invoice information, subsequent payments, and cut-off support. Staff and partner advances need issue records, approved purposes, expenditure reports, supporting receipts, repayments, and remaining-balance analysis. Prepayments need contractual coverage periods and a supported release schedule. Fixed assets need the asset register, acquisition or handover evidence, custody or existence checks, and valuation calculations. Inventory requires appropriate quantity, custody, movement, and valuation evidence. Payroll and tax balances require appropriate subledgers and supporting compliance records. Apply the actual entity's accounting and reporting framework to determine recognition, measurement, and disclosure. Evidence appropriate for one class may be insufficient for another.

Study instruction:
- "Create an evidence checklist for bank, receivable, payable, advance, prepayment, fixed-asset, and inventory reconciliations."
Key point:
- Evidence requirements must match the nature and risk of the balance.
- Arithmetic agreement cannot replace existence, completeness, valuation, or rights-and-obligations checks.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
18. Q: How should interoffice, interfund, interproject, and other reciprocal balances be reconciled?
A: Show answer
Compare the sending and receiving records by counterparty, entity, direction, currency, transaction reference, reporting period, and relevant accounting dimensions. Show receivable and payable balances gross by direction before assessing any permissible offset. Differences may arise from timing, inconsistent references, missing counterpart entries, incorrect coding, currency conversion, incomplete interfaces, or different accounting dates. Obtain confirmations and supporting transaction records from the counterpart process owner where appropriate. Assign each difference an owner, action, target date, and escalation route. Do not net unrelated balances, omit amounts because the other office has not confirmed them, or declare a balance settled merely because the two totals appear similar. Any offset or consolidation treatment must satisfy the applicable accounting requirements and organizational policy. Reconcile the individual counterpart relationships before incorporating them into the close or consolidation process.

Study instruction:
- "Compare reciprocal balances by counterparty and currency, then classify each difference as timing, missing entry, coding, exchange, or unresolved evidence."
Key point:
- Reciprocal balances require traceable bilateral comparison and explanation of differences.
- Netting must not conceal unsupported or mismatched positions.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Inter-Fund Reconciliation
19. Q: What must an independent reviewer do before accepting a reconciliation?
A: Show answer
The reviewer must assess source completeness, the appropriateness of the method, the correctness of the equation and signs, independent recalculation of selected amounts, support for reconciling items, aging, material or unusual movements, cut-off, prior-period carry-forwards, and resolution evidence. Review should challenge whether the source evidence is sufficiently independent and whether the explanation is consistent with the underlying events. For significant, unusual, overdue, or high-risk items, perform additional testing and seek corroboration rather than relying solely on preparer explanations. Record comments, required actions, responsible people, due dates, resubmission versions, and evidence of resolution. The reviewer must have appropriate competence and independence from preparation. A tick mark or signature without documented work is insufficient. Approval must refer to an identifiable version of the reconciliation and must be reconsidered if relevant amounts, sources, or conclusions change afterward.

Study instruction:
- "Perform an independent review of a sample reconciliation and document evidence inspected, recalculations performed, challenges raised, and closure of comments."
Key point:
- Review must include substantive challenge, not merely acknowledgement of preparation.
- Approval applies to the evidence and version actually reviewed.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Adjustment
20. Q: Why must preparation, review, approval, and correction processing be appropriately segregated?
A: Show answer
Separating incompatible responsibilities reduces the risk that errors, unsupported adjustments, or unauthorized transactions pass through without challenge. The preparer documents and proposes the reconciliation result; the reviewer independently tests it; the approver authorizes decisions within a valid delegation; and the designated source-process owner processes approved corrections. Actual access permissions must be examined to determine whether this separation operates in practice. If staffing constraints prevent full segregation, implement documented compensating controls, such as independent approval, direct evidence inspection, targeted post-processing review, and periodic access checks. Delegations must be current, scoped to the transaction or decision, and available for inspection. Expired or out-of-scope authority must not be accepted merely because the person holds a senior job title. The controls must also prevent the same person from solely preparing, approving, and verifying their own correction.

Study instruction:
- "Map actual users and permissions against the reconciliation workflow and identify self-approval opportunities or missing compensating controls."
Key point:
- Responsibilities must be separated where practical and supported by effective compensating controls where separation is limited.
- A job title does not by itself prove authority to approve or close an issue.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes
21. Q: What is the boundary between a reconciliation finding and an authorized accounting correction?
A: Show answer
A reconciliation identifies and explains a difference, but it does not automatically authorize the accounting entry needed to correct it. The preparer should record the discrepancy, investigate the underlying cause, assemble supporting evidence, and refer the proposed remedy to the authorized transaction or accounting process. The referral should include the account, transaction references, affected period, amount and currency, proposed treatment, supporting documents, required approval, and expected evidence of completion. Depending on the issue, the correction may need to be processed through accounts payable, accounts receivable, payroll, grants, assets, inventory, treasury, or a journal workflow. Approval of a proposed journal is not proof that it was posted. The reconciliation remains open until the actual posting or other remedy is verified in the relevant source record and GL and the resulting balance is independently retested.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one proposed journal referral through approval, posting, source-record update, GL reflection, and independent verification."
Key point:
- Identifying a difference and authorizing its correction are separate control steps.
- An approved journal or referral cannot close an item without evidence of the actual outcome.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Adjustments
22. Q: How should a correction be verified before a previously open reconciliation item can be closed?
A: Show answer
Confirm the correction reference, authorization, posting date, amount, currency, accounting period, accounts affected, and transaction status. Inspect the updated originating subledger or other source record and confirm that the corresponding accounting entry is reflected in the GL. Reperform the affected reconciliation calculation, confirm that the expected residual has been removed or correctly reduced, and investigate any new difference introduced by the correction. Retain the original issue record, supporting evidence, proposed and approved treatments, final posting evidence, verification, and independent closure approval. If a system timeout or partial failure occurred, first inspect the actual posting state and duplicate risk before retrying the correction. Never infer that a transaction failed simply because a response was missing, and never assume success from an approval message alone. Closure must reflect the verified final state, not the expected outcome.

Study instruction:
- "Use a completed correction case to prove the source record, GL entry, remaining difference, independent retest, and authorized closure."
Key point:
- Actual correction and independent verification are both required for closure.
- Duplicate retries and unverified postings can create additional accounting errors.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
23. Q: What conditions should block month-end or year-end close readiness?
A: Show answer
Close readiness should be assessed at the account and issue level using approved completion criteria, not inferred from an aggregate percentage of reconciliations completed. Missing mandatory source evidence, unsupported balances, unresolved material differences, suspected fraud, invalid authority, incorrect period coverage, failed critical controls, and unreviewed changes may require a hard stop. Some open timing items may be permitted under an approved policy when they are supported, risk-assessed, time-bound, assigned to an owner, and subject to appropriate review. The organization must define which states block close, which conditions allow conditional completion, and who can authorize those decisions. A dashboard cannot override a critical hold. Where an item remains open, the close pack should describe the amount, cause, risk, owner, due date, permitted status, and required follow-up. Reopening a closed period or previously approved reconciliation should follow documented authority and evidence requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample open items into hard-stop, permitted open-item, and resolved states using approved criteria, and document the effect on close readiness."
Key point:
- Close completion depends on evidence and risk, not simply the percentage of accounts reconciled.
- Any conditional open-item treatment must be formally permitted and documented.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Period-End Adjustments
24. Q: When may an account be classified as not applicable, dormant, or outside the monthly reconciliation cycle?
A: Show answer
Such a decision requires objective evidence and an approved basis, not simply the absence of a current balance or the difficulty of obtaining support. Review the account master, transaction activity, reporting obligations, continuing balances, and any applicable organizational policy for reconciliation frequency. Document the account or scope affected, source evidence, rationale, assessed risks, authorizing role, effective and review dates, and conditions that trigger reactivation. New activity, reactivated codes, reporting requirements, or changes in policy should prompt reassessment. An account must not be excluded to remove an overdue item, unsupported amount, or difficult control problem from the dashboard. If evidence or approval is missing, keep the status pending and follow the approved hold or escalation path. A not-applicable classification is itself a controlled decision that must remain reviewable.

Study instruction:
- "Examine a proposed dormant-account exemption and determine which activity records, approval evidence, review date, and reactivation triggers are required."
Key point:
- Not-applicable status needs evidence, authorization, and continued monitoring.
- Dormancy cannot be used to conceal overdue or unsupported balances.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
25. Q: How should exceptions, root causes, and corrective actions be managed?
A: Show answer
Maintain an exception register that records the issue, affected account and period, amount and currency where known, date identified, risk level, source evidence, suspected cause, accountable owner, due date, containment measures, escalation, corrective action, and closure evidence. Distinguish symptoms from root causes. A missing receipt may reflect a late submission, an unclear responsibility, a broken interface, an invalid workflow, or an inadequate source-data requirement. Corrective actions should address the underlying weakness through specific process changes, ownership clarification, training, source-control improvements, or system fixes as appropriate. High-risk items must be escalated according to approved criteria, and suspected fraud, tampering, or security incidents must use the designated protected reporting route rather than an ordinary reconciliation closure workflow. The register should preserve the sequence of actions and explanations even after the issue is resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a recurring reconciliation exception using documented evidence, identify the root cause, assign corrective action, and define an independent test of recurrence prevention."
Key point:
- An explanation is not sufficient unless the underlying issue is addressed or an authorized resolution is documented.
- Repeated exceptions may indicate a broader process or system-control weakness.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
26. Q: What information must the reconciliation audit trail retain?
A: Show answer
Retain a traceable history of the reconciliation source, preparation, review, approval, comments, changes, open items, referrals, actual corrections, retesting, and closure. Record who performed the action, when it occurred, what changed, why the change was made, the affected version, prior and revised values where relevant, supporting evidence, and the approval or decision reference. Preserve original explanations and ages rather than silently replacing them with later summaries. Source reports should be identifiable by period and extraction details, and approved reconciliations should be distinguishable from drafts. The audit trail must support both directions of investigation: from a reported balance to its transactions and from a source transaction to its accounting and reconciliation outcome. It should also allow a reviewer to reconstruct why an item was considered resolved or why an open balance was permitted at close.

Study instruction:
- "Select a closed reconciliation item and reconstruct its full history from initial identification through approval, correction, retesting, and final closure."
Key point:
- Historical explanations and decisions must remain retrievable and attributable.
- An audit trail should support reconstruction of both calculations and control decisions.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Processes
27. Q: How should evidence retention, privacy, access, and security be incorporated into reconciliation procedures?
A: Show answer
Define an approved retention schedule based on applicable law, organizational policy, donor or contractual obligations, and any legal or investigation hold. Before disposal, verify that retention has expired, no hold applies, and the authorized disposal process is satisfied. Restrict source reports and working files according to their sensitivity and operational purpose. Payroll, staff, banking, donor, and protected incident records should not be made generally accessible merely because they are needed for reconciliation. Access changes must be authorized, recorded, and reviewed periodically; passwords, authentication codes, and security secrets must never be placed in ordinary reconciliation workbooks. Backup and recovery arrangements should be tested to establish that required records can actually be restored. Suspected tampering, data breaches, or inappropriate access must be preserved as evidence and referred to the appropriate incident process.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect the reconciliation archive and access register, verify the retention authority, test a recovery procedure, and identify any sensitive information exposed unnecessarily."
Key point:
- Retention, disposal, privacy, and access must be controlled as part of the evidence lifecycle.
- A backup is useful only when required records can be recovered and verified.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes
28. Q: Which indicators help management evaluate the effectiveness of reconciliation controls?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include the percentage of in-scope accounts assigned, reconciliations prepared on time, independent reviews completed, accounts with missing source evidence, overdue open items, outstanding exposure by age and risk, unresolved hard stops, repeated exceptions, correction turnaround time, and items reopened after closure. Define each metric's population, calculation, data source, reporting frequency, owner, target, and escalation criteria. A completion rate should use the verified population as its denominator and must not treat blocked reconciliations as complete. Open exposure should reconcile to the underlying issue register, and counts of prepared, reviewed, approved, held, and open items should tie to the account-level records. Explain material changes in performance and identify the actions needed. Metrics are monitoring tools, not substitutes for account-level review or authorization decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Design a reconciliation dashboard and independently verify its population, aging, open exposure, review status, and hard-stop counts against the underlying registers."
Key point:
- Metrics require controlled definitions, complete populations, and reproducible calculations.
- Good aggregate performance cannot override a critical unresolved balance.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
29. Q: When does a change to a reconciliation or its supporting data invalidate the previous approval?
A: Show answer
A change that affects the reporting period, account population, source evidence, balance, reconciling items, calculation, accounting conclusion, or material explanation must trigger an assessment of whether the prior review and approval remain valid. Where the change affects the reviewed scope or conclusion, invalidate the affected approval and require resubmission, re-review, and reapproval as defined by policy. Preserve the previously approved version and document the reason, requester, affected fields, old and new values, review, testing, approval, and new effective status. A change to a report's formatting that does not affect content may be handled differently, but this distinction must be governed by an approved change-control rule. Do not overwrite the approved file or reuse an approval reference for a materially changed reconciliation. The final archive should make both the original decision and the subsequent correction traceable.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a material change after approval and determine which calculations, reviews, authorizations, reports, and archived versions must be revisited."
Key point:
- Approval applies to a defined version and scope, not to every later copy of a workbook.
- Material post-approval changes require a documented impact assessment and appropriate re-review.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
30. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, and VBA support balance sheet reconciliation without weakening accounting controls?
A: Show answer
A controlled workbook can use standardized input tables, account master data, refreshable queries, reconciliation formulas, aging calculations, exception registers, workflow status fields, and dashboard summaries to reduce repetitive manual work. Power Query can combine eligible source files, normalize fields, apply documented transformations, and identify candidate matches or exceptions. Excel formulas can implement approved equations, while VBA can support controlled report generation, status updates, audit logging, and export of approved outputs. These tools must not decide unresolved accounting treatment automatically or treat imported data as verified merely because a refresh succeeds. Define the input source, query version, formulas, parameter ownership, error-handling behavior, review requirements, and approval gates. Validate automated outputs against independently calculated samples and investigate exceptions before release. Changes to logic require testing and approval, and users must be able to trace each output back to the underlying source records.

Study instruction:
- "Build a reconciliation prototype using controlled tables and documented calculations, then test it against independently verified results and known exceptions."
Key point:
- Automation supports consistent processing but does not establish accounting validity or authorization.
- Formula, query, and macro changes need controlled testing, review, and traceability.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Workflows
31. Q: What negative tests should be performed to establish that reconciliation controls fail safely?
A: Show answer
Negative tests deliberately introduce conditions that should prevent approval or closure. Examples include a missing source report, a ledger-only confirmation, an unexplained population difference, a duplicated item, an unsupported balance, a missing owner or due date, an expired delegation, a reviewer who is also the sole preparer, an unauthorized not-applicable status, a materially edited approved file, and an unresolved high-risk item. Expected outcomes should be defined before testing, including whether the process must reject the input, place the account on hold, require independent review, or prevent close. Record the test data, expected result, actual result, evidence, defect severity, remediation, and retest. A green test against synthetic data proves only that the defined test behaved as expected; it does not prove operational effectiveness in a live organization. External application or schema validation and human visual inspection must be reported separately if they have not been completed.

Study instruction:
- "Develop negative test cases for missing evidence, self-approval, broken references, unauthorized closure, and post-approval changes, then compare actual results with expected safeguards."
Key point:
- A robust control must reject or hold invalid states rather than merely report them.
- Synthetic test success must never be presented as proof of live operational effectiveness.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
32. Q: How should an unexplained bank debit be investigated when no corresponding payment appears in the accounting records?
A: Show answer
First verify the statement transaction, account, value date, currency, amount, and available bank narrative. Search the payment register, accounts-payable records, cashbook, clearing accounts, and relevant authorization logs using multiple matching attributes. Consider whether the debit is an authorized bank charge, an incorrectly referenced payment, an unrecorded legitimate transaction, a duplicate, a bank error, or a potentially unauthorized disbursement. Request clarification from the bank or relevant transaction owner through approved channels where the evidence remains incomplete. Preserve the original statement line and record all investigations, findings, referrals, and decisions. If fraud or unauthorized activity is reasonably suspected, follow the protected incident process and applicable urgent escalation requirements. Do not create an unsupported journal or label the debit a routine timing item solely to complete reconciliation. Close the issue only after authorized action and independent verification of the final balance.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a fictional unexplained debit from source statement through payment records, bank inquiry, risk assessment, corrective action, and verified closure."
Key point:
- Unidentified bank movements require investigation and risk assessment, not assumption.
- Suspected unauthorized transactions must follow the designated incident-response route.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
33. Q: What should happen when a staff or implementing-partner advance remains outstanding beyond its expected settlement date?
A: Show answer
Verify the original advance approval, payment evidence, contractual or program requirements, reporting deadline, expenditure submission, receipts, repayments, and any correspondence supporting an extension. Reconcile the advance register and subledger with the GL, retaining the original age and remaining amount. Determine whether the issue arises from missing documentation, a delayed activity, disputed expenditure, an unapplied repayment, incorrect allocation, or a potentially unrecoverable balance. Assign a responsible person, establish an evidence-based action plan, and escalate according to approved aging, materiality, and qualitative-risk criteria. Do not clear the balance merely because a new advance is issued, the reporting period changes, or the account is difficult to reconcile. Any write-off, expense recognition, impairment, recovery, or reclassification must follow the applicable accounting policy and authorized approval process. Closure requires confirmation that the supported outcome is reflected in the appropriate records.

Study instruction:
- "Use an overdue advance case to distinguish genuine timing differences from unsupported expenditure, repayment mismatches, and potential recoverability issues."
Key point:
- Aging, support, and follow-up must continue until the advance has an authorized and evidenced resolution.
- Reclassification or write-off is not a substitute for the required approval and accounting analysis.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable and Receivable Assessment
34. Q: How should reconciliation proceed when a source report is missing or cannot be independently verified before close?
A: Show answer
Determine which accounts, periods, balances, and assertions are affected by the missing source. Notify the source owner and controller, request the report or appropriate alternative evidence, and record the issue, deadline, risk, and escalation. Consider whether an approved alternative can genuinely support the required reconciliation; a copied ledger balance or unsupported management estimate is not automatically an acceptable replacement for an independent source. If a critical evidence gate remains unresolved, place the affected account on hold and follow the organization's close-gating procedure. Any permitted temporary treatment must be explicitly authorized, risk-assessed, time-bound, and disclosed to the relevant decision-maker. Retain the original request, responses, alternative evidence considered, approval, and subsequent verification. Once the source becomes available, complete the reconciliation and assess whether the earlier close or reporting decision needs to be revisited.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a missing source report and document the escalation, evidence alternatives considered, close decision, and subsequent reconciliation once the source is obtained."
Key point:
- Missing evidence is a control exception and must not be hidden by a forced reconciliation result.
- Temporary decisions require valid authority and later evidence-based follow-up.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Period-End Reconciliation
35. Q: How should interfaces and data transfers between finance systems be controlled during reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Map each transfer from its originating system through transformation, import, posting, and reconciliation. Record interface identifiers, source and destination periods, record counts, control totals, processing statuses, rejected records, duplicate checks, and evidence of successful completion. Compare pre-transfer and post-transfer totals at the appropriate scope and investigate missing, repeated, altered, or incorrectly mapped records. Where a transaction spans related subledgers and the GL, verify that the expected corresponding entries were created and that the balances remain consistent. Document the owner of each interface, the expected processing frequency, error handling, escalation route, and recovery procedure. A successful upload message does not prove that all records were correctly accepted, classified, and posted. After correcting an interface failure, reconcile the actual system state before retrying to avoid duplicate transactions.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample batch across source extraction, transformation, system import, GL posting, rejected records, and final reconciliation."
Key point:
- Interfaces need completeness and accuracy controls at both ends of the transfer.
- Before retrying a failed or uncertain batch, verify whether any records were already posted.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Umoja Overview and Simultaneous Posting
36. Q: What should be included in the controlled reconciliation forms, registers, and workflow inventory?
A: Show answer
The implementation pack should include an authority and source register, configuration parameters, responsibility and delegation matrix, account population register, reconciliation-method assignment, source completeness checklist, account-specific evidence standards, calculation templates, reconciling-item and aging register, independent review and resubmission form, correction-referral register, month-end close checklist, exception and corrective-action log, retention and access register, change log, test inventory, and final adoption checklist. Each component should have a defined owner, required fields, status transitions, validation rules, reference links, and approval requirements. Forms should use consistent identifiers so that account, issue, evidence, and correction records can be connected without relying on free-text descriptions. Configuration fields such as materiality thresholds, due dates, tolerances, retention periods, and delegated authorities must remain on hold until supported by actual approved policy. The inventory must distinguish implemented controls from proposals and unverified requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Inventory the SOP forms and registers, then test whether their fields and references support traceability from source evidence through review, correction, and close approval."
Key point:
- Controlled forms and registers make the reconciliation workflow reproducible and auditable.
- Unverified settings and proposed controls must not be represented as approved operating requirements.
Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Processes
37. Q: How should an organization test and authorize the implementation of the reconciliation SOP?
A: Show answer
Start by verifying the legal entity, applicable framework, authorized policy owner, reviewer, approver, source systems, account population, and required parameters. Validate the documented workflow and cross-references, calculation examples, aging logic, matching rules, source-completeness controls, risk escalation, approval invalidation, and close-gating behavior. Perform positive tests to establish that valid cases proceed correctly and negative tests to establish that missing evidence, invalid authority, self-approval, unsupported balances, and other critical failures are rejected or held. Separate automated structural checks from independent accounting review, live-system testing, document application testing, and human visual inspection. Record each test's scope, expected result, actual outcome, evidence, defects, remediation, and retest. The supplied example explicitly distinguishes synthetic test success from verification in an actual organization. Final adoption should occur only after the applicable gates are satisfied and the authorized decision is recorded.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an implementation acceptance checklist that distinguishes rule-design tests, synthetic calculation tests, live-system evidence, independent review, and formal organizational approval."
Key point:
- A tested template is not automatically a validated live process or approved organizational policy.
- Adoption requires documented evidence, resolved critical defects, and authorization by the proper authority.

- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual
