- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
9. Q: Who should prepare, review, approve, issue, and archive financial statements?
A: Show answer
Separate source-data ownership, statement preparation, technical review, management review, final authorization, controlled distribution, and record custody. Source owners certify the completeness of their schedules; preparers assemble and calculate the statements; independent reviewers challenge accounting judgments and verify corrections; management assesses performance, liquidity, risks, and significant assumptions; the authorized approver approves the exact reporting package within delegated authority; and the release operator distributes the locked version. The records custodian preserves the approved package and its evidence. A preparer must not be the sole independent reviewer and approver of their own work. Where staffing limits full segregation, document an appropriately independent compensating review, its scope, its evidence, and the authorization supporting the arrangement.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a RACI matrix for each reporting activity and test actual user permissions against delegated responsibilities."

Key point:
- Responsibilities must be clear, independent where required, and supported by evidence.
- Job titles alone do not prove authority to approve or issue statements.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
10. Q: What evidence should be required when financial data is received from source systems?
A: Show answer
Each source extract should identify the entity, ledger, period, reporting date or timestamp, source system and version, extraction date, filters, currency, preparer, row count, control totals, and an integrity reference such as a checksum where appropriate. Supporting schedules should cover relevant subledgers, bank reconciliations, receivables, payables, grants or funding, payroll and employee benefits, fixed assets, leases, provisions, foreign exchange, tax, inter-entity balances, approved journals, and material estimates. Compare the received dataset with the expected source population and investigate omitted, duplicated, stale, unauthorized, or unexplained records. Record the intake status as requested, received, validating, accepted, rejected, replaced, or excluded with authority. Missing evidence must remain visible; an incomplete source cannot automatically be treated as zero.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a source-data pack and verify its population, control totals, source identity, extraction parameters, period, and approval status."

Key point:
- Data receipt is not the same as data acceptance.
- Source completeness must be demonstrated before dependent reporting can proceed.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
11. Q: How should the trial balance be validated before it is used to prepare financial statements?
A: Show answer
Confirm that the trial balance belongs to the identified entity, ledger, reporting period, and currency and that the extraction method and filters are appropriate. Recalculate debit and credit totals, verify that total debits equal total credits under the system's sign convention, and reconcile material balances to the general ledger and relevant subledgers. A balanced trial balance does not prove that every transaction has been recorded, that all accounts are correctly classified, or that balances are properly measured. Investigate unusual balances, duplicate entries, unsupported journals, stale reconciliations, unexplained suspense accounts, and differences between accounting schedules and the ledger. Retain the original extract and record all subsequent adjustments separately with unique identifiers and approvals.

Study instruction:
- "Perform an independent trial-balance validation and trace material account balances to their source ledgers and supporting schedules."

Key point:
- A balanced trial balance is necessary but not sufficient evidence of reliable financial statements.
- Completeness, classification, measurement, and reconciliation require separate controls.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
12. Q: How can every financial statement line be traced back to its supporting records?
A: Show answer
Maintain a source-lineage structure linking the statement line to the general ledger account, relevant dimensions, source schedule, adjustment, approved account mapping, calculation, foreign-exchange record where applicable, and note reference. Each relationship should use stable identifiers rather than relying only on descriptive names. Preserve the original data extract and record query or transformation versions, reporting period, preparer, reviewer, and approval status. The process should support tracing from an aggregate statement amount down to individual transactions and from a transaction forward to the statement and its disclosures. If a source amount or mapping changes, identify all affected calculations, notes, reconciliations, reviews, and approvals. Recalculate and revalidate the affected dependency chain rather than silently replacing figures in the final package.

Study instruction:
- "Select one material statement balance and trace it in both directions between the statement, reconciliation, ledger, source transactions, and supporting evidence."

Key point:
- Reliable financial reporting requires end-to-end data lineage.
- Material changes must trigger appropriate downstream revalidation.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
13. Q: How should the account-to-statement mapping register be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled mapping between each source account and its presentation destination, including statement, line item, note reference, applicable reporting view, sign convention, effective date, approval, and any authorized split or allocation logic. Validate that every required account has an approved mapping effective for the reporting date and that accounts do not disappear or appear twice in the same reporting view without an explicit, reconcilable rule. Test aggregation and disaggregation, the treatment of unusual balances, and consistency between current and comparative periods. A new account or changed mapping must not take effect merely because a preparer edited a spreadsheet. Require independent review, authorized approval, documented testing, and renewed validation of dependent statements and notes before release.

Study instruction:
- "Test the mapping register for unmapped accounts, duplicate destinations, expired mappings, unapproved changes, and incorrect sign conventions."

Key point:
- Effective-dated and approved mappings protect the completeness and classification of reported amounts.
- Mapping changes can invalidate statements, notes, and prior review evidence.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
14. Q: How should financial statement reconciliations be prepared and independently reviewed?
A: Show answer
For each material balance, identify the account, period, opening and closing amounts, supporting population, reconciling items, owner, independent reviewer, evidence, outstanding-item age, and required follow-up. Relevant reconciliations may include cash, receivables, payables, grants and funding balances, fixed assets, leases, employee benefits, provisions, tax, foreign exchange, and inter-entity accounts. Reconcile accounting records with independently supported schedules or external evidence where appropriate. Explain timing differences, corrections, duplicate postings, and unsupported balances individually. A preparer's assertion without corroborating evidence is not sufficient for a material item. Retain the review outcome and retest evidence following corrections. Material unexplained differences must remain unresolved and prevent approval unless a permitted, fully documented, and authorized exception is available under the applicable framework.

Study instruction:
- "Review a material account reconciliation and verify that every reconciling item has an explanation, supporting evidence, owner, due date, and disposition."

Key point:
- Reconciliation requires evidence and independent challenge, not just matching totals.
- Unresolved material differences must be explicitly escalated.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
15. Q: How should reporting adjustments, estimates, and judgments be documented?
A: Show answer
Each adjustment should have a unique identifier, period, amount, currency, accounting basis, supporting schedule, rationale, preparer, independent reviewer, approver, and a clear indication of whether it is a ledger-posted journal or a reporting-only adjustment. Identify affected accounts, statement lines, notes, comparatives, and other dependent outputs. Estimates should document the valuation or calculation method, data sources, assumptions, uncertainty, sensitivity where relevant, alternatives considered, and the basis for management judgment. Significant judgments should be reviewed by an appropriately qualified person and approved within delegated authority. An unsupported estimate or material adjustment cannot be legitimized by appearing in a balanced statement. Maintain the calculation, decision trail, and subsequent review evidence so the conclusion can be reproduced and challenged.

Study instruction:
- "Select an estimate or adjustment and prepare its evidence file, including method, assumptions, accounting rationale, independent challenge, approval, and reporting impact."

Key point:
- Estimates and adjustments require documented judgment and accountability.
- A spreadsheet result does not replace the accounting rationale or approval.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
16. Q: How should materiality be applied during financial statement preparation and review?
A: Show answer
Materiality concerns whether an omission, misstatement, aggregation, or presentation decision could influence the decisions or assessments of users. Its evaluation may involve amounts and qualitative factors, including the nature of the transaction, donor restrictions, compliance significance, related-party relationships, fraud indicators, and the sensitivity of a disclosure. Establish the organization's approved materiality methodology and document the applicable threshold, rationale, approver, and intended use. Do not invent a universal materiality percentage or use one numerical threshold to dismiss every qualitative issue. Aggregate uncorrected misstatements where required, assess their combined effect, and consider whether a seemingly small error signals a wider control failure. Apply the framework-specific materiality requirements and record why material judgments and unresolved items are or are not acceptable for the intended reporting purpose.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate several errors using both quantitative and qualitative factors and document why each requires correction, disclosure, further analysis, or an authorized disposition."

Key point:
- Materiality is a documented judgment, not a universal numerical shortcut.
- Qualitatively significant issues may require action even when their amounts are small.

Official links:
- IPSASB — Definition of Material and Related Amendments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
17. Q: How should comparative figures and prior-period corrections be controlled?
A: Show answer
Reconcile comparative information to the prior period's approved financial statements and retained supporting records. If a current-period presentation reclassifies comparative information, document the original and revised amounts, affected lines and notes, the reason for the change, the relevant accounting requirement, and any required explanation. Where an error is identified, distinguish a prior-period error from a change in accounting estimate or an authorized change in accounting policy. These categories can have different treatment, including retrospective correction or prospective application where applicable and practicable. Do not relabel an error as an estimate change merely to avoid restatement. Retain the original issued records, approval of the correction, recalculated comparisons, and evidence that every dependent statement and note has been updated appropriately.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a proposed comparative adjustment with the prior approved package and determine whether it represents a reclassification, estimate change, policy change, or prior-period error."

Key point:
- Comparative changes require an explicit rationale and traceable approval.
- Error correction must not erase the historical record of what was issued.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
18. Q: How should the statement of financial position be prepared and validated?
A: Show answer
Map the approved adjusted trial balance to the asset, liability, and net-assets or equity categories required by the confirmed framework. Validate recognition, measurement, classification, and current/non-current presentation where applicable. Reconcile material cash, receivable, inventory, fixed-asset, lease, payable, employee-benefit, provision, tax, and other balances to their supporting schedules. Recalculate all line items and subtotals, verify the overall accounting equation using the approved sign convention, and tie material statement lines to their disclosures. Check comparative values, unusual negative balances, missing accounts, unexplained changes, and consistency with other statements. A balanced statement does not prove that the assets exist, the liabilities are complete, or all measurement judgments are appropriate. Unresolved material issues must be documented and held before approval.

Study instruction:
- "Rebuild the statement of financial position from the approved trial balance and independently recalculate each material line, subtotal, total, and note reference."

Key point:
- The statement must agree with its supporting schedules and applicable accounting requirements.
- Arithmetic balance does not establish completeness or appropriate measurement.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
19. Q: How should the statement of financial performance be prepared and reviewed?
A: Show answer
Derive income, revenue or support, expenses, gains, losses, and the resulting surplus or deficit from approved ledger balances and supporting schedules, using the terminology and classifications required by the selected framework. Review whether transactions have been recognized in the appropriate period, classified according to their substance, and presented with sufficient disaggregation. In an NGO, donor labels or account names do not automatically determine accounting recognition. Review significant changes from prior periods, unusual amounts, project and fund allocations where applicable, foreign-exchange effects, estimates, and any applicable tax balances. Recalculate totals independently and tie relevant line items to the notes and other statements. Hold unexplained variances, inconsistent note values, and unsupported recognition judgments until reviewed and appropriately resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Select material income and expense categories, trace them to supporting schedules, and reconcile their presentation to the ledger and related notes."

Key point:
- Presentation must reflect the applicable framework and the substance of transactions.
- Variances and unusual balances require explanation rather than automatic reclassification.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
20. Q: How should the statement of cash flows be prepared and reconciled?
A: Show answer
First confirm whether cash-flow reporting is required, the applicable definition of cash and cash equivalents, the permitted or required presentation method, currency treatment, and the classification rules under the selected framework. Reconcile opening cash to closing cash through operating, investing, and financing cash flows as applicable. Under an indirect method, independently derive the operating cash-flow calculation from the relevant performance and working-capital information. Evaluate non-cash investing and financing transactions, exchange effects, acquisitions, disposals, restricted cash, and classification changes according to the governing requirements. Prevent double counting between movements in cash and changes in accrual-based balances. An unexplained cash-flow bridge, unsupported cash-equivalent assumption, or mismatch with the statement of financial position must remain unresolved before approval.

Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the cash-flow statement and verify the opening-to-closing cash bridge, classification, non-cash items, and currency effects."

Key point:
- The cash-flow statement must reconcile to the approved cash balances.
- Non-cash movements and exchange effects require appropriate treatment under the applicable framework.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
21. Q: How should changes in net assets or equity be reconciled to the financial statements?
A: Show answer
Where required, prepare a reconciliation of opening to closing net assets or equity that distinguishes the period's performance from other movements recognized directly in net assets or equity. Evaluate transfers between categories, prior-period corrections, changes in reserves, and any other required movements under the applicable framework. Tie the opening balance to the approved comparative records, the current-period result to the statement of financial performance, and the closing balance to the statement of financial position. In an NGO, restricted and unrestricted fund reporting may be relevant, but internal fund categories do not automatically dictate recognition or measurement. Document the basis for each movement and investigate any difference that cannot be traced to an approved entry or supported adjustment.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the opening and closing net-assets or equity balances, matching every movement to the performance statement, ledger, and supporting evidence."

Key point:
- All material movements must be traceable to an appropriate accounting source.
- Fund presentation must not be confused with the underlying recognition rules.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
22. Q: When should a statement comparing budget and actual amounts be prepared?
A: Show answer
Determine whether budget comparison is required by the selected framework, applicable law, reporting purpose, or donor or institutional requirements. Verify which approved budget is relevant, how the budget period and basis correspond to the financial reporting period, which entities or funds are covered, and what level of comparison and explanation is necessary. Reconcile the actual amounts to the relevant accounting records and apply the required budget basis rather than assuming that the accounting and budget bases are identical. Explain material variances using approved criteria and reliable operational information. Do not copy another organization's variance threshold or budget format without authority. Retain the approved budget version, calculation method, source data, variance explanations, reviewer comments, and evidence supporting any amendments.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the reporting requirements with the approved budget, actual ledger data, and variance explanations, documenting the applicable budget basis."

Key point:
- Budget comparison requirements depend on the applicable reporting framework and purpose.
- Budget actuals and accounting actuals may require a documented reconciliation.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
23. Q: How should financial statement notes and disclosure applicability be managed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a disclosure register identifying each potentially relevant note, its applicable requirement, factual trigger, responsible owner, required quantitative and qualitative information, supporting evidence, comparative requirements, statement references, review status, and approval. Assess disclosures individually against the selected framework, balances, agreements, judgments, risks, events, and reporting purpose. A note should not be marked complete solely because text has been drafted. Required values must tie to the approved statements and underlying schedules, and significant explanations must be consistent with the accounting judgments actually adopted. Distinguish a verified conclusion that a disclosure is not applicable from a topic that has not yet been assessed. Missing evidence, unresolved applicability questions, and incomplete review should remain visible and block release where required.

Study instruction:
- "Build a disclosure register and test that each applicable note has a documented requirement, supporting evidence, tie-out, independent review, and valid completion status."

Key point:
- Disclosure completeness requires evidence-based assessment, not merely completed text.
- An unassessed disclosure cannot be treated as not applicable.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
24. Q: What information should the reporting-entity and basis-of-preparation notes contain?
A: Show answer
Depending on the applicable framework, the notes should explain the entity's identity and reporting perimeter, purpose of the statements, reporting period, basis of preparation, applicable accounting framework, significant accounting policies, functional and presentation currencies, measurement basis, comparative information, significant judgments and estimates, and authorization for issue. Each statement must describe the actual reporting entity and relevant approved facts. If the reporting framework, legal form, or measurement basis is still unverified, do not copy the assumptions from a demonstration template into the issued notes. Include only the disclosures required or appropriate under the applicable authority and actual circumstances. Verify dates, names, currency, and reporting-purpose descriptions against approved source records and reconcile the note to the decisions documented during reporting-package initiation.

Study instruction:
- "Draft the reporting-entity and basis-of-preparation notes using verified entity information and compare the disclosures with the selected framework."

Key point:
- The basis-of-preparation note must describe actual facts and approved policies.
- Template wording cannot substitute for framework selection or legal verification.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
25. Q: How should accounting-policy choices and future accounting pronouncements be monitored?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled policy register identifying the accounting policy, governing standard and paragraph, current edition, effective date, applicability assessment, responsible owner, reviewer, approval, and dependent procedures or calculations. Monitor official standard-setter publications and identify new, amended, or future-effective requirements that could affect recognition, measurement, presentation, disclosure, or transition. Assess whether early application is permitted and appropriate before treating an amendment as operative. Record the reporting periods affected, systems and templates requiring change, comparative implications, training needs, and implementation approval. A published standard does not by itself establish that it applies to the organization. For example, the relevant IPSASB requirements effective from 2026 or 2027 must be assessed against the entity's confirmed framework and the specific reporting period.

Study instruction:
- "Select a future-effective pronouncement and document its official source, effective date, organizational applicability, transition assessment, implementation plan, and approval."

Key point:
- Accounting requirements must be monitored by source and effective date.
- Future standards should not be treated as automatically applicable to every reporting entity.

Official links:
- IPSASB — 2026 Handbook of International Public Sector Accounting Standards Board Pronouncements
- IPSASB — Revenue and Transfer Expense Pronouncements
26. Q: How should foreign-currency balances and translation effects be incorporated into the reporting package?
A: Show answer
Confirm the applicable accounting requirements for functional currency, transaction-date rates, period-end translation, settlement effects, and the presentation of exchange differences. Maintain the original transaction currency and amount, rate source, rate date, converted amount, calculation, and approval of material rate parameters. Reconcile foreign-currency schedules to the ledger and distinguish transaction-level exchange differences from translation effects and cash-flow effects where required. If the organization consolidates entities with different functional currencies, document the translation basis and applicable treatment for the reporting period. Never assume that the illustrative United States dollar basis in a template applies to the actual organization. Any unsupported rate, inconsistent translation, or unreconciled currency difference should be investigated and documented before the related statement lines and disclosures are approved.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a foreign-currency balance from the original transaction through translation, ledger posting, reconciliation, and relevant disclosure."

Key point:
- Currency treatment depends on the applicable framework and verified currency parameters.
- Original values, exchange rates, and resulting differences must be reproducible.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
27. Q: How should material inter-entity balances and consolidation eliminations be reviewed?
A: Show answer
Where consolidation is required, obtain reporting packages from all included entities, confirm their period and accounting-policy basis, and reconcile inter-entity receivables, payables, transfers, income, expenses, and other relevant balances. Record each elimination using a unique identifier linked to both source entries, amounts and currencies, translation treatment if applicable, supporting evidence, preparer, independent reviewer, approval, and subsequent validation. Investigate differences rather than using unexplained balancing adjustments or duplicate eliminations. Verify that the consolidated balances, statements, and notes agree after elimination. Where consolidation is not required, retain the applicability assessment and its supporting authority. Unresolved material differences or unsupported scope decisions must remain open and be escalated before the consolidated package is approved.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a sample of reciprocal entity balances and verify that each elimination is supported, independently reviewed, and reflected consistently in the consolidated statements."

Key point:
- Eliminations require traceable source pairs and independent validation.
- A consolidated balance must not rely on an unsupported plug.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
28. Q: How should technical review and management review differ?
A: Show answer
Technical review challenges the application of the accounting framework, statement calculations, account mappings, disclosure completeness, estimates, comparatives, consolidation, and accounting-policy judgments. Management review focuses on financial performance, liquidity, risks, going concern where relevant, significant assumptions, unresolved matters, and decisions required. Neither review replaces the other. Record each review point with a unique identifier, severity, affected period and statement or note, issue description, evidence, owner, response, proposed correction or disposition, independent verification, and closure evidence. Material review findings must not be closed merely because a preparer provides a verbal explanation. Assign an appropriately independent reviewer to verify corrections, and preserve the history of the issue and the decision. Open material matters must prevent approval unless a permitted, documented, and properly authorized disposition is available.

Study instruction:
- "Take a sample reporting package and separate technical review findings from management review findings, assigning owners and evidence for each resolution."

Key point:
- Technical review and management review serve distinct purposes.
- Review points require evidence-based closure and appropriate independence.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
29. Q: How should uncorrected misstatements and unresolved reporting issues be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Accumulate identified misstatements and assess their individual and combined effects using approved quantitative and qualitative criteria. Consider the affected accounts, statement lines, notes, periods, compliance implications, donor restrictions, and potential impact on user decisions. Document whether a correction is proposed, whether the correction is accepted, and the rationale and authority for any permitted unadjusted disposition. An unresolved matter should have a named owner, severity, deadline, required evidence, financial-reporting impact, escalation route, and approval requirement. Do not use a general suspense category or an arbitrary materiality threshold to conceal unsupported balances. Required accounting and regulatory obligations cannot be waived by internal convenience. Ensure that every final decision is reflected consistently in the statements, notes, review records, and release-gate assessment.

Study instruction:
- "Compile a misstatement register and evaluate the combined impact of open items against approved criteria before recommending release."

Key point:
- All material and qualitatively significant issues require documented assessment.
- An unresolved balance must not be treated as resolved simply to meet a deadline.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
30. Q: What controls should prevent an incomplete or unapproved reporting package from being issued?
A: Show answer
Establish explicit release gates for confirmed framework applicability, reporting scope, accepted source data, reconciliations, approved mappings, required disclosures, technical review, management review, approval authority, and the exact package version. Define prerequisites and permitted status transitions for each gate. The workflow must reject attempts to move a draft directly to authorized, issued, or completed status. Approval must relate to the identified entity, reporting period, purpose, exact version, and authority scope. A failed test or missing material evidence must return the package to a blocked state with an assigned correction route. Record the final approval, release authorization, version fingerprint or equivalent identity, distribution list, issue date, and receipt evidence. Release control must be enforced by permissions and workflow rules rather than only by a warning on the cover page.

Study instruction:
- "Test the release workflow with a complete package and with a deliberately missing reconciliation or approval, confirming that only the compliant package can proceed."

Key point:
- Release gates prevent incomplete or unauthorized statements from being issued.
- Approval applies to a specific version, not to any file with a similar name.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
31. Q: How should the final financial statements be authorized, issued, and distributed?
A: Show answer
Confirm that all mandatory reporting and approval gates have passed and that the authorized approver has reviewed the exact final version within the delegated scope. Preserve the approved package in a locked or otherwise controlled state before distribution. The release operator should distribute only that version to authorized recipients and record the issue date, version identifier, report scope, recipient list, distribution method, and receipt evidence where required. The release operator must not alter approved content during formatting or distribution. If a recipient requires a different format, confirm that the transformation does not change the approved figures or disclosures. Record the status as approved, authorized for issue, issued, or reissued according to distinct lifecycle definitions. A signed approval record must not be treated as proof of actual issuance unless the distribution evidence exists.

Study instruction:
- "Trace an approved package through final authorization, controlled export, distribution, recipient logging, and archive confirmation."

Key point:
- Final approval and actual issuance are separate events.
- Distribution must preserve the exact approved content and version.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
32. Q: What should happen if a material error is identified after the financial statements have been issued?
A: Show answer
Preserve the originally issued package and immediately record the suspected error, discovery date, affected amounts, periods, statements, disclosures, and recipients. Assess the applicable framework's requirements for correction or restatement, as well as any statutory, donor, contractual, and governance notification requirements. Document the technical conclusion, financial impact, proposed correction, reviewer challenge, authorization, and required communications. Create a distinct corrected or reissued version linked to the original instead of overwriting historical records. Recalculate affected statements and notes, rerun relevant checks, obtain the required approval, and issue the correction through an authorized process. Update the archive index and any applicable comparative information. Also investigate the root cause and implement corrective controls to prevent recurrence.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a post-issuance error case from discovery to technical assessment, approval, corrected version, communication, and root-cause review."

Key point:
- Post-issuance corrections must preserve the original record and maintain a clear version history.
- The applicable framework determines the accounting treatment and required disclosures.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
33. Q: How should events occurring after the reporting date be assessed before issuance?
A: Show answer
Establish a procedure for identifying significant events between the reporting date and the date the financial statements are authorized for issue. Determine whether the applicable framework requires an adjustment to amounts recognized at the reporting date or disclosure of a material event that does not change those amounts. Collect relevant evidence, assess the circumstances and timing, identify affected assets, liabilities, estimates, going-concern assumptions, and disclosures, and document the conclusion. Events may include major funding changes, litigation developments, damage to assets, significant financial difficulties, or other circumstances relevant to the entity. The treatment must be based on the selected framework and the facts of the event, not simply on when management learned about it. Preserve the assessment and its approval with the reporting package.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an events-after-reporting-date register and classify sample events under the applicable framework, documenting evidence and proposed reporting effects."

Key point:
- Events after the reporting date can affect amounts, disclosures, or both, depending on the applicable requirements.
- The assessment must be completed before authorization for issue.

Official links:
- IAS 10 — Events after the Reporting Period, where the IFRS framework applies
34. Q: How should evidence, archive retention, privacy, and access be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain a structured archive by entity, reporting period, package version, statement, note, supporting schedule, approval, and release evidence. Preserve the source files, reconciliations, journals, adjustments, judgments, review comments, authorization, issued package, distribution record, and any subsequent corrections. Apply approved retention rules based on applicable law, donor and contractual requirements, and organizational policy. Limit access according to role and protect sensitive employee, donor, partner, and financial information. Legal holds must prevent disposal where applicable. Backups should be tested through retrieval or restoration exercises, not merely assumed to be effective because copies exist. Assign a records custodian, record retrieval tests, and ensure that the archive preserves both the content and the history needed to reconstruct the reporting decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Perform an archive retrieval exercise and verify the availability, integrity, access controls, retention status, and version history of a complete reporting package."

Key point:
- Reliable archiving supports audit, accountability, and future correction.
- Backup existence alone does not prove successful recovery or record completeness.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
35. Q: How should the reporting process respond to incomplete evidence or an unavailable source owner?
A: Show answer
Record the missing source or evidence, the affected balances and disclosures, the responsible owner, the date needed, the reporting impact, and the escalation route. Distinguish a genuinely zero balance from a balance that cannot yet be determined. Seek the original source or independently corroborating evidence and assess whether an authorized alternative is permitted under the applicable framework and organizational policy. Do not fill gaps with unsupported estimates, copy prior-period numbers without analysis, or mark the reconciliation as complete merely to meet a deadline. Where a permitted limitation exists, document its scope, justification, approval, quantitative and qualitative effects, and any disclosure consequences. Otherwise, retain the affected output on hold and escalate the deadline risk to the responsible management and approval authorities.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a missing material source schedule and document the required investigation, escalation, permitted alternatives, and release decision."

Key point:
- Missing evidence must remain visible until resolved or validly dispositioned.
- An incomplete source must never default to zero without a supported basis.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
36. Q: How should a stale reconciliation or unsupported balance be investigated?
A: Show answer
Establish the balance's origin, last verified date, transaction population, responsible owner, prior reconciling items, and whether the underlying records have changed. Obtain the relevant source ledger, external confirmation or independent support where appropriate, and subsequent-period activity. Investigate long-outstanding items, unusual movements, duplicate entries, omitted transactions, incorrect account mappings, and differences between subledgers and the general ledger. Document the root cause, potential misstatement, proposed journal or correction, reviewer challenge, authorization, and retest. A journal entry should not be used to eliminate an unexplained difference without appropriate evidence. If the balance is material or remains unsupported, maintain the hold and report the financial and deadline implications until a permitted disposition is approved.

Study instruction:
- "Choose an aged reconciling item and trace it through the original transaction, subsequent activity, corrective action, approval, and retest."

Key point:
- Aged differences require investigation and an evidence-backed resolution.
- Unsupported balances cannot be cleared solely through a convenient journal adjustment.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
37. Q: How can reporting workflows, status transitions, and automated checks strengthen financial statement controls?
A: Show answer
Use controlled workflow states to distinguish source requested, received, validating, accepted, rejected, or replaced; reconciliation prepared, reviewed, and resolved; disclosure not assessed, applicable, not applicable with evidence, drafted, under review, or completed; and package draft, approved, authorized, issued, or reissued. Define permitted transitions, prerequisite evidence, authorized roles, and required tests for each change. Automated checks can identify missing mappings, unbalanced trial balances, mismatched totals, missing disclosures, duplicate identifiers, and release attempts that bypass approvals. However, a successful automated check proves only the condition tested. It does not establish that the accounting treatment is correct, that evidence is genuine, or that the applicable framework has been selected. Retain test results, failures, resolutions, version information, and independent validation of important calculations.

Study instruction:
- "Build and test a workflow containing blocked and permitted transitions, checking that each status change requires the appropriate evidence and authority."

Key point:
- Status labels should reflect verifiable process states.
- Automated validation supplements but does not replace professional judgment and review.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
38. Q: How should Excel, Power Query, and reporting systems be controlled when preparing financial statements?
A: Show answer
Use structured source tables, approved account and dimension master lists, consistent identifiers, documented query transformations, controlled calculation logic, and versioned reporting templates. Reconcile imported populations and total amounts to their source records and preserve the date, parameters, and result of each refresh. Automated calculations should be tested against independently prepared examples, including missing records, duplicate transactions, invalid accounts, unusual signs, and changes in mapping. Any amendment to a formula, mapping, query, or configuration that affects the reporting package must trigger appropriate testing, review, approval, and recalculation of dependent outputs. Restrict editing rights over approved logic and final outputs. Software should improve consistency and traceability, but it cannot determine which accounting framework applies or establish that a recognition, measurement, or disclosure decision is correct.

Study instruction:
- "Test a reporting workbook against independently calculated results and document how source changes, formula errors, and mapping changes affect downstream statements."

Key point:
- Automation should support controlled, reproducible reporting.
- Accounting authority and professional review remain separate from software implementation.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
39. Q: What negative tests should be performed before a reporting process is accepted for use?
A: Show answer
Test deliberately defective inputs and workflow attempts to confirm that the process rejects or holds them safely. Relevant fixtures include duplicate account codes, an unbalanced trial balance, missing or expired mappings, unknown disclosure statuses, unsupported not-applicable conclusions, altered statement figures without recalculated totals, cash-flow mismatches, malformed monetary values, unapproved mappings, missing required disclosures, unauthorized self-approval, attempts to issue drafts, and attempts to overwrite an issued package. For each test, define the defect, expected result, controlling rule, responsible owner, and corrective route. Verify that rejected inputs do not silently become zero, bypass approvals, or corrupt unrelated outputs. A test marked successful must have actual execution evidence rather than only a declared expected outcome.

Study instruction:
- "Execute representative negative tests and retain the defective input, system response, validation evidence, and correction procedure for each test."

Key point:
- Effective controls must reject invalid conditions rather than merely describe them.
- Declared test expectations are not proof that tests were executed successfully.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
40. Q: How should implementation, training, and periodic SOP review be managed?
A: Show answer
Assign a policy owner and establish a controlled implementation plan covering source verification, approval of accounting policies, adaptation of the reporting calendar, configuration of mappings and registers, user permissions, training, testing, and formal adoption. Train preparers, source owners, reviewers, approvers, release operators, and custodians on their individual responsibilities and the evidence expected from them. Monitor recurring late sources, overdue reconciliations, repeated review findings, reporting adjustments, disclosure gaps, rejected tests, issuance delays, and archive-retrieval failures. Use these indicators to identify whether problems arise from training, process design, unclear ownership, incorrect data, system limitations, or accounting judgments. Review the SOP periodically and whenever authoritative requirements, organizational structure, systems, reporting purposes, or material risks change. Approve revisions, communicate them to affected users, and preserve superseded versions.

Study instruction:
- "Design an implementation and review plan with accountable owners, training evidence, control indicators, change triggers, and an approval process for revisions."

Key point:
- An SOP must be implemented, tested, monitored, and maintained to remain effective.
- The approved procedure should reflect verified requirements and actual organizational practice.

Official links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual
- IPSASB — 2026 Handbook of International Public Sector Accounting Standards Board Pronouncements
Rows: 40
Table 10
Financial Records Management and Document Retention
1. Q: What is the purpose of a financial records management and document retention SOP for an international NGO?
A: Show answer
A financial records management and document retention SOP establishes a controlled process for creating, receiving, classifying, registering, storing, retrieving, protecting, retaining, transferring, and disposing of financial records. Its purpose is to preserve confidentiality, integrity, availability, provenance, traceability, and accountability throughout the records lifecycle. Financial records include invoices, contracts, purchase orders, bank statements, payment evidence, payroll records, general ledger extracts, journal vouchers, grant agreements, donor reports, reconciliations, approval records, audit correspondence, and supporting schedules. The SOP must identify who owns each record class, where the authoritative copy resides, which rules govern retention, who may access records, and what evidence proves completion of each control. The supplied DOCX is explicitly a fictional demonstration and is not an adopted organizational policy. Its fictional entity, test dates, roles, permissions, retention fixtures, and workflow outcomes must not be treated as facts about a real NGO. An actual SOP must be customized using verified legal requirements, executed donor agreements, organizational policies, repository configurations, and authorized decisions before operational use.

Study instruction:
- "Map the full lifecycle of one financial record and identify the owner, authoritative location, access rules, retention basis, and final disposition control."
Key point:
- A records SOP establishes accountable lifecycle controls; it does not independently certify the underlying transaction as correct or lawful.
- A fictional template must be validated and formally adopted before operational use.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Understanding Records Management
2. Q: What principles should govern the management of financial records throughout their lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Financial records should be managed according to documented principles of authenticity, reliability, integrity, usability, confidentiality, availability, accountability, and traceability. Authenticity concerns whether a record is what it claims to be, while integrity concerns whether it remains complete and protected from unauthorized alteration. Reliability concerns whether the record adequately supports the activity or transaction it documents. Usability requires that authorized personnel can identify, locate, retrieve, interpret, and use the record when needed. Confidentiality limits access to authorized purposes, while availability requires appropriate access and recovery arrangements. These principles should be translated into operational controls, including controlled registration, metadata, version history, access permissions, audit logs, reconciliation, backup, restoration testing, and approved disposition. A successful upload, checksum, balanced record count, or system response proves only the property actually tested. None independently establishes that the transaction was eligible, that the signer was authorized, or that the retention period was legally correct. The record owner must therefore define the required evidence and the reviewer must evaluate that evidence against explicit acceptance criteria.

Study instruction:
- "For a financial record, distinguish authenticity, reliability, integrity, usability, confidentiality, and availability, then identify one control supporting each applicable principle."
Key point:
- Records-management principles must be expressed as testable controls and retained evidence.
- Technical success does not prove complete legal, financial, or procedural correctness.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN Archives — Records Management Glossary
3. Q: What records and activities should fall within the scope of an NGO financial records management SOP?
A: Show answer
The scope should cover records generated or received through accounting, treasury, budgeting, procurement, payroll, grants, donor reporting, partner management, tax administration, fixed assets, inventory, project delivery, and financial governance. It should also cover emails and electronic approvals when they document material decisions, commitments, authorizations, exceptions, or transactions. The SOP must specify which legal entities, country offices, project offices, repositories, physical archives, cloud platforms, vendors, and record classes are covered. It should identify exclusions and interfaces with other procedures, including payroll processing, procurement, tax filing, privacy incidents, investigations, legal advice, cybersecurity, audit execution, and enterprise archives. Exclusion from the SOP does not mean an obligation disappears. When a matter belongs to another process owner, finance should record a formal referral containing the case reference, record identifiers, outstanding issue, responsible owner, deadline, preservation requirements, and conditions for returning the matter to the records workflow. Ambiguous ownership or uncertain entity coverage must be recorded as an unresolved applicability issue rather than silently assumed to be covered.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a scope matrix covering legal entity, country, financial process, record class, system, repository, process owner, exclusion, and referral route."
Key point:
- Scope must be explicit enough to establish which records and obligations the SOP actually governs.
- Referral to another department must preserve accountability and evidence of follow-up.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Manage Records
4. Q: How should legal requirements, donor agreements, internal policies, and technical procedures be reconciled when they affect record retention?
A: Show answer
Establish a governed authority register identifying each applicable law, regulation, executed donor or grant agreement, contract, organizational policy, approved retention schedule, and relevant system requirement. Record the issuer, exact source, version or amendment, verification date, jurisdiction, entity and record classes covered, retention trigger, interpretation, owner, approval status, and unresolved questions. Mandatory legal obligations must be assessed within their actual scope, while donor or contractual terms must be interpreted according to the executed agreement, applicable law, and enforceability. Internal policy can impose an approved control but cannot remove an external legal obligation. Software defaults are not legal authority, and a policy statement cannot establish the content of an unsigned donor agreement. Where two obligations appear inconsistent, suspend the affected disclosure or destructive action as appropriate, preserve the records, and refer the issue to qualified Legal, Compliance, Tax, Privacy, or Grants personnel. Document the decision and the source evidence before changing the operational rule. Source existence alone does not prove applicability to a particular NGO, transaction, or record class.

Study instruction:
- "Build an authority register for one financial record class and trace every retention rule to its exact source, applicability decision, and approval."
Key point:
- Rules must be verified for the actual entity, record class, jurisdiction, and circumstances.
- Conflicts require documented competent review rather than an automatic assumption about precedence.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- Indonesia Government Regulation No. 71 of 2019 on Electronic Systems and Transactions
5. Q: What is the difference between an official financial record, an authoritative copy, a working copy, and a duplicate?
A: Show answer
An official financial record is information retained as evidence of organizational business, an obligation, a transaction, a decision, or another activity that must be documented. An authoritative copy is the designated record copy relied upon for the relevant business or evidentiary purpose. A working copy is used for preparation, review, analysis, or temporary processing and may contain annotations or intermediate calculations. A duplicate is another representation of substantially the same information, although its context or metadata may differ. The official status of a record should be determined by the organization's recordkeeping rules and the transaction's circumstances, not merely by its filename, upload location, or PDF format. A signed contract, approved journal, bank confirmation, and supporting invoice may all be separate official records because each proves a different part of the transaction. A draft spreadsheet does not automatically become authoritative just because it contains the final total. The register should identify record status, authority, repository, version, relationship to supporting records, and conditions under which a superseded copy must still be retained.

Study instruction:
- "Classify a signed contract, its working draft, an approved purchase order, an invoice, and a duplicate PDF according to their evidence role and authoritative-copy rules."
Key point:
- Record status depends on evidence function and approved recordkeeping rules, not appearance alone.
- A duplicate may remain relevant when it demonstrates a distinct approval, delivery, or decision.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Understanding Records Management
- UN Archives — Records Management Glossary
6. Q: Who should own, create, register, store, review, retrieve, and dispose of financial records?
A: Show answer
Assign separate responsibilities for record creation or receipt, transaction completeness, record ownership, custody, repository administration, access approval, independent review, legal or privacy decisions, and authorized disposition. The transaction owner identifies the expected supporting evidence and confirms whether the financial event is adequately documented. The record owner defines the record class, permitted business uses, quality requirements, and appropriate access. The custodian registers and files records, maintains indexes, manages retrieval, and tracks outstanding issues. The system administrator implements authorized technical changes but should not approve their own privileged access or conceal administrative activity. Independent reviewers verify completeness, authority, integrity, reconciliation, and closure evidence. Legal, Tax, Grants, or Privacy specialists provide decisions within their competence. The disposal executor should not be able to bypass eligibility checks or independently approve destruction. Each role must be linked to a current delegation record defining scope, authority limits, effective dates, and replacement arrangements. Where staffing levels prevent full segregation of duties, document the risk and implement compensating independent review.

Study instruction:
- "Create a RACI matrix for intake, classification, access, retention, legal holds, migration, retrieval, and destruction, then test potential conflicts."
Key point:
- High-risk record actions require clearly delegated authority and independent oversight.
- Limited staffing requires documented compensating controls rather than informal self-approval.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
7. Q: How should financial record classes be defined for an NGO?
A: Show answer
Develop a functional classification scheme that groups records according to the business activities and obligations they document. Typical classes include general ledger and journal records, bank and cash records, invoices and payment support, procurement and contracts, payroll and employee reimbursements, grant and donor agreements, partner advances and liquidations, fixed assets, inventory, tax records, audit requests, governance approvals, and records-management evidence. Each class should have a definition, inclusion and exclusion criteria, responsible owner, expected source population, required metadata, authoritative repository, applicable authority references, retention rule, confidentiality classification, and disposition route. Subclasses may be needed when records differ in legal requirements, sensitivity, trigger events, or business value. Avoid creating so many classes that staff cannot apply them consistently, but do not combine different obligations simply to reduce the number of categories. Classification must be reviewed when programs, donor agreements, legal entities, systems, or regulatory obligations change.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a financial records classification scheme and test whether payroll, bank statements, partner liquidation files, contracts, and tax evidence each have a defensible class."
Key point:
- Record classes should reflect function, evidence purpose, sensitivity, and retention requirements.
- One generic category is insufficient when underlying obligations differ materially.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
8. Q: What checks should be completed when a financial record is first received or created?
A: Show answer
The receiving employee or designated custodian should capture the source, receipt or creation date, record owner, transaction or case reference, period, record class, repository, version, confidentiality level, and any relevant donor, project, contract, or legal-hold reference. Check that the file can be opened, the relevant pages and attachments are present, and the apparent document identity agrees with the transaction or submission. Compare the received material with the expected submission, such as an invoice package containing an approved purchase order, delivery evidence, invoice, and payment authorization where required. Record missing documents, conflicting dates, corrupt files, uncertain signatures, or unexplained revisions as exceptions. Do not replace the original received file with a corrected version without retaining the necessary provenance and history. The source owner must clarify material gaps, and the record should not be marked complete until the applicable evidence criteria have been satisfied or an authorized exception has been documented.

Study instruction:
- "Use an intake checklist on a sample payment file and record every missing, conflicting, unreadable, or unapproved document."
Key point:
- Intake must establish identity, provenance, expected completeness, and traceable ownership.
- Receipt of a file is not proof that its contents are complete or valid.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Understanding Records Management
9. Q: Which metadata fields should be maintained for financial records?
A: Show answer
A practical metadata dictionary should define a unique record identifier, record title, class, description, creator or source, record owner, creation and receipt dates, transaction or case reference, accounting period, donor and project codes when applicable, currency or amount where useful, confidentiality classification, authoritative-copy status, repository location, version, related document identifiers, retention schedule, trigger date, hold status, and disposition eligibility status. Some records may require additional attributes such as employee or supplier identifiers, invoice numbers, approval references, system extraction dates, or data-transfer restrictions. Define which fields are mandatory, optional, conditionally mandatory, and system-generated. Specify permitted formats and approved values so that different offices do not create incompatible labels. Avoid unnecessarily replicating sensitive personal data in filenames, shared indexes, or general-purpose logs. Metadata changes should be traceable, particularly when they affect record classification, access, ownership, retention, or disposition. A missing field should be explicitly marked as unresolved when it cannot be verified rather than filled with invented information.

Study instruction:
- "Build a metadata dictionary with field name, definition, format, mandatory status, data owner, validation rule, and permitted use."
Key point:
- Consistent metadata makes records findable, interpretable, linkable, and governable over time.
- Metadata must be accurate, proportionate, and protected against unauthorized changes.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Glossary
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
10. Q: How should financial records be named, filed, linked, and versioned to support reliable retrieval?
A: Show answer
Use an approved naming convention that identifies the appropriate record class, project or entity reference, transaction or reporting period, document type, and version or status where necessary. Avoid embedding excessive personal or confidential information in filenames. Filing rules should direct authoritative records to designated repositories and retain links between records arising from the same business event. Version control must distinguish drafts, reviewed documents, approved outputs, and superseded versions. Changes to an approved financial report should create an identifiable revised version with a change reason and the required reapproval. Do not overwrite the only retained evidence of an earlier approval or reporting submission merely to keep a folder tidy. Naming standards must also support consistent retrieval across countries, offices, and systems. Where documents are imported in bulk, automated naming and indexing rules should be tested against real examples before deployment. File naming is a supporting control, not a substitute for record IDs, metadata, permissions, or audit history.

Study instruction:
- "Apply the proposed naming convention to a donor report package and test whether every file can be located and its approval status identified without opening it."
Key point:
- Controlled naming and versioning improve retrieval and reduce the risk of using the wrong document.
- Approved and superseded evidence must remain distinguishable and traceable.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
11. Q: How can the organization prove that its financial record population is complete?
A: Show answer
Completeness requires a defined expected population and a reconciliation showing what was expected, received, registered, retained, and resolved. For example, a payment-register reconciliation may compare approved payment references with invoice packages, authorization records, bank transactions, journal postings, and repository record IDs. A payroll evidence review may compare the payroll population with the approved payroll register and supporting authorization records. Record counts alone are insufficient because duplicate files may inflate the count, while one missing material record may be obscured within a large population. Use transaction identifiers, period coverage, control totals where appropriate, source reports, completeness checks, and documented exception lists. Specify how the source population was obtained and what filters, date ranges, or exclusions were applied. An independent reviewer should investigate gaps and confirm whether each difference is resolved or remains formally open. A successful reconciliation must not be inferred solely from matching totals if individual records have not been adequately linked or exceptions remain unexplained.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a period's transaction population from the source system to the records repository, using record counts, references, and material exceptions."
Key point:
- Completeness must be demonstrated against an identified source population.
- Matching totals do not independently prove that every required record is present.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
12. Q: How should the organization preserve the integrity and provenance of digital financial records?
A: Show answer
Preserve the received or generated record, its identifying metadata, applicable source details, version history, and relevant evidence of subsequent handling. Use repository controls to limit unauthorized alteration, deletion, movement, and metadata changes. Where appropriate, calculate cryptographic checksums to detect whether file contents differ from a previously recorded state. Checksum verification supports integrity testing but does not prove that the original document was truthful, authorized, or complete. Maintain a clear distinction between an immutable or controlled source record and working copies used for analysis. Record significant activities such as import, version replacement, export, restoration, access change, migration, and destruction in an auditable log. Investigate unexpected changes or discrepancies and preserve the affected copies and available logs before making corrections. If content must be amended, document the reason and approval and maintain the required link to the earlier version. Do not overwrite evidence of suspected fraud, unauthorized alteration, or other incidents.

Study instruction:
- "Compare source and stored files using record identifiers, metadata, version history, and an appropriate integrity test, then explain what each test does and does not prove."
Key point:
- Provenance and integrity depend on evidence across the record lifecycle.
- A checksum detects content differences but cannot validate the business truth or authority of a record.
Source links:
- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
13. Q: How should access to financial records be granted, reviewed, and revoked?
A: Show answer
Access should be based on a documented business need, record sensitivity, delegated authority, and the principle of least privilege. An access request should identify the requester, role, purpose, record class or scope, requested operations, approving owner, start date, expiry or review date, and any restrictions. The administrator should implement only the approved permissions and retain evidence of the change. Access to view, edit, approve, export, disclose, administer, and destroy records may need to be separated. Review permissions periodically and when employees change roles, leave the organization, or no longer need the access. Review consultant and vendor access separately, including contract status and account expiration. Emergency access should be time-limited, logged, and reviewed afterward. Shared credentials undermine accountability because actions may no longer be attributable to an individual. Access authorization also does not automatically permit external disclosure, processing for a new purpose, or destruction of the record.

Study instruction:
- "Test a sample of repository permissions against approved roles and document any excessive, expired, unapproved, or untraceable access."
Key point:
- Access must be individually attributable, limited to authorized purposes, and removed when no longer required.
- Viewing permission and authority to disclose or destroy are separate decisions.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- Indonesia Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection
14. Q: How should confidential financial information and personal data be protected in records repositories?
A: Show answer
Classify records according to the sensitivity of the information and the risks arising from unauthorized access, use, disclosure, transfer, or loss. Payroll, employee identification, bank-account details, beneficiary information, supplier records, legal correspondence, and investigation files may require additional restrictions. Apply data minimization, role-based access, secure transmission, approved storage, appropriate encryption, monitored exports, and documented retention decisions. Before sharing records externally, assess the recipient, purpose, legal or contractual authority, required scope, transfer conditions, redaction needs, and approval requirements. A repository administrator's ability to export a file is not authorization to share it. Where Indonesian Law No. 27 of 2022 applies, verify the organization's actual role, processing purpose, lawful basis, rights obligations, transfer circumstances, and other applicable requirements. Other jurisdictions and donor agreements may impose additional conditions. Do not place passwords, authentication tokens, or secret recovery codes in records indexes, shared workbooks, or audit logs. Suspected exposure must be handled through the applicable incident and specialist-review procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Classify a sample payroll and donor-reporting package, identify personal data, and define the minimum permitted disclosure for an external reviewer."
Key point:
- Confidentiality requires purpose-based access and controlled disclosure throughout the lifecycle.
- Legal compliance depends on the actual processing circumstances, not merely on labeling a file confidential.
Source links:
- Indonesia Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
15. Q: What procedure should be followed when an auditor, donor, regulator, or legal representative requests financial records?
A: Show answer
Register the request with a unique case reference, requester identity, requesting organization, stated purpose, authority or contractual basis, scope, date received, deadline, and responsible owner. Define the expected record population and search locations before collecting documents. Check whether the requester is entitled to the records, whether the request falls within the applicable agreement or legal process, and whether privacy, confidentiality, privilege, or other restrictions apply. Assemble records from authorized sources while preserving their original identity and status. Any redaction or exclusion should follow documented approval and applicable requirements. Create a disclosure manifest listing the records delivered, versions, dates, recipients, redactions, approval reference, and transfer method. Use an approved secure delivery channel and retain evidence of the transfer. If the authority or scope is unclear, refer the matter to the relevant Legal, Compliance, Privacy, Tax, or Grants owner without destroying or improperly disclosing the records. Close the request only after delivery, unresolved items, and approvals have been documented.

Study instruction:
- "Work through an audit request from receipt to closure, checking authority, search scope, record completeness, disclosure approval, redaction, and delivery evidence."
Key point:
- Retrieval and disclosure are controlled processes, not informal file-sharing exercises.
- A complete disclosure requires both appropriate authorization and an auditable record of what was provided.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Manage Records
- Indonesia Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection
16. Q: How should a retention schedule be designed for financial records?
A: Show answer
A retention schedule should define each record class, its business purpose, responsible owner, relevant legal and contractual sources, retention duration or criteria, trigger event, required retention action, disposition method, approval authority, and review process. It must distinguish minimum legal retention, donor or contract requirements, operational needs, historical value, and other justified preservation requirements. A schedule should explain how overlapping obligations are assessed and how longer justified retention is documented where permitted. It should also define treatment for drafts, convenience copies, superseded documents, records held by vendors, and linked records that must remain together. Retention rules must be approved by competent organizational authorities after the applicable sources have been verified. Avoid copying an unrelated organization's retention periods into the NGO's policy. The UN's own retention schedules illustrate a governed approach to managing official records but do not automatically apply to an independent NGO. The schedule should be tested against real record classes before implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Draft a retention schedule for invoices, bank records, payroll, grant agreements, and audit evidence using verified sources and documented approval gates."
Key point:
- Every retention rule needs a defensible basis, a defined trigger, an owner, and an approved action.
- Another institution's retention schedule is a reference, not an automatic rule for the NGO.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
- UN Archives — Disposal of Records
17. Q: How should retention periods and trigger dates be determined for Indonesian tax and financial records?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the organization and the particular books, records, and underlying documents fall within the applicable legal requirement. Article 28(11) of Indonesia's Law No. 28 of 2007, as displayed on the Directorate General of Taxes website, states a ten-year storage requirement for specified books, records, and supporting documents for covered taxpayers. That wording must not be treated as a universal rule for every NGO record or every legal entity. Verify the current consolidated tax legislation, subsequent amendments, actual taxpayer status, covered record types, required storage location, and applicable trigger with qualified tax personnel. Separately assess donor agreements, contracts, litigation or investigation holds, privacy requirements, and organizational needs. The retention register should record the applicable source, the exact provision, the event from which retention is calculated, the responsible reviewer, the verification date, and the approved eligibility date. An unverified trigger or uncertain legal scope must block destructive disposition until resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Locate the relevant tax-record retention provision and document why it does or does not apply to the entity and record class under review."
Key point:
- A statutory duration must be assessed against current law, taxpayer scope, record type, and trigger.
- The ten-year wording in the cited provision does not authorize a blanket ten-year policy for all records.
Source links:
- Directorate General of Taxes — Law No. 28 of 2007, including Article 28
- Indonesia Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection
18. Q: How should retention triggers be defined and calculated consistently?
A: Show answer
A retention trigger is the defined event from which an approved retention period is calculated. Depending on the applicable authority, a trigger may be the end of a financial year, completion of a transaction, expiry or termination of a contract, grant close-out, completion of an audit, resolution of a claim, or another specified event. The appropriate trigger must be verified for each record class rather than chosen merely because it is convenient for the filing system. Store the trigger type, actual event date, evidence supporting that date, duration or criterion, calculation method, earliest review date, and relevant review decisions. If the trigger is missing or conflicting, flag it as unresolved rather than calculating an assumed eligibility date. Use controlled calculations with defined calendar rules and independent testing of edge cases. The organization should retain evidence of the calculation so a later reviewer can reproduce it and understand why a record was considered eligible for review at a particular time.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate a retention review date from an approved example trigger and test how a missing or disputed trigger affects disposition eligibility."
Key point:
- A retention period is meaningful only when its trigger and calculation method are defined.
- Incorrect trigger dates can cause premature destruction or unnecessary retention.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
- UN Archives — Records Management Glossary
19. Q: How should donor, grant, partner, and contractual retention requirements be incorporated into the records schedule?
A: Show answer
Review the executed agreement and relevant amendments for clauses concerning financial record retention, inspection, audit access, confidentiality, data transfer, grant close-out, record ownership, return of information, and deletion.
