Record the agreement identifier, counterparties, covered projects, relevant clauses, applicable dates, authorized interpretation, and responsible owner. Do not infer a retention period from customary practice or from an unsigned proposal. If a donor term appears inconsistent with law, privacy obligations, or another binding commitment, refer the issue to the appropriate specialist instead of automatically selecting the shortest or longest period. Related records may need coordinated retention because the main agreement, invoices, expenditure reports, and audit correspondence provide interconnected evidence. When the agreement changes or a project is extended, assess whether retention triggers, access arrangements, or close-out conditions must also change. Contractual terms should remain distinguishable from statutory and internal-policy requirements so their respective authority and applicability are clear.

Study instruction:
- "Review an executed grant agreement and map each relevant retention or audit-access clause to affected record classes, obligations, owners, and trigger events."
Key point:
- Donor-specific retention rules must come from executed terms and a documented applicability review.
- Contract changes can affect record access, retention, and disposition eligibility.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
20. Q: What is a legal hold, and why must it override routine destruction processing when applicable?
A: Show answer
A legal hold is a documented instruction to preserve relevant information because of litigation, investigation, a claim, a regulatory inquiry, or another matter requiring suspension of routine disposition. A hold must identify its authority, matter reference, responsible legal or compliance owner, record and custodian scope, applicable systems, effective date, preservation instructions, and review requirements. It should prevent covered records from being destroyed or altered under an ordinary retention workflow while allowing controlled access and necessary preservation. The hold owner must identify relevant records and custodians, notify affected parties, obtain acknowledgments, monitor compliance, and update scope when facts change. The records team should maintain evidence that repositories, backups, vendors, and other relevant sources have been considered. A hold must not be released solely because a retention date has passed or an operational case appears inactive. Release requires an authorized decision and confirmation that no other hold or obligation blocks disposition. The action log must retain the initiation, scope changes, notifications, checks, and release evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a legal hold affecting an old supplier-payment file and demonstrate how it blocks disposal until an authorized release is documented."
Key point:
- A retention date does not permit destruction of records subject to an applicable active hold.
- Hold initiation, enforcement, monitoring, and release all require traceable evidence.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN Archives — Disposal of Records
21. Q: How should financial records be digitized while preserving their evidentiary value?
A: Show answer
A digitization procedure should define which originals will be scanned, the reason for digitization, the required completeness and legibility criteria, the responsible operator, independent quality reviewer, metadata, file format, indexing scheme, and final storage location. Verify that all required pages, attachments, endorsements, signatures, stamps, and relevant annotations are captured. Compare the digitized file with the original using an approved quality checklist and retain evidence of the review. Record scanning date, operator, source record identifier, output identifier, and any rescan or correction. Do not assume that scanning automatically authorizes destruction of the paper original. The decision to retain or destroy the original must follow applicable law, evidentiary needs, contract obligations, internal policy, and authorized approval. If the original is damaged, incomplete, or disputed, preserve the condition evidence and escalate before replacing or disposing of it. Quality standards should be proportionate to the legal, financial, and operational importance of the record.

Study instruction:
- "Scan a sample invoice package, reconcile every page and attachment, record quality-test results, and determine whether the original may be retained or considered for an authorized disposition decision."
Key point:
- Digitization quality must be demonstrated against a defined source and completeness standard.
- A digital copy does not automatically authorize destruction of the original document.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- UN Records Management — Recordkeeping Quality Guidance
22. Q: How should financial records be migrated between repositories or transferred to a new service provider?
A: Show answer
A migration or transfer must begin with an approved plan identifying the source and destination systems, record population, scope, timing, responsibilities, risks, required metadata, access restrictions, relevant holds, rollback arrangements, and acceptance criteria. Before moving records, freeze or otherwise control the source population and produce an inventory of record IDs, versions, counts, and metadata. Test a representative sample to assess content rendering, searchability, permissions, links, audit history, retention attributes, and relevant attachments. After transfer, reconcile the source and destination populations and investigate missing records, duplicates, unexpected changes, broken links, or metadata loss. A successful technical job is not sufficient proof of business acceptance. An independent reviewer should verify that the required population is present, usable, appropriately restricted, and traceable. Retain acceptance evidence and an authorized decision before decommissioning the old repository. If unresolved discrepancies remain, maintain the source or a recoverable copy as appropriate and block final acceptance or destructive cleanup.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a migration manifest and define pre-transfer counts, test cases, post-transfer reconciliation, exception ownership, rollback conditions, and independent acceptance criteria."
Key point:
- Migration acceptance requires evidence of content, population, metadata, access, and record usability.
- The original source should not be destroyed merely because a transfer tool reports success.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
23. Q: How should backup and restoration procedures protect authoritative financial records?
A: Show answer
The organization should identify critical repositories and financial records, define backup scope and frequency under an approved policy, protect backup access, retain sufficient recovery information, and document restoration responsibilities. Backups should be separate from ordinary working access where appropriate and protected against unauthorized modification or deletion. Periodic restoration tests should demonstrate that selected records can actually be recovered, opened, interpreted, and reconciled to expected identifiers and relevant metadata. A backup-success message proves neither recoverability nor completeness of the recovered population. Tests should record the selected source, recovery point, target environment, file counts, integrity checks, permission checks, results, limitations, and independent review. Restored records should initially be placed in a controlled location so an incomplete or corrupted recovery does not overwrite the authoritative source. If a restore fails or produces differences, preserve diagnostic evidence and escalate to the relevant system and records owners. Retention and hold requirements must still be checked after restoration.

Study instruction:
- "Run a controlled restoration test for selected financial records and reconcile record identifiers, counts, file integrity, metadata, and access settings."
Key point:
- A backup is useful only when the required records can be recovered and independently verified.
- Restoration testing must not overwrite the only trustworthy source copy.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- UN Records Management — Recordkeeping Quality Guidance
24. Q: When may an NGO dispose of financial records whose retention period appears to have expired?
A: Show answer
Expiry of a calculated retention period should trigger an eligibility review, not automatic destruction. First verify the record identity and class, approved schedule, correct trigger date, applicable law, executed donor and contractual terms, continuing business need, active or pending legal holds, investigations, audits, claims, privacy obligations, and any other applicable preservation duty. Confirm whether linked records must be retained together and whether the proposed record is an authoritative copy or merely a convenience copy. Where any mandatory information is unknown or conflicting, place the record on hold for decision and assign an owner and review date. An authorized approver should approve the eligible batch before the executor freezes the manifest, verifies the selected population, carries out the authorized method, and reconciles the actual result. Preserve a disposition record containing the approved basis, scope, approval, execution date, method, executor, outcome, and unresolved exceptions. Never treat an elapsed date alone as sufficient authority to delete records.

Study instruction:
- "Use a disposition eligibility checklist on an old financial-record batch and identify every condition that must pass before destruction can proceed."
Key point:
- Expiry initiates an eligibility decision; it does not itself authorize destruction.
- Unverified scope, unresolved holds, and missing approvals must block destructive action.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Disposal of Records
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
25. Q: What evidence should be retained to prove that record destruction was properly authorized and executed?
A: Show answer
Retain the record class and disposition batch identifier, approved retention schedule, trigger calculation, eligibility checklist, applicable authority references, hold-check results, linked-case status, independent review, approval reference, frozen manifest, selected record IDs, execution method, date, authorized operator, system or vendor result, exceptions, and final reconciliation. A destruction certificate should state what was destroyed, under which authority and approval, by what method, when, by whom, and whether the result matched the authorized scope. It should not contain unnecessary personal or confidential information merely to make the audit trail more detailed. If the execution result differs from the approved manifest, stop closure, investigate the variance, preserve all available logs, and escalate the incident. The retained certificate is evidence of the described disposition action; it does not cure an invalid retention decision or prove that all legal requirements were correctly interpreted. Records about disposition themselves should have a documented retention rule.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical destruction certificate to its approved manifest, retention basis, hold checks, execution evidence, and final reconciliation."
Key point:
- Disposition evidence must establish both valid authority and actual execution within approved scope.
- A certificate cannot cure missing legal analysis or unauthorized destruction.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Disposal of Records
- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
26. Q: How should the NGO manage paper archives and records stored outside the main finance office?
A: Show answer
Maintain a location register identifying physical storage sites, responsible custodians, record classes, box or container identifiers, access restrictions, inventory references, environmental and security requirements, and retrieval procedures. Use a controlled checkout process when records are loaned to staff, auditors, advisers, or another office. Record who removed each item, when, for what purpose, and when it was returned. Periodically reconcile the inventory and investigate overdue loans, missing containers, unauthorized movement, or evidence of damage. Where storage is managed by an external vendor or local office, define custodial responsibilities, access arrangements, retrieval service expectations, confidentiality, incident reporting, and transfer or exit requirements. Physical security should be proportionate to the sensitivity and importance of records. A spreadsheet location field is useful only if it reflects the actual physical location and is updated after movement. For a missing file, preserve the last known location and movement history rather than reconstructing an unsupported location.

Study instruction:
- "Inventory a sample of paper financial files and test location accuracy, checkout records, access controls, and overdue-return escalation."
Key point:
- Physical custody must be documented as carefully as electronic repository custody.
- Retrieval and movement controls help prevent loss, unauthorized access, and untraceable handling.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Guidelines for Records Storage Facilities
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
27. Q: How should emails, instant messages, and electronic approvals be handled when they constitute financial evidence?
A: Show answer
Determine whether the communication documents a material transaction, approval, instruction, commitment, exception, dispute, or other organizational activity that must be retained. If it is a record, capture it in the designated repository with its relevant date, participants, subject or context, attachments, linked transaction, and approval or decision reference. Retain sufficient context to understand the communication rather than saving an isolated screenshot that omits essential information. Preserve required message metadata and original content where feasible under the applicable system and policy. Do not assume that keeping an email in an employee's mailbox satisfies the organization's official recordkeeping requirements. Conversely, not every transient message or convenience copy must become a permanent financial record. The classification and retention rules should specify how business communications are captured, how duplicates are managed, and how access or deletion is controlled. Where a legal hold or investigation applies, follow its preservation instructions before routine cleanup.

Study instruction:
- "Review an email approval for a payment and identify the content, metadata, linked documents, authoritative repository, and retention decision required."
Key point:
- Business communications may be essential financial records when they evidence decisions or transactions.
- Capture enough context to preserve the meaning, provenance, and authority of the communication.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN Archives — Understanding Records Management
28. Q: How should journal vouchers, general ledger reports, and supporting accounting schedules be retained and cross-referenced?
A: Show answer
Maintain the posted journal or authoritative ledger record together with the relevant supporting calculations, source documents, preparer and approver evidence, posting reference, accounting period, chart-of-accounts mapping, and reconciliation documentation. Where journals are uploaded from spreadsheets, preserve the approved upload or source file and the evidence showing how the final posting relates to it. Distinguish draft or parked transactions from approved and posted records, because they represent different workflow states. Reconciliations should link detailed subsidiary records to relevant general ledger balances and explain differences rather than merely reporting matching totals. The records repository should preserve stable identifiers and sufficient links to navigate between a journal, its source invoice or payroll record, the approval evidence, and the applicable reporting output. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes general ledger processes and integrated accounting records within Umoja; its system-specific procedures should not be presented as mandatory system settings for an independent NGO. Local chart-of-accounts, posting, and record-retention requirements must be confirmed separately.

Study instruction:
- "Select a posted journal and trace it to the source evidence, calculation, approval, posting confirmation, general ledger report, and related reconciliation."
Key point:
- Journal records and support should form a traceable evidence chain across accounting and reporting stages.
- UN Umoja procedures provide an accounting-process reference, not automatic implementation requirements for every NGO.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN Archives — Understanding Records Management
29. Q: How should bank statements, payment confirmations, reconciliations, and cash records be linked for audit purposes?
A: Show answer
Retain the external bank statement or authoritative banking evidence, the corresponding cashbook or ledger data, payment or receipt references, reconciliation workings, reviewer approval, and resolution evidence for outstanding items. Each record should be identifiable by account, period, transaction reference, and source where appropriate. The reconciliation must distinguish timing differences from unexplained variances, duplicate postings, missing transactions, and other errors. Retain evidence for adjustments and corrections rather than overwriting the original record. Where banking information includes confidential personal or supplier data, apply appropriate access and export restrictions. A bank transaction marked successful does not independently establish that the payment was authorized, correctly classified, or posted to the proper project. The recordkeeping process should therefore link technical payment evidence with the separate approval and accounting evidence. Missing statements or unresolved balances must remain visible in the exception log until the required investigation and review are complete.

Study instruction:
- "Trace three bank-statement items from external evidence through the cashbook, reconciliation, approval, journal, and archive."
Key point:
- Reconciliation evidence must connect external cash records with internal accounting records and approved corrections.
- Payment success and transaction authorization are different control assertions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
30. Q: How should payroll, personnel, and employee reimbursement records be protected and retained?
A: Show answer
Payroll and personnel evidence should be classified according to its purpose and sensitivity, with separate treatment where the record classes have different access or retention requirements. Relevant financial evidence may include approved payroll registers, calculation workings, payment confirmations, deduction records, signed authorizations, reimbursement claims, receipts, and reconciliation schedules. Preserve enough information to substantiate the financial transaction while limiting access to personal data that is not needed for the business purpose. Establish authorized HR, Finance, reviewer, and external-auditor access according to role and applicable law. If a single file contains information subject to different access restrictions, consider whether controlled separation or an approved redacted copy is appropriate while preserving the authoritative record. Retention must be determined from the applicable legal, contractual, and organizational rules; a generic retention period should not be assumed. Personnel disputes, payroll corrections, audits, or legal holds may require additional review before disposition.

Study instruction:
- "Design a payroll-record access matrix and distinguish financial evidence required for accounting from sensitive employee information that requires tighter restrictions."
Key point:
- Payroll evidence must remain sufficient for accountability without unnecessary exposure of personal data.
- Retention and access decisions require record-class-specific legal and policy review.
Source links:
- Indonesia Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
31. Q: How should grant, donor, and implementing-partner records be organized to support financial accountability?
A: Show answer
Organize records so that the executed agreement, budget and amendments, approved activity plans, expenditure evidence, advance transfers, partner financial reports, liquidation workings, correspondence, approvals, reconciliations, and final donor submissions can be linked by grant, project, period, counterparty, and transaction references. Establish one authoritative location for each record class and identify which office owns the original or official copy. Retain evidence of receipt, review, correction, acceptance, and any unresolved partner balance. Records must show both the substantive transaction and its approval and reporting history. The retention schedule should incorporate relevant executed agreement clauses and distinguish records needed for financial verification from materials subject to different confidentiality or access obligations. Before close-out or destruction, check outstanding audits, disputes, claims, reporting corrections, and holds. A donor report marked final is not sufficient evidence that all supporting records are complete or that every partner balance has been resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Create a record index for a grant from agreement signature through partner liquidation and final donor reporting, then test the traceability of selected expenditure lines."
Key point:
- Donor accountability depends on a connected evidence chain from agreement to expenditure and final reporting.
- Grant close-out does not automatically settle all retention or legal-hold obligations.

- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
32. Q: How should an NGO respond when a financial record is missing, corrupted, altered, or inconsistent with its source?
A: Show answer
Register the matter as an exception or incident, assign a case reference, preserve the available source files and system logs, and document when and how the discrepancy was discovered. Identify the affected record, period, transaction, repository, last known location, custody history, potential business impact, and any related hold or investigation. Search authorized repositories, backups, email records, source systems, and relevant vendors using documented search criteria. Any recovered or reconstructed copy should be clearly identified and independently compared with available corroborating evidence. Do not silently overwrite the defective file or report a successful recovery merely because a similar document has been found. Where content authenticity or legal admissibility is in question, refer the matter to the appropriate specialist. Record the root cause, corrective action, evidence of restoration, residual uncertainty, owner, deadline, and independent closure approval. The original discrepancy and corrective history must remain traceable.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a hypothetical missing payment approval and document containment, search coverage, recovery evidence, root cause, correction, and independent closure."
Key point:
- Missing or corrupted records require controlled investigation and evidence-based recovery.
- A replacement document must not conceal the original defect or unresolved uncertainty.
Source links:
- UN Records Management — Recordkeeping Quality Guidance
33. Q: How should records-management incidents and recurring exceptions be investigated and resolved?
A: Show answer
Maintain a centralized incident and exception register containing the reference, discovery date, affected records and processes, severity, source of detection, owner, deadlines, immediate containment, referral, investigation, root cause, corrective action, and closure evidence. Distinguish individual processing errors from systemic causes such as inadequate permissions, inconsistent metadata, missing retention rules, unreliable data migration, untested backups, or unclear ownership. Where confidentiality or security may be compromised, preserve relevant logs and restrict further exposure while referring the matter through the appropriate incident process. Assign corrective action to a named owner and require evidence of implementation and retesting. Repeated issues should trigger management review of the control design, training, workflow, or system configuration. Do not close an incident solely because a record was recovered; the underlying control failure and residual risk may still require action. Overdue or unresolved matters should remain visible and be escalated under approved risk criteria.

Study instruction:
- "Analyze recurring missing-document exceptions and distinguish immediate correction from root-cause remediation and control retesting."
Key point:
- An incident is resolved only when the defined closure criteria and required corrective evidence are satisfied.
- Recurrence analysis should lead to improvements in the process or control design, not only repeated reminders.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
34. Q: What controls should govern third-party repositories, outsourced archives, and records hosted by external service providers?
A: Show answer
Maintain a system and vendor catalogue identifying the provider, service and environment, record classes held, legal entity, data locations where relevant, responsible owner, contract identifier, access model, backup arrangements, service requirements, and exit process. Review contractual provisions on confidentiality, permitted processing, retrieval, audit access, incident notification, retention, transfer, subcontracting, return, and secure deletion. Confirm that these provisions match the organization's actual needs and applicable obligations; do not rely on a generic supplier description as proof of compliance. Changes to permissions, storage locations, system configuration, or vendor arrangements should be authorized and tested. Periodically assess performance, security evidence, unresolved exceptions, and continuity arrangements. Before termination, agree how records will be exported, reconciled, transferred, retained where required, and securely removed when authorized. Vendor confirmation alone is not sufficient evidence of successful exit: the organization should validate retained records, transfer results, outstanding copies, and the final disposition of information within the approved scope.

Study instruction:
- "Review an archive-provider contract and complete a vendor-control checklist covering access, retrieval, retention, holds, backups, incident response, exit, and deletion evidence."
Key point:
- Outsourcing custody does not remove the organization's need to govern access, retention, and record availability.
- Vendor exit requires a reconciled transfer and documented treatment of remaining copies.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- Indonesia Government Regulation No. 71 of 2019 on Electronic Systems and Transactions
35. Q: How should document exports and redacted financial-record packages be controlled?
A: Show answer
Treat an export as a controlled event distinct from internal repository access. Register the request, authorizing person, purpose, scope, recipient, transfer method, approved record version, and confidentiality restrictions. Apply the approved minimization and redaction process, using a working copy rather than altering the authoritative source. An independent reviewer should inspect the prepared package for unauthorized personal data, privileged content, unrelated transactions, hidden worksheets or document information where relevant, and omitted supporting material needed to preserve context. Record the final output identifiers, filenames, record IDs, versions, redactions, approval, creation date, recipient, transmission evidence, and any delivery acknowledgment required. If the package is revised after approval, evaluate whether the approval and checks must be repeated. Maintain the manifest and audit evidence under the relevant retention rules without unnecessarily duplicating the most sensitive content. Technical ability to download, print, or export a file is not a substitute for authorization to disclose it.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a controlled audit export from financial records and test scope, redactions, version identity, approval, recipient, and secure transmission."
Key point:
- Disclosure must be authorized, minimized, documented, and linked to the exact output delivered.
- Redaction should occur on a controlled copy while preserving the authoritative original.

- Indonesia Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
36. Q: How should version control and approval history work for financial reports and records-management documents?
A: Show answer
Define a controlled process for preparing, reviewing, approving, issuing, revising, and archiving documents. Each approved output should have a version identifier, owner, approval reference, approval date, effective date where relevant, and a link to the evidence used to prepare it. Working files should be distinct from issued or authoritative outputs. A material change after approval must trigger an impact assessment covering affected calculations, reports, recipients, downstream decisions, and required reapproval. Preserve the relationship between the earlier and revised versions so the organization can reconstruct what was approved at a particular time. Use controlled status values such as draft, under review, approved, issued, superseded, withdrawn, and archived, with explicit definitions. Do not infer approval from a filename, email subject, or upload timestamp. The version history should identify who made a change and why, while access rules should prevent unauthorized users from altering final evidence or its approval metadata.

Study instruction:
- "Trace an approved financial report through a post-approval correction and establish which checks, authorizations, and notifications must be repeated."
Key point:
- Approval applies to the exact version reviewed and authorized.
- Revisions must preserve the original decision history and document their downstream impact.

- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
37. Q: How can Excel and Power Query support a financial records register without replacing formal records controls?
A: Show answer
Excel can provide structured registers, validation lists, record inventories, review checklists, exception logs, and reporting summaries. Power Query can combine approved source lists, normalize identifiers, standardize formats, detect candidate duplicates, and produce population-reconciliation outputs. Structured tables and defined field rules improve reliability as data grows. However, the register must distinguish imported data from verified facts and should preserve the source, refresh timestamp, query version, transformation rules, record counts, control totals, and exception results. Do not allow a workbook to silently overwrite authoritative evidence or mark records as legally eligible for destruction based solely on a calculated date. Refresh results need validation, particularly after source-file, column, schema, or query changes. Assign an owner for the workbook's design, access, change approval, testing, and recovery. The accounting or legal meaning of a record must be determined from applicable authority and organizational requirements, not from a spreadsheet formula.

Study instruction:
- "Design a structured Excel register and a Power Query import that preserves source references, validates mandatory fields, identifies candidate duplicates, and reports reconciliation differences."
Key point:
- Automation can improve repeatability, searchability, and exception identification.
- A register or query output does not independently establish legal authority or disposition eligibility.
Source links:
- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
38. Q: How should VBA automation be controlled when it registers, exports, modifies, or disposes of financial records?
A: Show answer
VBA may assist with routine tasks such as generating record identifiers, preparing document manifests, exporting approved reports, updating workflow status, checking required fields, and producing control logs. Before deployment, define authorized functions, required inputs, expected outputs, exception behavior, access restrictions, and the approval process for code changes. Test normal cases, missing inputs, invalid paths, duplicate identifiers, interrupted execution, permission failures, and partial completion. For high-risk operations, the macro should stop safely when prerequisites are not met and must not report success merely because no error message appeared. Log the actor, timestamp, operation, scope, input and output references, outcome, and relevant exceptions. Independent controls are especially important for deletion, access changes, external disclosure, and other irreversible or sensitive actions. Code review and test results should be retained with version information. A macro must not convert an unverified retention calculation or a fictional test rule into automatic authorization for destruction.

Study instruction:
- "Test a VBA workflow using missing-file, unauthorized-access, duplicate-record, and partial-export scenarios, and verify that each failure is logged without a false success status."
Key point:
- Automation must enforce approved prerequisites, preserve evidence, and fail safely when checks do not pass.
- High-risk actions require explicit authorization and independent evidence beyond macro execution.

- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
- UN Records Management — Functional Requirements for Record-Keeping
39. Q: What should a records-management dashboard report to finance management?
A: Show answer
A management dashboard should measure defined populations and control outcomes rather than rely only on the number of files stored. Useful indicators include the percentage of required records registered, unresolved completeness exceptions, missing-record cases, retrieval turnaround, overdue access reviews, unverified retention triggers, records approaching eligibility review, active holds awaiting action, migration reconciliation differences, failed recovery tests, unauthorized-access incidents, overdue corrective actions, and disposition batches awaiting approval. Every indicator should have a documented definition, denominator, data source, reporting period, owner, threshold, and escalation rule. Distinguish confirmed results from estimates and unknowns. For example, an apparently high registration rate may be misleading if the expected source population is incomplete. Report material limitations and overdue decisions explicitly so management can allocate responsibility and resources. Dashboard totals must be traceable to underlying registers and evidence. A green status should only be displayed when its defined conditions are demonstrably satisfied.

Study instruction:
- "Build a dashboard using defined source populations and independently recalculate a sample of completeness, retrieval, access-review, and overdue-action metrics."
Key point:
- Dashboard measures need valid denominators, reliable source data, and explicit status definitions.
- Management reporting should surface unresolved risks and owners, not obscure uncertainty.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
40. Q: How should an NGO test whether its records-management SOP works in practice before adoption?
A: Show answer
Begin with a controlled pilot using a representative set of financial records and clearly defined acceptance criteria. Test intake, classification, metadata, official-copy designation, retrieval, completeness reconciliation, access approval, retention calculation, hold suppression, digitization or migration where applicable, incident handling, restoration, and disposition eligibility. Verify that each workflow has an owner, required inputs, explicit pass or fail conditions, allowed next steps, and evidence of the result. Test both successful and unsuccessful paths, including missing documents, disputed authority, inconsistent record counts, invalid metadata, expired permissions, unresolved legal holds, and failed technical operations. Distinguish checks that were actually executed from those that remain unverified. For example, a configuration review or document-generation check cannot prove that a live repository enforces the intended access restrictions. Independent review should record limitations, failed tests, corrective actions, retest evidence, and any outstanding mandatory adoption gate. The policy owner and authorized approver must accept the applicable risks and approve the final version before operational use.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a pilot-test matrix with scenario, control objective, input, expected result, observed evidence, pass/fail decision, defect owner, and retest date."
Key point:
- SOP adoption requires evidenced testing of applicable workflows and controls, not just a well-formatted document.
- Unverified legal, repository, access, and operational gates must remain visible and block actions they govern.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
41. Q: How should the records-management SOP be maintained when laws, donor terms, systems, and organizational responsibilities change?
A: Show answer
Establish a formal review process triggered by scheduled reviews and material events such as new legislation, regulatory guidance, executed contract changes, audit findings, security incidents, system migration, organizational restructuring, repository changes, or recurring recordkeeping failures. The policy owner should register the source of the change, publication and effective dates, affected record classes, obligations, workflows, metadata, permissions, training materials, and existing open cases. Qualified reviewers should assess the impact and determine whether the SOP or schedules require revision. Changes to retention periods, trigger rules, legal-hold handling, access, or disposition must be tested before implementation. Record the preparer, independent reviewer, authorized approver, effective date, revision summary, communication evidence, and superseded version. Users must be informed of changes affecting their responsibilities. Until a material ambiguity is resolved, affected operations should remain on hold or follow the applicable formally approved interim procedure. The organization must be able to reconstruct which version governed a historical action.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical regulatory or repository change from detection through applicability review, impact assessment, approval, testing, communication, and archival of the previous SOP version."
Key point:
- Controlled review keeps the SOP aligned with applicable obligations and actual operations.
- Every material revision requires documented authority, testing, approval, and version history.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
42. Q: How should a financial records management SOP distinguish legal applicability from technical or documentary verification?
A: Show answer
Use separate status fields for legal or policy applicability, source verification, technical execution, evidence completeness, independent review, and final approval. These statuses answer different questions. A source can be officially published while its applicability to the organization remains unresolved. A file can be present while its authenticity or completeness is unverified. A technical operation can succeed while its scope is wrong. A reviewer can confirm a calculation without having the authority to approve the underlying legal interpretation. The SOP should therefore avoid a single status such as "verified" when multiple criteria remain open. For each significant decision, define the evidence required, the competent decision-maker, the reviewer, and conditions preventing progression. The fictitious demonstration document itself emphasizes that verified existence of a legal source does not establish an approved retention requirement. Keep unknowns visible and assign owners and deadlines until the relevant evidence or decision is obtained.

Study instruction:
- "Separate source existence, legal applicability, technical result, evidence completeness, and approval for one proposed retention rule."
Key point:
- Each status must correspond to a specific assertion supported by appropriate evidence.
- A verified source is not the same as a verified conclusion about the organization's obligations.
Source links:
- Indonesia Law No. 43 of 2009 on Archives
43. Q: What should an NGO do when records are approaching their retention review date but the legal basis is still uncertain?
A: Show answer
Keep the records in a controlled state and prevent routine destructive disposition for the affected scope. Assign a responsible owner to verify the applicable legal entity, jurisdiction, record class, current rule, retention trigger, donor or contract terms, active holds, and relevant privacy or business obligations. Record the uncertainty, the sources already reviewed, the specialist referral required, the decision deadline, and the interim control. Do not convert an unknown requirement into an arbitrary date merely to clear the retention dashboard. Equally, do not assume that indefinite retention is automatically appropriate, particularly where personal data or confidentiality considerations apply. The authorized reviewer should document the conclusion, rationale, effective rule, and any exceptions before the register is updated. Once a valid basis is established, recalculate eligibility if necessary and resume the normal review process. The outcome should remain traceable to the evidence and approval that resolved the uncertainty.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a documented decision request for a record class with an uncertain retention period, including the missing facts, interim restrictions, competent reviewer, and release criteria."
Key point:
- Uncertain retention rules must be resolved through a controlled review, not an invented deadline.
- Preservation and data-minimization obligations must be evaluated together under applicable requirements.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
- Directorate General of Taxes — Law No. 28 of 2007
44. Q: What is the difference between records retention, archival preservation, backup, and ordinary file storage?
A: Show answer
Retention is the requirement or approved decision governing how long a record must or should be kept. Archival preservation concerns maintaining records that have enduring value under the applicable archival framework and policy. Backup creates a recovery copy intended to support continuity or restoration after loss, corruption, or system failure. Ordinary file storage is the technical location where information resides and may not, by itself, provide adequate recordkeeping controls. These functions overlap but are not interchangeable. A backup does not automatically become the authoritative record, a storage folder does not prove an approved retention schedule, and retaining a record does not guarantee that it can be retrieved or interpreted. Similarly, a record with historical or enduring value may need a preservation route different from routine destruction after a financial retention period. The organization should assign separate controls for official-copy designation, retention, backup, recovery, and archival transfer and document their relationships in its repository catalogue and records schedule.

Study instruction:
- "Compare retention, archival preservation, backup, and ordinary storage for one bank statement and identify the controls required for each function."
Key point:
- Keeping a file, preserving an authoritative record, and creating a recovery backup are different control objectives.
- Retention schedules must specify the required end-of-period action, including preservation or approved disposition where applicable.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Retention Schedules
- UN Archives — Disposal of Records
45. Q: How should recordkeeping controls be integrated into the NGO's month-end and year-end financial close?
A: Show answer
Include records-completeness requirements as explicit close tasks alongside reconciliations, accrual reviews, account analysis, grant reporting, tax reconciliation, and financial statement preparation. For each close task, identify the expected supporting population, authoritative records, preparer, reviewer, completion evidence, storage location, and outstanding exceptions. A completed journal or reconciliation should link to its source data, calculations, approvals, and relevant postings. Close checklists should distinguish accounting completion from records completion, because a number can be posted while supporting evidence remains missing or improperly stored. At year-end, assess whether required record populations, approved outputs, open audit requests, pending partner reports, unresolved balances, and applicable holds have been addressed. Record any permitted exception through an authorized process with an owner, deadline, and risk assessment. The records register and close pack should be cross-referenced so an auditor can locate the evidence without relying on individual staff members' memory or personal folders.

Study instruction:
- "Integrate a records-completeness checklist into one monthly close pack and trace selected balances from source evidence to approved journal, reconciliation, and final report."
Key point:
- Financial close should verify both accounting outputs and the completeness and traceability of supporting evidence.
- Posting an entry does not close an unresolved records-quality exception.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN Archives — Understanding Records Management
46. Q: How can an NGO measure whether its records-management controls are effective over time?
A: Show answer
Assess control effectiveness through defined tests, trends, exception analysis, and evidence of completed corrective actions. Examples include source-to-repository reconciliation differences, missing required documents, incorrect classifications, retrieval failures, unauthorized access, expired permissions, incomplete migration checks, unsuccessful restore tests, unresolved retention triggers, overdue hold acknowledgments, and unauthorized or unreconciled disposition events. For each metric, document the population, calculation, source, frequency, target or threshold, owner, reviewer, and escalation rule. Validate a sample independently rather than relying solely on the dashboard. Examine both the number and severity of failures because a single missing material contract may matter more than numerous harmless filing errors. Recurring defects should lead to root-cause investigation, control redesign, training, system improvements, or revised responsibilities. Management should track corrective actions to verified closure and periodically reassess whether the controls remain appropriate as the organization changes.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a quarterly control-testing plan that evaluates record completeness, access, retrieval, retention eligibility, and corrective-action closure."
Key point:
- Effectiveness must be demonstrated through tested evidence and meaningful exception trends.
- Management should address root causes instead of relying exclusively on repeated manual checks.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
47. Q: What should a financial records management handover contain when a finance employee or records custodian leaves the organization?
A: Show answer
Handover should identify current repositories, record-class ownership, open registration and reconciliation exceptions, pending audit or donor requests, active and pending holds, outstanding access reviews, unverified retention decisions, records awaiting migration or restoration, upcoming disposition reviews, vendor dependencies, and unresolved corrective actions. Include system access responsibilities without transferring passwords or personal authentication secrets. Confirm the incoming owner's authority, required training, access approvals, repository knowledge, and ability to retrieve essential evidence. Reconcile physical files, high-risk records, outstanding loans, and critical electronic record inventories where appropriate. Document the handover date, outgoing and incoming roles, reviewer, unresolved risks, action owners, and approval. Revoke access from the outgoing user through the authorized access-management process and verify that required records remain available under the new custody arrangement. Handover is complete only when ownership and required access are established and critical unresolved items have been assigned and acknowledged.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a handover checklist for a departing finance custodian and demonstrate how record ownership, outstanding requests, holds, repository access, and pending disposition work are transferred."
Key point:
- Handover must preserve continuity of custody, accountability, and access to evidence.
- Staff departure requires a controlled access change and documented ownership transfer.
Source links:
- UN Archives — Advisory Services
- UN Archives — Records Management Policy and Guidance
48. Q: What conditions must be satisfied before a financial records management SOP is approved for operational use?
A: Show answer
The organization should verify its legal entity and jurisdiction, the applicability of relevant legislation, executed donor and contractual obligations, approved record classes and retention rules, authoritative repositories, access configuration, records ownership, legal-hold procedures, vendor arrangements, backup and restoration capabilities, and authorized disposition controls. It should complete the required document review, resolve material test failures, obtain approval from the delegated authority, assign a policy owner, set an effective date, publish the controlled version, and communicate responsibilities to users. Training and handover materials should reflect the actual configuration rather than unverified placeholders. Outstanding low-risk matters may be handled under an approved action plan only where the relevant authority permits and the resulting risk has been assessed and accepted by a competent person. Mandatory legal or operational blockers must remain unresolved status items, not be concealed by a general approval or a successful document-generation check. Preserve the approved version, supporting decisions, and evidence of implementation so future reviewers can establish what the organization formally adopted and when.

Study instruction:
- "Use an adoption checklist to verify source applicability, approved retention rules, tested controls, authorized roles, final document status, and required training before issuing a controlled SOP."
Key point:
- A professionally generated document is not equivalent to an approved and operationally tested policy.
- Adoption requires verified applicability, authorized governance, and evidence that required controls can operate as designed.
